Open-closed maps and spectral local systems The Clynelish distillery.
Noah Porcelli, Ivan Smith

Abstract
Abstract: Let X X be a graded Liouville domain. Fix a pair of infinite loop spaces Ψ = ( Θ → Φ ) \Psi=(\Theta\to\Phi) living over ( B ​ O → B ​ U ) (BO\to BU) . This determines a spectral Fukaya category \scrF ​ ( X , Ψ ) \scrF(X;\Psi) whenever T ​ X TX lifts to Φ \Phi , containing closed exact Lagrangians L L for which T ​ L TL lifts compatibly to Θ \Theta ; and by Bott periodicity and index theory, a Thom spectrum R R with bordism theory R ∗ R_{*} . This paper has two main goals: we incorporate rank one spectral local systems ξ : L → B ​ G ​ L 1 ​ ( R ) \xi:L\to BGL_{1}(R) into the spectral category; and we prove that the bordism class [ ( L , ξ ) ] [(L,\xi)] defined by the open-closed map differs from the class [ L ] [L] by a multiplicative two-torsion element in R 0 ​ ( L ) × R^{0}(L)^{\times} determined by an action of the stable homotopy class of the Hopf map η ∈ π 1 s ​ t \eta\in\pi_{1}^{st} on ξ \xi . Methods include a twisting construction associating flow categories to spectral local systems, and a model for the open-closed map incorporating Schlichtkrull’s construction of the trace map B ​ G ​ L 1 ​ ( R ) ⊆ K ⁡ ( R ) → R BGL_{1}(R)\subseteq K(R)\to R . The companion paper [ PS:qi ] shows that (for Lagrangians which themselves admit spectral lifts) one can lift quasi-isomorphisms from ℤ \mathbb{Z} to Ψ \Psi at the cost of introducing rank one local systems. Together with the open-closed computation given here, this gives an essentially complete picture of the bordism-theoretic consequences of quasi-isomorphism in the classical exact Fukaya category.



Contents

1
Introduction

1.1
Context

Abouzaid and Blumberg
[
AB
]
have constructed a stable infinity-category of framed flow categories, which is equivalent to the category of spectra, with a view to creating ‘Floer homotopy theory without homotopy theory’. In this worldview, bordism takes centre stage: morphism groups in the spectral Fukaya category are bordism classes of flow modules. More classical viewpoints on Floer homotopy theory in the vein of work of Cohen, Jones and Segal live on the ‘other side’ of the Pontrjagin-Thom isomorphism.

In
[
PS24a
]
, we constructed a spectral Fukaya category for a stably framed Liouville manifold, with objects being compatibly stably framed compact exact Lagrangian submanifolds. That paper used results of
[
Lar
]
, in turn building on foundational work of
[
FOOO24
]
, to equip moduli spaces of curves with the structure of smooth manifolds with corners; unlike Large, we did not pass to spectra, but remained in the setting of flow modules. The sequel
[
PS24b
]
constructed an analogous spectral Fukaya category for any tangential structure
\Psi=(\Theta\to\Phi)
living over
BO\to BU
, thus allowing us to access information contained in more general bordism theories. Here the symplectic manifold
X
is assumed to have a lift of the classifying map for
TX
to
\Phi
, and objects of the category are compact exact Lagrangians equipped with lifts of their tangent bundles to
\Theta
. (Both
[
Lar
]
and
[
ADP24
]
also construct versions of exact spectral Fukaya categories, but both pass via Cohen-Jones-Segal from bordism to spectra and homotopy types.)

Objects of the classical Fukaya category are typically taken to be Lagrangians equipped with local systems, usually of rank one. Allowing local systems is essential from many viewpoints: rank one
\mathbb{Z}
-local systems (which are closely related to
Spin
structures) allow one to incorporate changes in orientation signs; when working over
\mathbb{C}
, rank one
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-local systems give rise to additional non-zero objects and idempotents, and underpin a deep relation to cluster theory; Novikov-unitary local systems are at the heart of the ‘SYZ viewpoint’ on mirror symmetry.

Any Lagrangian has a ‘fundamental class’, in general living in quantum homology, defined via a count of perturbed holomorphic discs on
L
via the open-closed map. An important point is that, in the exact case, the fundamental class of a Lagrangian equipped with a local system is the same as its classical fundamental class with no local system (this changes, in general, in the presence of holomorphic discs).

This paper has two goals:

1.

We incorporate rank one spectral local systems into the spectral Fukaya category; more precisely, we show how to ‘twist’ the Floer flow category associated to
L
and
K
when either carries a local system;

2.

we compute the corresponding open-closed fundamental class. Strikingly this does now depend on the local system, with an essentially universal dependence governed by the stable homotopy class of the Hopf map
\eta\in\pi_{1}^{st}
.

Even to formulate our main result requires revisiting the construction of the spectral Fukaya category
\scrF(X;\Psi)
in the special case where the tangential pair
(\Theta\to\Phi)
lives over
(BO\to BU)
as a diagram of infinite loop spaces, since the appearance of
\eta
reflects a trace map from algebraic
K
-theory of a ring spectrum to the original ring spectrum which only exists in the commutative setting.

Our restriction to rank one spectral local systems is motivated by both the ubiquity of rank one local systems in classical Floer theory, and the main theorem of the sequel
[
PS:qi
]
, which shows that if
L
and
K
themselve admit spectral lifts to
\scrF(X;\Psi)
, then a quasi-isomorphism of
L
and
K
over
\mathbb{Z}
lifts to a quasi-isomorphism of
(L,\xi)
and
K
over
\Psi
, for some rank one
\xi
.
Together with the computation of the open-closed map in the presence of the local system given here, this gives essentially complete control of the consequences in bordism of knowing that two Lagrangians are quasi-isomorphic in the integral Fukaya category.

Numerous applications of this result to classical symplectic topology questions are give in
[
PS:qi
]
; in particular, we show that the normal invariant of a nearby Lagrangian factors through
\eta
.

1.2
Results

The version of the Fukaya category we work with in this paper uses
commutative
tangential pairs, which amounts to asking that

\textstyle{\Theta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BU}

be a diagram of infinite loop spaces
1
1

1

In fact, we impose grading and orientation requirements that mean that our tangential structures more naturally live over
BSpin\to BS_{\pm}U
, with the latter being the fibre over
\pm 1
of the determinant map; indeed, we work with a slightly more general notion of oriented tangential pair, see Definition
56
.
. Let
F
be the homotopy fibre of the map
\Theta\to\Phi
. Using real Bott periodicity, we may define a commutative ring spectrum
R_{\Psi}
, as the Thom spectrum of an index bundle defined by the composition
\Omega F\to\Omega\widetilde{U/O}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Bott}}BO
, see Section
3
for a detailed construction. Its base, which we write as
E_{\Psi}
, is homotopy equivalent to
\Omega F
.

{ex}

We write
fr
for the tangential pair
\Psi=(\operatorname{pt},\operatorname{pt})
, corresponding to the case of framed symplectic manifold and Lagrangians. In this case,
R_{\Psi}
is equivalent to the sphere spectrum. If
\Psi=(\operatorname{pt},BS_{\pm}U)
,
R_{\Psi}
is the complex cobordism ring spectrum
MU
.

Similarly to the case of an ordinary ring,
R_{\Psi}
has a group of units
GL_{1}(R_{\Psi})
, and we may model
R_{\Psi}
-local systems over a base
B
as maps
B\to BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})
. We call these
\Psi
-local systems
. Through the Pontrjagin-Thom isomorphism, the Thom ring spectrum
R=R_{\Psi}
defines an associated bordism theory
\Omega_{*}^{E_{\Psi}}(\cdot)
.

To a pair
\Psi=(\Theta,\Phi)
as above and a Liouville domain
X
with a lift
\phi:X\to\Phi
of the classifying map of
TX
, we associate a spectral Fukaya category
\scrF(X;\Psi)
. This has objects pairs
(L,\xi_{L})
, where
L
is a closed exact Lagrangian with a compatible lift of
TL
to
\Theta
, and
\xi_{L}
is a
\Psi
-local system. The morphism groups are linear over the ring
\Omega_{*}^{E_{\Psi}}
. Our construction here incorporates the infinite loop structure of
\Psi
in a way that was absent from
[
PS24b
]
.

Let
\phi
denote the
\Phi
-orientation on
X
. There is a (open-closed or ‘
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}
’) bordism theory
\Omega_{*}^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}(X,\phi)
, which is a module over
R^{-*}(X)
by cap-product, and which is a variant of
\Omega_{*}^{E_{\Psi}}(X)
; see Section
5.3
for the precise definition, and Example
5.3
for relations to more familiar theories. This is
the ‘correct’ place for the open-closed map to land in this setting: for a
\Theta
-oriented Lagrangian
L
, or pair
(L,\xi_{L})
of such a Lagrangian with a spectral local system, the open-closed map defines a class
[(L,\xi_{L})]\in\Omega_{d}^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}(X,\phi)
. Our main result below computes how this class depends on
\xi_{L}
.

{ex}

If
\Psi=fr
,
\Omega^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}_{*}(X,\phi)
maps to the framed bordism groups of
X
. If
\Psi=(\operatorname{pt},BS_{\pm}U)
,
\Omega^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}_{*}(X,\phi)
maps to the complex cobordism groups of
X
.

The fact that
R
is commutative means that the stable homotopy class
\eta\in\pi_{1}^{st}
defines a distinguished subgroup of the group of units
R^{0}(L)^{\times}
, comprising classes that factor through a map
\eta^{*}:BGL_{1}(R)\to GL_{1}(R)
induced by
\eta
. Here we are using that, similarly to the case of ordinary rings, there is an inclusion
GL_{1}(R)\to R
. Slightly abusively we call this subgroup the ‘image of
\eta
’; it is composed of multiplicatively two-torsion elements (i.e. they square to 1).

Theorem 1

(Theorem
146
)
.

Let
\Psi
be a commutative tangential pair, and
X
a Liouville domain with
\Phi
-orientation
\phi
. Let
L\subseteq X
be a closed exact Lagrangian equipped with a compatible
\Theta
-orientation, and
\xi_{L}
a
\Psi
-local system on
L
(thus
L
and
(L,\xi_{L})
are objects of the spectral Fukaya category
\scrF(X;\Psi)
). Write
[L],[(L,\xi_{L})]\in\Omega_{*}^{E_{\Psi};\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}(X,\phi)
for their open-closed fundamental classes.

Then we have:
[(L,\xi_{L})]=[L]\cap[\eta\xi_{L}]
, where
[\eta\xi_{L}]\in R^{0}(L)
lies in the image of
\eta
.

The class
[\eta\xi_{L}]
is determined by
\xi_{L}
explicitly, cf. the full statement in Theorem
146
.

Corollary 2
.

With the notation of Theorem
1
:

1.

The restriction of the class
[\eta\xi_{L}]
to any point in
L
represents the class
+1\in R^{0}(\operatorname{pt})
.

2.

The difference
[(L,\xi_{L})]-[L]=0
after inverting 2, i.e. it is
2^{l}
-torsion for some
l
.

3.

If
L
is a homotopy sphere,
[(L,\xi_{L})]-[L]
is 2-torsion.

See Section
8.7
for the proof of Corollary
2
.

{rmk}

The class
[L]\cap[\eta\xi]
is in general not equal to
[L]
. Concretely, if
R_{\Psi}\simeq\mathbb{S}
and
L=S^{n}
,
[L,BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})]\simeq\pi_{n-1}\mathbb{S}
, and if we choose a class
\xi\in\pi_{n-1}\mathbb{S}
which is not annihilated by multiplication by
\eta
, we obtain such an example (such classes exist when
(n-1)=1,2,3,7,8,9,\dots
).

This is in contrast to the case of (exact) Fukaya categories over
\mathbb{Z}
, where the image of a Lagrangian brane under the open-closed map is independent of the local system. In light of Theorem
1
, morally this is because
\eta
acts trivially on ordinary rings.

Theorem
1
is obtained using work of Schlichtkrull
[
Sch04
]
. Here it is essential to have the commutativity of the tangential pair.

{rmk}

Philosophically
\eta
should also arise in
[
AAGCK25
]
(which, however, is not directly about either open-closed maps or local systems). Theorem
1
essentially confirms, in our setting, the speculation in that direction given in the Introduction of
[
AAGCK25
]
; the result is accessible here because we keep track of more commutativity than
op. cit.
. Amongst the applications of the main results given in
[
PS:qi
]
(which also use commutativity in an essential way), those related to normal invariants of nearby Lagrangians in cotangent bundles can be seen as mild refinements of the results of
[
AAGCK25
]
, where the refinement arises because
\eta
is visible rather than in the background.

1.3
Methods of proof

Construction of the Fukaya category

To incorporate commutativity of the tangential pair into the Fukaya category, we prove that all relevant moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}
admit coherent
\Psi
-orientations, namely a lift of their stable tangent bundle to
E_{\Psi}
. In
[
PS24b
]
, we constructed related tangential structures on such moduli spaces, but these were not a priori compatible with the commutativity of the tangential pair: heuristically, the point of view in
[
PS24b
]
was that breaking of moduli spaces corresponds to concatenation of loops in
\Omega U/O
whereas the natural way to construct an infinite loop structure on
\Omega U/O
uses direct sum in
U/O
and ignores the loop factor. In Section
5
we carefully carry out an explicit Eckmann-Hilton-type argument to bridge the gap between these two notions.

Some similar ideas appear in work of Bonciocat
[
Bon25
]
in the setting of moduli spaces of strips.

To incorporate
\Psi
-local systems into the spectral Fukaya category, we ‘twist’ the flow category
\mathcal{M}^{LK}
associated to a pair of
\Psi
-branes
L,K
equipped with local systems
\xi_{L},\xi_{K}
to produce a new flow category
\mathcal{M}^{\xi_{L},\xi_{K}}
. The data of the local systems is used to construct maps from the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}^{LK}_{xy}
into Thom spaces of finite rank index bundles over spaces of abstract holomorphic caps, and the twisted flow category moduli spaces are given by zero-sets of these maps.

Central to our technical results (both here and in
[
PS:qi
]
) is the notion of a flow category
over
a manifold
L
(possibly equipped with an
E_{\Psi}
-orientation, cf. Definition
87
). This consists of a flow category
\mathcal{F}
, along with maps of spaces
ev:\mathcal{F}_{xy}\to\Omega L
, compatible with concatenation on both sides. These arise naturally in Floer theory: the flow category
\mathcal{M}^{LK}
associated to a pair of Lagrangians has such a map, essentially by restricting a holomorphic strip to one of its boundary components. A similar construction has appeared in
[
BC07
,
Abo12
,
BDHOb
]
.

There is a category of (
E_{\Psi}
-oriented) flow categories over
L
; in the sequel
[
PS:qi
]
we further exploit these ideas to construct (cf.
[
PS:qi
, Section 5.1]
) a Viterbo-style restriction functor

\scrF(X;\Psi)\longrightarrow{\Flow}^{E_{\Psi}}_{/L}.

{rmk}

There is also a truncation of
\Flow_{/L}
which recovers
C_{*}(\Omega L)\mathchar 45\relax
mod, making contact with well known ideas of Floer theory with ‘universal local systems’, cf. for instance
[
BDHOb
,
BDHOa
]
.

Algebraic
K
-theory and the Hopf map

For the ordinary (
A_{\infty}
) Fukaya category, the
open-closed
map is a map from Hochschild homology to ordinary homology,
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}:HH_{*}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\scrF(X;\mathbb{Z}))\to H_{*+d}(X)
. From the TFT perspective, this can be viewed as composing the unit morphism of an object with a ‘co-unit’-type moduli space of caps, as arises in trace maps in more abstract frameworks such as
[
HSS17
]
.

More concretely, the ordinary Fukaya category can be enlarged to contain Lagrangians with local systems of arbitrary rank, as is done in
[
Kon17
]
. We may further restrict to the full subcategory
\scrF^{L}(X)
of arbitrary-rank local systems supported on a single Lagrangian
L
. Then the open-closed map
HH_{*}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\scrF^{L}(X;\mathbb{Z}))\to H_{*+d}(X)
can be seen to be a form of trace map more directly; for example in the construction of the open-closed map with higher-rank
\mathbb{Z}
-local systems in
[
Kon17
, Section 3.2.2]
, one takes traces of parallel transport matrices explicitly.

In the spectral setting, the spectral open-closed map is expected to be a map

\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}:\pi_{*}THH^{R_{\Psi}}(\scrF(X;\Psi))\to\Omega^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}_{*}(X,\phi)

from (relative) topological Hochschild homology, the ‘spectral analogue’ of ordinary Hochschild homology.
Amongst other things, this would require an
A_{\infty}
version of our spectral Fukaya category, which has not yet been built.

Recall that algebraic
K
-theory
K(R)
of a ring spectrum
R
is obtained from the colimit
\lim_{k\to\infty}BGL_{k}(R)
by applying Quillen’s plus construction. For
R
commutative, there is a standard map from
K
-theory,
K(R)\to\Omega^{\infty}R
.

Crucially, Schlichtkrull computed the composition of these standard maps when restricted to
BGL_{1}(R)\subseteq K(R)
, surprisingly finding an instance of the Hopf map
\eta
:

Theorem 3

(
[
Sch04
]
)
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring spectrum. The restriction of the trace map to
BGL_{1}(R)
is determined by the Hopf map:

\textstyle{BGL_{1}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{*}}
\textstyle{K(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{GL_{1}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\infty}R}

(1.1)

commutes up to homotopy.

This is the motivation for
\eta
arising in the study of spectral local systems and the open-closed map. Our model of the open closed map is constructed similarly to some model for the map
K(R)\to\Omega^{\infty}R
, and to prove Theorem
1
in Section
8
we pass through several cyclic bar-type constructions to be able to compare to the models for
\eta
arising in
[
Sch04
]
.

{rmk}

For
R
an ordinary ring,
\eta_{*}:BGL_{1}(R)\to GL_{1}(R)
is nullhomotopic, reflecting Remark
1.2
.

Speculation on higher ranks

One should also be able to incorporate higher rank spectral local systems on a Lagrangian
L
into the spectral Fukaya category. These can be modelled as maps
\xi_{L}:L\to BGL_{n}(R_{\Psi})
for
n>1
(or, more generally, maps
\xi_{L}:L\to B\Aut(M)
for
M
a perfect
R_{\Psi}
-module). The natural generalisation of Theorem
1
would be that
[(L,\xi)]=[L]\cap[Tr\,\xi_{L}]
, where
Tr\,\xi_{L}
is the composition of the map
BGL_{n}(R)\subseteq K(R)\to R
and
\xi_{L}:L\to BGL_{n}(R)
.

Unlike Schlichtkrull’s full computation in the
n=1
case, we are not aware of any general computations of the map
BGL_{n}(R)\to R
for
n>1
, though there is has been some study of a related filtration on algebraic
K
-theory
[
Rog92
]
.

Consider a plumbing of two closed manifolds along a point,
X=T^{*}Q_{1}\#T^{*}Q_{2}
.
[
AS12
]
shows that, once
\dim(Q_{i})\geq 3
, any closed exact graded Spin Lagrangian
L\subseteq X
is generated (in the integral Fukaya category) by local systems over the core components. Since trace maps from
K
-theory send exact sequences to sums, one may expect that the open-closed map sends
L
to a sum of classes of the form
[(Q_{i},\xi)]
, for
\xi
a (possibly high-rank) spectral local system on
Q_{i}
. Combined with higher-rank generalisations of Schlichtkrull’s computation, this could be used to constrain bordism classes of Lagrangians in plumbings.

2
Homotopical preliminaries

Our main results make essential use of commutativity of tangential pairs (as we have explained in the introduction). A convenient model for infinite loop spaces is commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids, which are a special kind of
\mathcal{I}
-space. An
\mathcal{I}
-space is a family of spaces indexed by elements of a set
\mathcal{I}
(e.g. the natural numbers), with good functoriality properties under inclusions of subsets (e.g. maps
[n]\to[m]
). For instance,
BO
can be constructed as an infinite loop space in many ways; the commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid structure does this using maps
\oplus:BO(n)\times BO(m)\to BO(n+m)
and stabilisations
BO(n)\to BO(n^{\prime})
. The ‘underlying’ space one is naively thinking of is built as a homotopy colimit, but having access to the ‘finite levels’ of these spaces is very natural in symplectic topology (where we stabilise Cauchy-Riemann operators or complex vector bundles by finite-dimensional spaces, even if one has to pass to larger and larger such spaces to see the underlying homotopy type emerge). Working with
\mathcal{I}
-spaces is also convenient for enabling us to directly leverage the results of
[
Sch04
]
when proving Theorem
1
.

Whilst
\mathcal{I}
-spaces permeate the paper, some of the more delicate homotopical preliminaries on bar constructions,
\Gamma
-spaces, etc are only used essentially in Section
8
.

2.1
Homotopy colimits

Let
Spc
be the category of (compactly generated, Hausdorff) topological spaces, and
Spc_{*}
the category of (well-)based such spaces. We write
Spc_{(*)}
to mean either of these two categories.

Definition 4

(
[
BK72
]
, cf. also
[
Bru00
, Section 2.5]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category, and
F:\mathcal{C}\to Spc_{(*)}
a functor. The
homotopy colimit
of
F
,
\mathrm{hocolim}_{\mathcal{C}}F
, is defined to be the realisation of the simplicial space:

[k]\mapsto\bigvee\limits_{x_{0}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow x_{k}\in\mathcal{C}}F(x_{k})

(2.1)

where
\vee
means
\sqcup
in
Spc
, and the wedge is over sequences of
k
composable morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.

The face maps come from composition in
\mathcal{C}
and the degeneracy maps from inserting the identity of
x_{i}
.

For an object
x\in\mathcal{C}
, we write
\iota_{0}
for the inclusion into the 0-simplices
F(x)\to\mathrm{hocolim}_{F}\mathcal{C}
.

\mathrm{hocolim}
takes functors to spaces, and natural transformations to maps of spaces:

Definition 5
.

Homotopy colimits are functorial in the following way. Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be small categories, and
F:\mathcal{C}\to Spc_{(*)}
and
G:\mathcal{D}\to Spc_{(*)}
be functors. Let
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor, and let
T:F\to G\circ A
be a natural transformation.

This induces a map
(A,T)_{*}:\mathrm{hocolim}_{\mathcal{C}}F\to\mathrm{hocolim}_{\mathcal{D}}G
, constructed as follows. The map sends each term
F(x_{k})
of the
k
-simplices corresponding to the sequence
x_{0}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow x_{k}
in
\mathcal{C}
to the term
F(A(x_{k}))
corresponding to the sequence
A(x_{0})\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow A(x_{k})
, and on this term is given by the map
F(x_{k})\xrightarrow{T}G(A(x_{k}))
.

The following is used in Section
8
.

Lemma 6
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be small categories, and
F:\mathcal{C}\to Spc_{(*)}
and
G:\mathcal{D}\to Spc_{(*)}
be functors.

Let
A,A^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be functors, and
T:F\to G\circ A
,
T^{\prime}:F\to G\circ A^{\prime}
natural transformations.

Let
U:A\to A^{\prime}
be a natural transformation, such that the two natural transformations
F\to G\circ A^{\prime}
given by
T^{\prime}
and
(G\circ U)\circ T
agree.

Then the two maps
(A,T)_{*},(A^{\prime},T^{\prime})_{*}:\mathrm{hocolim}_{\mathcal{C}}F\to\mathrm{hocolim}_{\mathcal{D}}G
are homotopic (via a naturally-constructed homotopy).

Proof 2.1
.

Let
A_{1}
be the category with objects
0,1
, and one nontrivial arrow
a:0\to 1
. Let
H:\mathcal{C}\times A_{1}\to\mathcal{D}
be the functor given by
F
on
\mathcal{C}\times 0
,
G
on
\mathcal{C}\times 1
and
U
on
\mathcal{C}\times a
.
T
and
T^{\prime}
together define a natural transformation
S:F\to G\circ H
of functors
\mathcal{C}\times A_{1}\to Spc_{(*)}
.

From Definition
5
, we obtain a map
(H,S)_{*}:\mathrm{hocolim}_{\mathcal{C}\times A_{1}}F\to\mathrm{hocolim}_{\mathcal{D}}G
. There is an equivalence
\mathrm{hocolim}_{\mathcal{C}\times A_{1}}F\simeq(\mathrm{hocolim}_{\mathcal{C}}F)\times[0,1]
, and
(H,S)_{*}
restricts to
(A,T)_{*}
and
(A^{\prime},T^{\prime})_{*}
over
0
and
1
respectively, thus providing the desired homotopy.

2.2
\mathcal{I}
-spaces

In this section, we recap the notions of
\mathcal{I}
-spaces and
\mathcal{I}
-monoids, following
[
Sch04
, Section 2]
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be the category with objects nonnegative integers
n
, and morphisms
n\to m
given by (not necessarily order-preserving) injections
[n]\to[m]
, where
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Definition 7
.

An
\mathcal{I}
-space
is a functor from
\mathcal{I}
to the category of spaces.

For any category
\mathcal{C}
, one can similarly consider an
\mathcal{I}
-object in
\mathcal{C}
as a functor
\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{C}
.

Given an
\mathcal{I}
-space
X
, we define
X_{h\mathcal{I}}=\operatorname{hocolim}_{n\in\mathcal{I}}X(n)
to be the homotopy colimit of the
X(n)
.

We say an
\mathcal{I}
-space
X
is
convergent
if the connectivity of the maps
X(n)\to X(n^{\prime})
goes to
\infty
as
n\to\infty
.

Lemma 8

(
[
Bru00
, Lemma 2.5.1]
)
.

If
X
is a convergent
\mathcal{I}
-space, the connectivity of the inclusion map
X(n)\to X_{h\mathcal{I}}
goes to
\infty
as
n\to\infty
.

{rmk}

All examples of
\mathcal{I}
-spaces we encounter throughout this paper are convergent.

\mathcal{I}
is symmetric monoidal, with monoidal structure given by
(n,m)\mapsto n+m
. The unit of the symmetric monoidal structure is the empty set
0
and the symmetric structure comes from the bijection
n+m\to m+n
which sends the first
n
elements to the last
n
elements (preserving their order) and the last
m
elements to the first
m
elements (also preserving their order).

{rmk}

Explicitly, the monoidal structure is defined by
(X\otimes Y)(n)=\operatorname{colim}_{[n_{1}]\sqcup[n_{2}]\to[n]}X(n_{1})\times Y(n_{2})
. If
\mathcal{C}
is a cocomplete (symmetric) monoidal category, the category of
\mathcal{I}
-objects in
\mathcal{C}
is also (symmetric) monoidal.

\otimes
is not always well-behaved homotopically, though it does have a better-behaved derived version, cf.
[
SS13
, Section 2]
for further discussion.

{ex}

Other categories of interest
\mathcal{C}
that satisfy these conditions include simplicial sets, and spaces over a fixed space
R
.

Definition 9
.

An
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
is a monoidal object in the category of
\mathcal{I}
-spaces. Explicitly, this consists of an
\mathcal{I}
-space
X
equipped with maps
\mu_{mn}:X(m)\times X(n)\to X(m+n)
, which form an associative and unital natural transformation
X(\cdot)\times X(\cdot)\to X(\cdot+\cdot)
, where we view both sides as functors
\mathcal{I}^{2}
to spaces. Here unitality means that the basepoint acts as a two-sided identity element in the natural way.

An
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
X
is
commutative
if the following diagram commutes for all
m,n
:

\textstyle{X(m)\times X(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{swap}}
\textstyle{X(m+n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{mn}}
\textstyle{X(n)\times X(m)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{X(n+m)}

(2.2)

where
\sigma_{mn}
swaps the first
m
factors of
[m+n]
with the final
n
factors.

{rmk}

Let
X
be a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid, and let
S
be a(n unordered) finite set. Let
n_{s}\in\mathcal{I}
for each
s\in S
, and let
N\in\mathcal{I}
. Let
f:\sqcup_{s\in S}[n_{s}]\to[N]
be an injective map.

Choose an ordering on
S
, i.e. an isomorphism
g:S\simeq[k]
for some
k
, and consider the composition:

\prod_{s\in S}X(n_{s})\xrightarrow{g}X(n_{g^{-1}(1)})\times\ldots\times X(n_{g^{-1}(k)})\xrightarrow{\mu}X(n_{g^{-1}(1)}+\ldots+n_{g^{-1}(k)})\xrightarrow{X(f)}X(N)

(2.3)

By commutativity of
X
, this map does
not
depend on the choice of ordering on
S
: it only depends on the map
f
. We will use this construction in several places in Sections
2.5.1
and
8
.

For an
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
X
,
X_{h\mathcal{I}}
forms a topological monoid, with product given by the composition:

X_{h\mathcal{I}}\times X_{h\mathcal{I}}=\operatorname{hocolim}\limits_{(m,n)\in\mathcal{I}^{2}}X(m)\times X(n)\to\operatorname{hocolim}_{(m,n)\in\mathcal{I}^{2}}X(m+n)\to X_{h\mathcal{I}}

(2.4)

The first arrow uses the product maps
\mu_{mn}
of
X
and the second uses the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{I}
.

Definition 10
.

An
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
X
is
group-like
if the monoid
X_{h\mathcal{I}}
is group-like, i.e.
\pi_{0}X_{h\mathcal{I}}
is a group.

{rmk}

\mathcal{I}
-spaces model spaces, group-like
\mathcal{I}
-monoids model loop spaces, and group-like commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids model infinite loop spaces. More precisely, each of these is realised by a Quillen equivalence between the appropriate categories
[
SS12
]
.

{ex}

The groups
U(n)
are functorial under injections
n\to m
, and so form a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid in the category of Lie groups rather than spaces. Since the bar construction is functorial, applying
B
gives us an
\mathcal{I}
-monoid in spaces
n\mapsto BU(n)
; we write
BU
for this
\mathcal{I}
-monoid. This construction applies similarly to other classical groups.

A similar example is
U/O
: here each
U/O(n)
is the space of totally real subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. This is naturally based at
\mathbb{R}^{n}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
; another example is then
\Omega U/O
, where we apply
\Omega
levelwise.

Another example is the (levelwise) universal cover
\widetilde{U/O}
of
U/O
.

{ex}

Let
G(n)
be the topological monoid of based homotopy autoequivalences of
S^{n}
, viewed as the 1-point compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(with base-point at infinity). By identifying
S^{n+m}\simeq S^{n}\wedge S^{m}
, along with the symmetric group action by permuting co-ordinates,
G(\cdot)
becomes a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid in the category of (group-like) topological monoids. Applying the bar construction levelwise then provides a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
BG
.

{ex}

The maps
O(n)\to G(n)
, via the obvious linear actions of
O(n)
on
S^{n}
, induces a map of commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids
J:BO\to BG
, called the
J
homomorphism
.

{ex}

The fibration
\det^{2}:U(N)\to S^{1}
is a group homomorphism so its (homotopy) fibre
S_{\pm}U(N)
is a Lie group- explicitly, it is the subgroup of
U(N)
consisting of matrices of determinant
\pm 1
, a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-extension of
SU(N)
. Its delooping
BS_{\pm}U(N)
is the homotopy fibre of the map
BU(N)\to K(\mathbb{Z},2)
classifying
2c_{1}
. The squared determinant homomorphism is compatible with the maps
U(N)\to U(M)
induced by injections of finite sets
[N]\to[M]
. Hence the diagram

\textstyle{BS_{\pm}U(N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BU(N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{\pm}U(N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U(N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{1}}

(with vertical arrows indicating taking based loops) exhibits
N\mapsto BS_{\pm}U(N)
as an
\mathcal{I}
-space.

Lemma 11
.

There is a commutative diagram of commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids:

\textstyle{\widetilde{U/O}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Re}
\textstyle{BO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot\otimes\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{BS_{\pm}U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U/O\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Re}
\textstyle{BO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot\otimes\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{BU}

(2.5)

where the rows are homotopy fibre sequences.

{rmk}

Working with
BS_{\pm}U(N)
(rather than the homotopy fibre
BSU(N)
of the map classifying
c_{1}
) is natural from the viewpoint of gradings, and necessary for applications to the cotangent bundle
T^{*}Q
of a non-orientable manifold
Q
, since then
c_{1}(T^{*}Q)
is 2-torsion.

Definition 12
.

A map of
\mathcal{I}
-spaces
f:X\to Y
is
k
-connected
if the induced map
X_{h\mathcal{I}}\to Y_{h\mathcal{I}}
is
k
-connected. Similarly we set
\pi_{*}X:=\pi_{*}X_{h\mathcal{I}}
.

Definition 13
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map of
\mathcal{I}
-spaces. Its
homotopy fibre
obtained by taking homotopy fibres of spaces levelwise:
\mathrm{hofib}(f:X\to Y)
is the
\mathcal{I}
-space sending
n
to
\mathrm{hofib}(X(n)\to Y(n))
.

Homotopy pullbacks are similarly defined levelwise.

Lemma 14
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map of convergent
\mathcal{I}
-spaces. Then
\mathrm{hofib}(f)
is a convergent
\mathcal{I}
-space, and
\mathrm{hofib}(f)_{h\mathcal{I}}\to X_{h\mathcal{I}}\to Y_{h\mathcal{I}}
is a homotopy fibre sequence of spaces.

Similarly, taking homotopy pullbacks commutes with applying
\cdot_{h\mathcal{I}}
.

Proof 2.2
.

If
X(n)\to X(m)
and
Y(n)\to Y(m)
are both
k
-connected, so is
\mathrm{hofib}(f)(n)\to\mathrm{hofib}(f)(m)
; the convergence claim follows.

The canonical nullhomotopies of each composition
\mathrm{hofib}(f)(n)\to X(n)\to Y(n)
assemble to a nullhomotopy of the composition
\mathrm{hofib}(f)_{h\mathcal{I}}\to X_{h\mathcal{I}}\to Y_{h\mathcal{I}}
; there is therefore a map
\mathrm{hofib}(f)_{h\mathcal{I}}\to\mathrm{hofib}(f_{h\mathcal{I}})
, which induces an isomorphism of homotopy groups.

The same argument applies to homotopy pullbacks.

For an
\mathcal{I}
-space
X
, we let
\Omega X
be the
\mathcal{I}
-space obtained from
X
by applying
\Omega\cdot
levelwise:
(\Omega X)(n):=\Omega(X(n))
.

Applying Lemma
14
to the map of
\mathcal{I}
-spaces
\operatorname{pt}\to X
, we find:

Corollary 15
.

Let
X
be a convergent
\mathcal{I}
-space. Then
\Omega X
is convergent, and there is an equivalence of spaces
(\Omega X)_{h\mathcal{I}}\simeq\Omega(X_{h\mathcal{I}})
.

2.3
Bar constructions

We use bar constructions in various parts of the paper, particularly Sections
7
and
8
.

Definition 16
.

Let
G
be a topological monoid. The
bar construction

BG
is defined to be the realisation of the simplicial space
BG_{\bullet}
with
k
-simplices
BG_{k}:=G^{k}
. The face and degeneracy maps are given by:

\displaystyle d_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})

\displaystyle={\begin{cases}(x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})&\textrm{ if }i=0\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1})&\textrm{ if }i=k\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}x_{i+1},\ldots x_{k})&\textrm{ otherwise}\end{cases}}

(2.6)

\displaystyle s_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})

\displaystyle=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},1_{G},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{k})

(2.7)

This is a based space with base-point given by the unique 0-simplex
BG_{0}=G^{0}
.

Let
M
be a small topological category with object set
O
. Let
A
be a right module and
D
a left module over
M
. Then the
bar construction

B(A,M,D)
is defined to be the realisation of the simplicial space
B(A,M,D)_{\bullet}
with
k
-simplices:

B(A,M,D)_{k}:=\bigsqcup\limits_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}\in O}A(x_{0})\times M(x_{0},x_{1})\times\ldots\times M(x_{k-1},x_{k})\times D(x_{k})

(2.8)

Lemma 17
.

B
defines a functor from topological monoids to spaces.

If
f:G\to G^{\prime}
is a map of topological monoids such that
G
and
G^{\prime}
are both group-like (meaning
\pi_{0}G
is a group) and
f
is a weak equivalence of spaces,
Bf:BG\to BG^{\prime}
is also a weak equivalence of spaces.

{rmk}

In a few constructions, it is more convenient to work with simplicial sets rather than topological spaces. Note that the (cyclic) bar constructions throughout Section
8
still make sense as written when
G
is a Kan complex rather than space; each construction is then the realisation of a bisimplicial set, which can be modelled by taking the diagonal simplicial set: in symbols, if
S_{\bullet\bullet}
is a simplicial simplicial set,
|S_{\bullet\bullet}|
is the simplicial set with
|S_{\bullet\bullet}|_{k}:=S_{kk}
.

We obtain similar constructions in the cases of topological spaces by using the equivalence
|\Sing_{\bullet}(Y)|\to Y
, where
\Sing_{\bullet}(Y)
is the singular simplicial set of a space
Y
.

We model the standard topological
k
-simplex as
\Delta^{k}:=\{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in[0,1]^{k}\,|\,0\leq s_{1}\leq\ldots\leq s_{k}\leq 1\}
. We define maps
r_{k}:\Delta^{k}\to[0,k]
, to send
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\mapsto s_{1}+\ldots+s_{k}
.

Lemma 18
.

Let
Y
be a path-connected based space which is homotopy equivalent to a CW complex.

Then there is a weak equivalence of spaces
ev:B\Omega Y\to Y
.

Proof 2.3
.

We give a construction of the map here; that this is an equivalence is standard.

We first define a map of simplicial spaces
ev^{\prime}:B\Sing_{\bullet}(\Omega Y)\to\Sing_{\bullet}Y
as follows. On
k
-simplices, for
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\in\Sing_{k}\Omega Y^{k}
, we define
ev^{\prime}_{k}
to send this tuple to the
k
-simplex in
Y
sending
s\in\Delta^{k}
to:

ev^{\prime}_{k}(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})(s):=\left[\overline{\gamma}_{1}^{s}\star\ldots\star\overline{\gamma}_{k}^{s}\right](r_{k}(s))

(2.9)

For
\gamma
a simplex in
\Sing_{k}\Omega Y
and
s\in\Delta^{k}
, we write
\gamma^{s}
for the corresponding loop in
\Omega Y
.

We define the map
ev
of spaces by requiring that the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

\textstyle{|B\Sing_{\bullet}\Omega Y|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ev^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{|\Sing_{\bullet}Y|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{B\Omega Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ev}
\textstyle{Y}

(2.10)

The space of 1-simplices in
BG
is exactly
G
. Since all such 1-simplices have boundary on the unique 0-simplex, we obtain a canonical map of spaces
\iota_{1}:G\to\Omega BG
. This is a weak equivalence of spaces. Unfortunately,
\iota
only respects the product structure on
G
and
\Omega BG
up to (coherent) homotopy, and not on the nose. Instead, we have:

Lemma 19
.

Assume
G
is group-like (meaning
\pi_{0}G
is a group). Then there is a zig-zag:

G\xleftarrow{\simeq}WG\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Omega BG

(2.11)

where the intermediate space
WG
is a group-like topological monoid, and both maps are maps of topological monoids which are homotopy equivalences of (unbased) spaces.

Furthermore, this can be chosen so that the equivalence of spaces
BG\simeq B\Omega G
obtained by applying
B
to (
2.11
) agrees with the one from applying Lemma
18
to
BG
.

Proof 2.4
.

This follows immediately from the statement of
[
Vog15
, Theorem 1.3]
.

For completeness, we give a rough overview of the construction. Associated to a topological monoid
M
there is another group-like topological monoid
WM
(
[
Vog15
, Definition 2.2]
), along with a monoid map
\varepsilon:WM\to M
which is a homotopy equivalence of spaces
2
2

2

We remind the reader
shrinkable
maps as considered in
[
Vog15
, Definition 2.2]
are always homotopy equivalences.
.
WM
has the property that for another topological monoid
N
, monoid maps
WM\to N
biject with
(unital) homotopy homomorphisms

M\to N
(
[
Vog15
, Definition 2.1]
): roughly this is a map
M\to N
which respects the products of
M
and
N
up to coherent higher homotopy (this is similar to a map of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-spaces).

Applying
[
Vog15
, Theorem 4.4]
to
M=G
and
X=BG
, the map of based spaces
B\varepsilon:BWG\to BG
is induced by a (unique up to homotopy) map of monoids
WG\to W\Omega BG
; composing with
\varepsilon
gives a map
WG\to\Omega BG
. This completes the required zig-zag.

The idea behind these constructions is that in
WM
, unlike in
M
, the product map
WM\times WM\to WM
is an embedding with a “collar neighbourhood”, and the collar neighbourhood coordinates can be used to keep track of the homotopy coherence.

Definition 20
.

We call the zig-zag (
2.11
), from
G
to
\Omega BG
,
\widetilde{\iota}_{1}
.

We write
\rightsquigarrow
instead of
\to
for a zig-zag: this will either be the zig-zag
\widetilde{\iota}_{1}
of topological monoids as in (
2.11
), or the zig-zag of spaces obtained by applying a functor such as
B
(or variants such as
B^{cyc}
) to this zig-zag.

Definition 21
.

Let
G
be a topological monoid. The
(1-pointed) cyclic bar construction

B^{cyc}G
is defined to be the realisation of the simplicial space
B^{cyc}G_{\bullet}
with
k
-simplices
B^{cyc}G_{k}:=G^{k+1}
. The face and degeneracy maps are given by:

\displaystyle d_{i}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{k})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(x_{k}x_{0},\ldots,x_{k-1})&\textrm{ if }i=k\\
(x_{0},\ldots,x_{i}x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{k})&\textrm{ otherwise }\end{cases}

(2.12)

\displaystyle s_{i}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{k})

\displaystyle=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},1_{G},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{k})

(2.13)

The
2-pointed cyclic bar construction

\prescript{}{2}{B}^{cyc}G
is defined to be the realisation of the simplicial space
\prescript{}{2}{B}^{cyc}G_{\bullet}
with
k
-simplices
G^{2k+2}
, and face and degeneracy maps given by:

\displaystyle d_{i}(u,x_{0}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm},v)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(x_{0}^{+}ux_{0}^{-},x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm},v)&\textrm{ if }i=0\\
(u,x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{k-1}^{\pm},x_{k}^{+}vx_{k}^{-})&\textrm{ if }i=k\\
(u,x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{i}^{\pm}x_{i+1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm},v)&\textrm{ otherwise}\end{cases}

(2.14)

\displaystyle s_{i}(u,x_{0}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm},v)

\displaystyle=(u,x_{0}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{i}^{\pm},(1_{G},1_{G}),x_{i+1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm},v)

(2.15)

where each
x_{i}^{\pm}
represents a pair of elements in
G
, and
x_{i}^{\pm}x_{i+1}^{\pm}
means pairwise multiplication.

There is a map
\prescript{}{2}{B}_{1}:\prescript{}{2}{B}^{cyc}G\to B^{cyc}G
(compare with
[
Gan13
, (2.196)]
) induced by a map
(\prescript{}{2}{B}_{1})_{\bullet}
of simplicial spaces, defined on
k
-simplices by:

(\prescript{}{2}{B}_{1})_{k}(u,x_{0}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm},v)=(vx_{k}^{+}\ldots x_{0}^{+}u,x_{1}^{-},\ldots,x_{k}^{-})

(2.16)

Definition 22
.

Let
Y
be a based space.

There is a map
ev_{0}:\prescript{}{2}{B}^{cyc}\Omega Y\to Y
which is defined on
k
-simplices to send
(u,x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm},v)
to
v(c/2)
, where
c
is the Moore length of
v
.

We define the space
\prescript{}{2}{\overline{B}}^{cyc}\Omega Y
to be the realisation of the simplicial subspace of
\prescript{}{2}{B}^{cyc}\Omega Y
with
k
-simplices consisting of the subspace of tuples
(u,x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm},v)
, such that:

•

Each
x_{i}^{-}
is the reverse loop of
x_{i}^{+}
.

•

If
c
is the Moore length of
u
, then
u|_{[0,c/2]}
is the reverse path of
u|_{[c/2,c]}
, and similarly for
v
.

2.4
Free loop spaces

In Section
8
we will need to use the free loop space and its relationship to the bar construction.

Definition 23
.

Let
Y
be a space. Its
(Moore) free loop space

\mathcal{L}
is the space of pairs
(\gamma,r)
, where
r\geq 0
and
\gamma:[0,r]\to Y
is a map such that
\gamma(0)=\gamma(r)
. We call
r
the
Moore length
of such a loop.

The inclusion of constant loops (with Moore length 0) defines a map
\operatorname{const}:Y\to\mathcal{L}Y
.

For a Moore path
\gamma
, we write
\overline{\gamma}
for its reparametrisation to an ordinary path: if
\gamma
has Moore length
l
,
\overline{\gamma}(t)=\gamma(t/l)
(note this is well-defined since if
l=0
,
\gamma
is constant). We write
\star
for Moore concatenation of loops: i.e.
\gamma\star\delta
is the Moore loop with Moore length given by the sum of those of
\gamma
and
\delta
.

Definition 24
.

Let
Y
be a space. We define a map of simplicial sets
e^{\prime}:B^{cyc}\Sing_{\bullet}(\Omega Y)\to\mathcal{L}\Sing_{\bullet}(Y)
as follows. On
k
-simplices,
e^{\prime}
is defined to be adjoint to the map
S^{1}_{k}\times\Sing_{k}(\Omega Y)^{k+1}\to\Sing_{k}(Y)
which sends a simplex
(\tau^{k}_{i},\gamma_{0},\ldots,\gamma_{k})
to the singular simplex in
Y
sending
s\in\Delta^{k}
to:

\left[\overline{\gamma}_{i+1}^{s}\star\ldots\star\overline{\gamma}^{s}_{k}\star\overline{\gamma}^{s}_{0}\star\ldots\star\overline{\gamma}^{s}_{i-1}\right](r_{k}(s))

(2.17)

We define a map of spaces
e:B^{cyc}\Omega Y\to\mathcal{L}Y
by requiring that the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

\textstyle{|B^{cyc}\Sing_{\bullet}(\Omega Y)|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{|\mathcal{L}\Sing_{\bullet}(Y)|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{B^{cyc}\Omega Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}Y}

(2.18)

{ex}

We recap the definition of the standard simplicial circle, which (by abuse of notation) we write as
S^{1}
. This has
k
-simplices
\{\tau_{0}^{k},\ldots,\tau^{k}_{k}\}
. The face and degeneracy maps are defined by:

\displaystyle d_{j}\tau^{k}_{i}=\begin{cases}\tau^{k-1}_{i-1}&\textrm{ if }j\leq i\\
\tau_{i}^{k-1}&\textrm{ if }j>i\end{cases}

\displaystyle s_{j}\tau^{k}_{i}=\begin{cases}\tau^{k+1}_{i+1}&\textrm{ if }j\leq i\\
\tau^{k+1}_{i}&\textrm{ if }j>i\end{cases}

(2.19)

Note
\tau^{1}_{0}
is the unique non-degenerate 1-simplex.

This can be viewed as the sub-simplicial set of
\Sing_{\bullet}(S^{1})
, where
\tau_{i}^{k}
corresponds to the singular simplex
\Delta^{k}\to S^{1}
sending
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\Delta^{k}
to
1
if
i=k
and to
s_{i-1}
if
i\leq k-1
.

Definition 25

(
[
Jon87
]
)
.

Let
G
be a group-like monoid in the category of Kan complexes. We define a map of simplicial sets
q^{\prime}:B^{cyc}G_{\bullet}\to\mathcal{L}BG_{\bullet}:=\hom_{sSet}(S^{1}_{\bullet},BG_{\bullet})
as follows.

On
k
-simplices,
q^{\prime}_{k}
is defined to be adjoint to the map
S^{1}_{k}\times G^{k+1}_{k}\to G^{k}_{k}
, defined by:

(\tau^{k}_{i},g_{0},\ldots,g_{k})\mapsto(g_{i+1},\ldots,g_{k},g_{0},\ldots,g_{i-1})

(2.20)

For
G
a group-like topological monoid, we define a map of spaces
q:B^{cyc}G\to\mathcal{L}BG
by requiring that the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

\textstyle{B^{cyc}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}BG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{|B^{cyc}\Sing_{\bullet}G|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}}
\textstyle{|\mathcal{L}BG_{\bullet}|}

(2.21)

The two ways of relating cyclic bar constructions to free loop spaces agree in the following sense:

Lemma 26
.

Let
Y
be a based space. Then the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

\textstyle{B^{cyc}\Omega Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}B\Omega Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}ev}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}Y}

(2.22)

Proof 2.5
.

It suffices to check this when
Y
is instead assumed to be a Kan complex
Y_{\bullet}
, and
q,e,ev
are replaced by
q^{\prime},e^{\prime},ev^{\prime}
respectively. The corresponding diagram then commutes on the note: this is a straightforward check from the definitions.

Corollary 27
.

Let
G
be a group-like topological monoid. Then the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

\textstyle{B^{cyc}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{B^{cyc}\Omega BG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}BG}

(2.23)

Proof 2.6
.

Consider the following diagram:

\textstyle{B^{cyc}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{B^{cyc}\iota_{1}}
\textstyle{B^{cyc}\Omega BG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}BG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}B\iota_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}B\Omega BG}

(2.24)

The outer square commutes up to homotopy by naturality of
q
and
\iota_{1}
. The lower triangle commutes up to homotopy by Lemma
26
applied to
BG
, recalling
ev
is a homotopy inverse to
B\iota_{1}
by construction. Since all the maps are equivalences, it follows that the upper triangle also commutes up to homotopy; this is exactly (
2.23
).

Lemma 28
.

Let
Y
be a based space. Then the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

\textstyle{\prescript{}{2}{\overline{B}}^{cyc}\Omega Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ev_{0}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{const}}
\textstyle{\prescript{}{2}{B}^{cyc}\Omega Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prescript{}{2}{B}_{1}}
\textstyle{B^{cyc}\Omega Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}Y}

(2.25)

Proof 2.7
.

Note that (
2.25
) commutes after further concatenating with the map
ev_{0}:\mathcal{L}Y\to Y
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{L}}Y\subseteq\mathcal{L}Y
be the subspace of loops
\gamma:S^{1}\to Y
which, up to reparametrisation (by an autoequivalence of
S^{1}
fixing the basepoint
0\in S^{1}
), are given by some path concatenated with its inverse, i.e. of the form
\delta*\overline{\delta}
,
\delta:[0,1]\to Y
. The concatenation given by going down and then right twice in (
2.25
) naturally factors through
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
; let
f
be this factorisation. Then
\operatorname{const}\circ ev_{0}=ev_{0}\circ f
. The claim then follows from homotopy commutativity of the following diagram:

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{const}}
\textstyle{\overline{\mathcal{L}}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ev_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}Y}

(2.26)

2.5
\Gamma
-spaces

We briefly recap the theory of Segal
\Gamma
-spaces
[
Seg74
]
, following Schlichtkrull’s treatment
[
Sch04
]
. Let
Spc_{*}
be the category of (compactly generated, Hausdorff, well-based) based spaces.

Definition 29
.

Let
\Gamma^{op}
be the category of based finite sets and based maps. A
\Gamma
-space
is a functor
E:\Gamma^{op}\to Spc_{*}
such that
E(\operatorname{pt})=\operatorname{pt}
.

We say a
\Gamma
-space
E
is
special
if the map
E(S\vee T)\to E(S)\times E(T)
is a weak equivalence for all
S,T\in\Gamma^{op}
.

For a special
\Gamma
-space
E
,
E(S^{0})
obtains the structure of a homotopy-commutative
H
-space in a natural way, via the zig-zag:

E(S^{0})\times E(S^{0})\xleftarrow{\simeq}E(S^{0}\vee S^{0})\to E(S^{0})

(2.27)

We say
E
is
very
if the ensuing unital monoid
\pi_{0}E(S^{0})
is a group.

{ex}

Let
G
be a commutative topological monoid. Let
E(S)=G^{\overline{S}}
(where
\overline{S}
is
S
without the basepoint) and, for a map
f:S\to T
in
\Gamma^{op}
, let
E(f):E(S)\to E(T)
be given by multiplying together all of the factors in each fibre of
f
. This is well-defined because
G
is commutative.

This defines a special
\Gamma
-space
E
, which is very if
G
is group-like.

{rmk}

Example
2.5
is the prototypical example of a
\Gamma
-space. However, most
\Gamma
-spaces do not arise in this way; see for example
[
Hat02
, Corollary 4K.7]
.

In general, (very) special
\Gamma
-spaces model (group-like) coherently homotopy-commutative and homotopy-associative topological monoids; more precisely, there is an equivalence (in the sense of Quillen
[
Qui67
]
) between very special
\Gamma
-spaces and infinite loop spaces
[
BF78
, Theorem 5.8]
.

Let
E
be a
\Gamma
-space. We extend the functor
E:\Gamma^{op}\to Spc_{*}
to all based sets by left Kan extension. More explicitly, for a based set
S
,
E(S)
is the (naïve, i.e. not homotopy) colimit over
E(T)
, for
T\subseteq S
a finite subset.

We may also extend
E
to based simplicial sets. For a based simplicial set
S_{\bullet}
, we define
E(S_{\bullet})
to be the realisation of the simplicial based space with
k
-simplices
E(S_{k})
, and face/degeneracy maps induced by those of
S
.

2.5.1
From
\mathcal{I}
to
\Gamma

In this section, we construct a functor from commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids to
\Gamma
-spaces. A similar construction appears in
[
Seg74
, Section 2]
; here we follow
[
Sch04
, Section 5.2]
.

Definition 30
.

Let
S
be a based finite set. We write
\overline{S}
for
S
minus its basepoint, and
\mathcal{P}(S)
for the category of subsets of
\overline{S}
, with morphisms given by inclusions of nested subsets.

We define
\mathcal{S}(S)
to to be the category with objects functors
\theta:\mathcal{P}(S)\to\mathcal{I}
, such that for disjoint
U,V\subseteq\overline{S}
, the images of
\theta(U)
and
\theta(V)
in
\theta(U\cup V)
are disjoint. The morphisms are natural transformations of functors.

{rmk}

\mathcal{P}(\cdot)
defines a contravariant functor from finite based sets to categories: for
f:S\to T
and
U\in\mathcal{P}(S)
, we obtain a functor
f^{*}:\mathcal{P}(T)\to\mathcal{P}(S)
, which is given on objects by
f^{*}(U):=f^{-1}U
. This preserves inclusions and so is naturally defined on morphisms too.

\mathcal{S}(\cdot)
defines a covariant functor from finite based sets to categories, by precomposition with
f^{*}
as above.

{rmk}

There is a canonical equivalence of categories
\mathcal{S}(S)\to\mathcal{I}^{\overline{S}}
, but no canonical functor in the other direction without making a choice of ordering on
S
(which cannot be done coherently in our subsequent constructions).

{rmk}

We think of objects of
\mathcal{S}(S)
as a way of coherently adding together tuples in
\mathcal{I}
indexed by subsets of
\overline{S}
: for each
s\in\overline{S}
we have some
\theta(s)\in\mathcal{I}
, corresponding to a finite approximation
E(\theta(s))
of
E_{h\mathcal{I}}
, and for each subset
U\subseteq\overline{S}
, we have a “coherent” way of applying the multiplication in
E
to the
E(\theta(s))
for
s\in U
.

Definition 31
.

Let
E
be a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid, and let
S
be a finite based set.
E
defines a functor
E_{S}:\mathcal{S}(S)\to Spc_{*}
, defined as follows.

For an object
\theta\in\mathcal{S}(S)
,
E_{S}(\theta)
is defined to be
\prod_{s\in\overline{S}}E\left(\theta(s)\right)
.

For a natural transformation
\theta\to\phi
of functors
\mathcal{P}(S)\to\mathcal{I}
, this induces morphisms
\theta(s)\to\phi(s)
for all
s\in\overline{S}
, and by applying the functor
E
this induces maps of spaces
E_{S}(\theta)\to E_{S}(\phi)
.

Let
f:S\to T
be a map of finite based spaces. There is a natural transformation
E_{ST}:E_{S}\to E_{T}\circ f_{*}
of functors
\mathcal{S}(S)\to Spc_{*}
constructed as follows. Let
\theta\in\mathcal{S}(S)
. We define:

E_{ST}:\mathcal{S}(S)(\theta)=\prod\limits_{s\in\overline{S}}\theta(s)\to\prod\limits_{t\in\overline{T}}\theta(f^{-1}\{t\})=E_{T}\circ f_{*}(T)(\theta)

(2.28)

so that the map is induced by multiplication in
E
induced by the injection
\sqcup_{s\in f^{-1}\{t\}}[\theta(s)]\to[\theta(f^{-1}\{t\})]
induced by
\theta
, for each
t\in\overline{T}
.

Definition 32
.

Let
E
be a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid. We define a
\Gamma
-space
E^{\Gamma}
as follows.

Let
S
be a finite based set. We set:

E^{\Gamma}(S):=\underset{\theta\in\mathcal{S}(S)}{\mathrm{hocolim}}\,E_{S}(\theta)

(2.29)

For a morphism
f:S\to T
of finite based sets, applying the construction of Definition
5
to the functor
f_{*}:\mathcal{S}(S)\to\mathcal{S}(T)
and the natural transformation
E_{S}\to E_{T}\circ f_{*}
defines a map of based spaces
E^{\Gamma}(S)\to E^{\Gamma}(T)
. It is clear that this is compatible with composition and so
E^{\Gamma}
defines a functor
\Gamma^{op}\to Spc_{*}
.

Lemma 33

(
[
Sch04
, Proposition 5.3]
)
.

Let
E
be a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid.

Then
E^{\Gamma}
is a special
\Gamma
-space. If
E
is group-like,
E^{\Gamma}
is a very special
\Gamma
-space.

{rmk}

[
SS12
]
shows that the functor
\cdot^{\Gamma}
is part of a Quillen equivalence between group-like commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids and very special
\Gamma
-spaces.

2.5.2
Circles

{ex}

Let
E
be a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid. We compute
E^{\Gamma}(S^{0}_{\bullet})
. We consider
S^{0}_{\bullet}
with its standard model as a simplicial set: there are exactly two
k
-simplices for each
k
, one of which is over the basepoint.

Since
\overline{S^{0}}=\operatorname{pt}
, its
k
-simplices
\overline{S^{0}_{k}}
are each a single point. Therefore each
E^{\Gamma}(S^{0}_{k})
is naturally identified with
E^{\Gamma}(S^{0})
, with all the face and degeneracy maps being the identity. It follows that the inclusion of the zero-simplices
E^{\Gamma}(S^{0}_{0})
into the realisation
E^{\Gamma}(S^{0}_{\bullet})
is a homeomorphism.

\mathcal{S}(S^{0})
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathcal{I}
, so we find that
E^{\Gamma}(S^{0})
and hence
E^{\Gamma}(S^{0}_{\bullet})
are both naturally homeomorphic to
E_{h\mathcal{I}}
.

Lemma 34

(
[
Sch04
, Proposition 5.3]
)
.

Let
E
be a convergent commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid. Then there is a weak equivalence of spaces
BE_{h\mathcal{I}}\to E^{\Gamma}(S^{1})
.

Lemma 35

(
[
Sch04
, Proposition 6.3]
)
.

Let
E
be a convergent commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid. Then there is a weak equivalence of spaces
F:B^{cyc}E_{h\mathcal{I}}\simeq E^{\Gamma}(S^{1}_{+})
. Here
S^{1}_{+}
is
S^{1}
with a disjoint basepoint added.

The equivalence of Lemma
35
is constructed as a zig-zag. We recap the construction here as it will be used explicitly in Section
8
. The intermediate space in the zig-zag,
V^{cyc}E
, is defined to be the realisation of the simplicial space
V^{cyc}E_{\bullet}
, with
k
-simplices:

V^{cyc}E_{k}=\underset{(n_{0},\ldots,n_{k},\theta)\in\mathcal{I}^{k+1}\times\mathcal{S}((S^{1}_{+})_{k})}{\mathrm{hocolim}}E(n_{0}+\theta(\tau_{0}))\times\ldots\times E(n_{k}+\theta(\tau_{k}))

(2.30)

The natural functors
\mathcal{I}^{k+1}\to\mathcal{I}^{k+1}\times\mathcal{S}((S^{1}_{+})_{k})\leftarrow\mathcal{S}((S^{1}_{+})_{k})
induce maps of spaces
B^{cyc}E_{h\mathcal{I}}\to V^{cyc}E\leftarrow\mathcal{S}(S^{1}_{+})
, which are shown to be equivalences in
[
Sch04
]
using the convergence condition.

Lemma 36
.

Let
E
be a very special
\Gamma
-space. Then there is a natural equivalence
E(S^{1}_{+})\to\mathcal{L}E(S^{1})
.

This equivalence is constructed on
[
Sch04
, Page 668]
.

For
E
a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid, we now have two equivalences between the free loop space and the cyclic bar construction, coming from Definition
25
and Lemma
36
. They agree in the appropriate sense:

Lemma 37
.

Let
E
be a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid. Then the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

\textstyle{B^{cyc}E_{h\mathcal{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{V^{cyc}E}
\textstyle{E^{\Gamma}(S^{1}_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}BE_{h\mathcal{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}E^{\Gamma}(S^{1})}

(2.31)

where
q
is as in Definition
25
and the right vertical arrow is the map from Lemma
36
.

Proof 2.8
.

This exact statement is proved in
[
Sch04
, Proof of Theorem 1.2]
. We remark on the differences on notation: what Schlichtkrull calls
W^{cyc}
we call
V^{cyc}
, and what he calls
GL_{1}(R)
3
3

3

Note that the
X
appearing in
[
Sch04
, Proof of Theorem 1.2]
is a typo and should say
GL_{1}(R)
.
can be replaced with our
E
without affecting the argument.

2.6
Delooping

Definition 38
.

Let
E
be a
\Gamma
-space. We define the
\Gamma
-space
BE
so that
BE(S)
is the realisation of the simplicial space
[k]\mapsto E(S\times[k])
.

The category of
\Gamma
-spaces is presentable
[
Sch99
, Appendix A]
;
B
is constructed as a colimit and hence preserves colimits, so
B
has a right adjoint
\Omega
. For very special
E
, the unit
E\to\Omega BE
of the adjunction is a weak equivalences (i.e. induces an equivalence of spaces for all
S\in\Gamma^{op}
)
[
Seg74
]
.

Lemma 39

(
[
Seg74
]
)
.

Let
E
be a very special
\Gamma
-space. Then
BE
is also very special.

Lemma 40
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be a based simplicial set and
E
a very special
\Gamma
-space. Then there are natural equivalences
E(X_{\bullet}\wedge S^{i})\simeq B^{i}(X_{\bullet})
for all
i
.

Proof 2.9
.

Since both sides preserve colimits send disjoint unions to products, it suffices to check this when
X_{\bullet}
is
S^{0}
. For
i=1
, this is Lemma
34
, and for
i\geq 2
, we may apply Lemma
34
iteratively using
S^{i}=(S^{1})^{\wedge i}
.

The stable homotopy groups of spheres act on very special
\Gamma
-spaces in the following way.

Definition 41
.

Let
E
be a very special
\Gamma
-space. Let
\alpha\in\pi_{i}\mathbb{S}
. We define
\alpha_{*}:E(S^{i})\to E(S^{0})
to be the composition:

E(S^{i})\simeq\Omega^{k}E(S^{i+k})\xrightarrow{\Omega^{k}E(\alpha^{\prime})}\Omega^{k}(S^{k})\simeq E(S^{0})

(2.32)

where
\alpha^{\prime}:S^{i+k}\to S^{k}
is an unstable representative of
\alpha
.

This is a well-defined map of spaces, up to homotopy.

{rmk}

Let
X
be a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid, and
\alpha\in\pi_{1}\mathbb{S}
. By Lemma
34
,
\alpha
then induces a map
BX_{h\mathcal{I}}\to X_{h\mathcal{I}}
, well-defined up to homotopy.

Let
\eta\in\pi_{1}\mathbb{S}
be the stable Hopf map. Schlichtkrull proves the action of
\eta
on an infinite loop space admits the following alternative description; this will be crucial in Section
8
:

Proposition 42

(
[
Sch04
, Proposition 7.3]
)
.

Let
E
be a very special
\Gamma
-space. Then the following composition is homotopic to
\eta_{*}
:

E(S^{1})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{const}}\mathcal{L}E(S^{1})\xleftarrow{\simeq}E(S^{1}_{+})\xrightarrow{S^{1}_{+}\to S^{0}}E(S^{0})

(2.33)

where the backwards arrow is the equivalence from Lemma
36
.

2.7
Symmetric spectra

Definition 43

(
[
HSS00
, Definition 1.2.2]
, cf. also
[
MMSS01
]
)
.

A
symmetric spectrum

Z
consists of:

•

A sequence of based spaces
Z_{n}
, along with (left) actions of the symmetric group
\mathrm{Sym}_{n}
.

•

Maps
\varepsilon_{n}:S^{1}\wedge Z_{n}\to Z_{n+1}
.

such that the compositions:

\varepsilon^{i}_{n}:S^{i}\wedge Z_{n}=(S^{1})^{\wedge i}\wedge Z\to(S^{1})^{\wedge(i-1)}\wedge Z_{1+n}\to\ldots\to Z_{i+n}

(2.34)

are
\mathrm{Sym}_{i}\times\mathrm{Sym}_{n}
-equivariant.

A symmetric spectrum
Z
is
convergent
if the connectivity of the maps
\varepsilon^{i}_{n}
goes to
\infty
as
n\to\infty
.

We will often refer symmetric spectra just as spectra.

Definition 44
.

A
ring spectrum
is a spectrum
R
along with:

•

\mathrm{Sym}_{n}
-equivariant maps
\iota:S^{n}\to R_{n}
.

•

\mathrm{Sym}_{i}\times\mathrm{Sym}_{j}
-equivariant maps
\mu:R_{i}\wedge R_{j}\to R_{i+j}
.

such that for all
i,j,k
,

•

\iota_{i+j}=\varepsilon^{i}(\id_{S^{i}}\wedge\iota_{j})

•

(Unitality): The following diagrams commute:

\textstyle{S^{i}\wedge R_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{i}_{j}}
\textstyle{R_{i}\wedge R_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{R_{i}\wedge S^{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{swap}}
\scriptstyle{\id_{R_{j}}\wedge\iota_{j}}
\textstyle{R_{i}\wedge R_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{R_{i+j}}
\textstyle{S^{j}\wedge R_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{j}_{i}}
\textstyle{R_{i+j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{ij}}
\textstyle{R_{i+j}}

(2.35)

where
\sigma_{ij}\in\mathrm{Sym}_{i+j}
swaps the first
i
and last
j
entries.

•

(Associativity): The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{R_{i}\wedge R_{j}\wedge R_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{R_{i}\wedge R_{j+k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{R_{i+j}\wedge R_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{R_{i+j+k}}

(2.36)

R
is
commutative
if the following diagram also commutes:

\textstyle{R_{i}\wedge R_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{swap}}
\textstyle{R_{i+j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{ij}}
\textstyle{R_{j}\wedge R_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{R_{i+j}}

(2.37)

{rmk}

The category of symmetric spectra is a symmetric monoidal
[
HSS00
, Section 2]
, and a (commutative) ring spectrum is exactly a (commutative) ring object in this category.

2.8
Units of ring spectra

Definition 45

(
[
Sch04
, Section 2.3]
)
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring spectrum. There is a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
\Omega^{\infty}R
sending
n
to
\Omega^{n}R_{n}
, with
\mathcal{I}
-space structure maps constructed as follows. For a morphism
f:i\to j
in
\mathcal{I}
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{i}R_{i}
,
f
sends
\alpha
to the composition:

S^{j}\xrightarrow{\tilde{f}^{-1}}S^{j}\xrightarrow{=}S^{j-i}\wedge S^{i}\xrightarrow{\alpha}S^{j-i}\wedge R_{i}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{i}^{j-i}}R_{j}\xrightarrow{\tilde{f}}R_{j}

(2.38)

where
\tilde{f}:[j]\to[j]
is defined so that if
x>j-i
,
\tilde{f}=f(x-j+i)
, and is order-preserving on the first
j-i
entries. The product structure is the composition:

\Omega^{i}R_{i}\wedge\Omega^{j}R_{j}\to\Omega^{i+j}R_{i}\wedge R_{j}\xrightarrow{\Omega^{i+j}\mu}\Omega^{i+j}R_{i+j}

(2.39)

By abuse of notation, we also sometimes write
\Omega^{\infty}R
for
\Omega^{\infty}R_{h\mathcal{I}}
.

Definition 46

(
[
Sch04
, Section 2.3]
)
.

Let
R
be a commutative symmetric ring spectrum. There is a corresponding group-like commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
GL_{1}(R)
. Each
GL_{1}(R)(i)
is defined to be the union of components of
\Omega^{\infty}R(i)
, which admit an element
c
such that there is some
j
and
c^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\infty}R(j)
such that
\mu(c,c^{\prime})\in\Omega^{\infty}R(i+j)
lies in the trivial path component. The product structure is inherited from
\Omega^{\infty}R
.

Note that the assignment
R\mapsto GL_{1}(R)
is functorial in
R
.

{rmk}

GL_{1}(R)
corresponds to an infinite loop space under the equivalence of
[
SS12
]
and hence gives rise to a connective spectrum, denoted
gl_{1}(R)
, and similarly delooping this infinite loop space gives another connective spectrum
bgl_{1}(R)
, which is equivalent to the shift
\Sigma gl_{1}(R)
. The map of infinite loop spaces induced by a class
\alpha\in\pi_{1}\mathbb{S}
as in Definition
41
gives a map of spectra
\alpha_{*}:bgl_{1}(R)\to gl_{1}(R)
whose delooping is (
2.32
). Explicitly, this is the map
\Sigma gl_{1}(R)\to gl_{1}(R)
induced by
\alpha
using the action of
\pi_{*}\mathbb{S}
on any spectrum.

{ex}

When
R=\mathbb{S}
is the sphere spectrum,
GL_{1}(\mathbb{S})\simeq G
is as considered in Example
2.2
. Note however that for an arbitrary commutative ring spectrum
R
, each
GL_{1}(R)(n)
is not a monoid, so we cannot apply
B\cdot
levelwise to obtain a new commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
BGL_{1}(R)(\cdot)
: one must apply
\cdot_{h\mathcal{I}}
and then apply
B\cdot
.

{rmk}

If
R
is a commutative ring spectrum such that
R
is convergent, so are both
\Omega^{\infty}R
and
GL_{1}(R)
.

2.9
Thom spaces

Definition 47
.

A
Thom space
is a pair
(X,E)
, where
X
is a space and
E
is a vector bundle over
X
. A
morphism

(X,E)\to(X^{\prime},E^{\prime})
is a map of spaces
f:X\to X^{\prime}
along with an isomorphism of vector bundles
E\to f^{*}E
. We write
\Thom
for the ensuing category of Thom spaces.

The category of Thom spaces is symmetric monoidal, with
(X,E)\times(X^{\prime},E^{\prime}):=(X\times X^{\prime},E\boxplus E^{\prime})
.

The category of Thom spaces is tensored over both the category of spaces by setting
(X,E)\times Y:=(X\times Y,E)
, and the category of (finite-dimensional) vector spaces by setting
(X,E)\times V:=(X,E\oplus V)
. Similarly, if
E^{\prime}\to X
is another vector bundle, we set
(X,E)\oplus E^{\prime}
to be
(X,E\oplus E^{\prime})
.

Given a Thom space
(X,E)
, we may take its Thom space (in the classical sense)
\Thom(E\to X)
; the convention that we use is that this is the quotient of the fibrewise one-point compactification of the total space
\mathrm{Tot}(E\to X)
by the section at infinity.

Definition 48
.

A
Thom
\mathcal{I}
-space

X
consists of Thom spaces
X(n)
for each
n\in\mathcal{I}
, and for each morphism
f:n\to m
in
\mathcal{I}
, a map of Thom spaces
f(n):X(n)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{m-n}\to X(m)
, where
m-n
denotes the finite set
[m]\setminus f[n]
.

We require that, for morphisms
n\to m\to l
, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{X(n)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{m-n}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{l-m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{X(n)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{l-n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X(m)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{l-m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X(l)}

(2.40)

A
morphism

X\to Y
of Thom
\mathcal{I}
-spaces consists of maps
X(n)\to Y(n)
for each
n
, such that for any morphism
n\to m
in
\mathcal{I}
the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{X(n)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{m-n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y(n)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{m-n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X(m)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y(m)}

(2.41)

The category
\Thom^{\mathcal{I}}
of Thom
\mathcal{I}
-spaces is symmetric monoidal, via the same construction of the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{I}
-spaces (cf. Remark
9
and
[
SS13
, Section 2]
): for example, a map
X\otimes Y\to Z
consists of the same data as a sequence of maps in
\sSet\Vect

X(n)\times Y(m)\to Z(n+m)
, satisfying a similar requirement to Definition
9
. There is a ‘shift functor’ on Thom
\mathcal{I}
-spaces, where
X[1]
is defined so that
X[1](n)=X(n)\oplus\mathbb{R}
.

A
(commutative) Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
is a (commutative) monoid in the category
\Thom^{\mathcal{I}}
.

{rmk}

Note that a Thom
\mathcal{I}
-space is not the same as an
\mathcal{I}
-object in the category
\Thom
.

Since commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids model infinite loop spaces, if
X
is a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid then
X_{h\mathcal{I}}
admits an “inversion” map
-1
. This is not typically realised on the finite approximations
X(n)
, so we introduce the following:

Definition 49
.

Let
X
be a group-like commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid and
f:Z\to X(n)
a map. Then an
inverse
to
f
comprises

1.

positive integers
m
and
l
with
[n]\sqcup[m]\hookrightarrow[l]
;

2.

a map (schematically denoted)
f^{-1}:Z\to X(m)
;

3.

a nullhomotopy of the composite

Z\to X(n)\times X(m)\to X(l)

Lemma 50
.

If
Z
is a finite-dimensional cell complex, then for
m
and
l
sufficiently large, a map
f:Z\to X(n)
admits an inverse. Moreover, the connectivity of the space of choices (i)-(iii) as in Definition
49
becomes unbounded as
m,l\to\infty
.

Proof 2.10
.

Both statements follow from the fact that
X_{h\mathcal{I}}
has the homotopy type of an infinite loop space.

Now if
E
is a commutative Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid and
(Z,F)\to E(n)
is a map of Thom spaces, then choosing an inverse to
f:Z\to\Base E(n)
as above, one obtains

F\oplus(f^{-1})^{*}E(m)\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{rk}(E(l))}

(2.42)

and this trivialisation is canonical up to homotopy.

Definition 51
.

There is a symmetric monoidal functor from
\Thom

\mathcal{I}
-spaces to symmetric spectra, sending
(X,E)
to the symmetric spectrum sending
n
to the Thom space
\Thom(E(n)\to X(n))
.

There is a symmetric monoidal functor
\Base
from
\Thom
(
\mathcal{I}
-)spaces to (
\mathcal{I}
-)spaces, sending
Y=(X,E)
to
X
.

{rmk}

Let
(X,E)
be a (commutative) Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid. Its image under the functor to symmetric spectra is a (commutative) ring spectrum; explicitly, its
n^{th}
space is
\Thom(E(n)\to X(n))
. If
(X,E)
is convergent, so is the corresponding ring spectrum.

Definition 52
.

Let
X,Y
be Thom (
\mathcal{I}
-)spaces, and
f:X\to Y
a map.

f
is an
equivalence
if
\Base(f)
is and that
f
is
k
-connected
if
\Base(f)
is. If
Z
is a Thom
\mathcal{I}
-space,
Z
is
convergent
if
\Base(Z)
is convergent.

Lemma 53
.

Let
f:(X,E)\to(X^{\prime},E^{\prime})
be a
k
-connected map of Thom spaces, and
g:(Z,F)\to(X^{\prime},E^{\prime})
a map of Thom spaces. Consider the lifting problem:

\textstyle{(X,E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{(Z,E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{(X^{\prime},E^{\prime})}

(2.43)

If
Z
is a CW complex of dimension
\leq k
, then there exists a homotopy lift
h
of Thom spaces as in (
2.43
). Explicitly, this means a map
h
as in the diagram, along with a homotopy between the two ways around the diagram.

If instead
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
is a CW subcomplex, a homotopy lift
h
of (
2.43
) is given on
(Z^{\prime},E|_{Z^{\prime}})
and
Z
is obtained from
Z^{\prime}
by attaching cells of dimension
\leq k
, then there exists a homotopy lift over all of
Z
, extending the given one over
Z^{\prime}
.

Proof 2.11
.

This is standard in spaces. The case of Thom spaces follows from the fact that an isomorphism of vector bundles over
Z\times\{0\}
can always be extended to one over
Z\times[0,1]
.

Many of our constructions are more naturally done on the level of simplicial sets, so it will be convenient to work with the following variants of the above definitions.

Definition 54
.

For a simplicial set
S_{\bullet}
, a
vector bundle
on
S_{\bullet}
is a vector bundle (in the usual sense) on its geometric realisation
|S_{\bullet}|
.

Definition 55
.

A
Thom simplicial set
consists of a pair
(X_{\bullet},E)
, where
X_{\bullet}
is a simplicial set and
E
is a vector bundle over
X_{\bullet}
. The category of Thom simplicial sets is symmetric monoidal, by setting
(X_{\bullet},E)\otimes(X^{\prime}_{\bullet},E^{\prime}):=(X_{\bullet}\times X^{\prime}_{\bullet},E\boxplus E^{\prime})
(using the canonical homeomorphism
|X_{\bullet}|\times|X^{\prime}_{\bullet}|\simeq|X_{\bullet}\times X^{\prime}_{\bullet}|
).

The
geometric realisation

|(X_{\bullet},E)|
of a Thom simplicial set or Thom
\mathcal{I}
-simplicial set is given by applying the geometric realisation functor to the underlying simplicial set(s).

The functor
\Base
from Thom (
\mathcal{I}
-)simplicial sets to (
\mathcal{I}
-)simplicial sets, as well as the notions of connectivity and equivalence, are defined the same as in spaces.

3
Abstract discs

In this section, we give models for Bott periodicity well-adapted to index theory for Cauchy-Riemann operators; this is largely similar to
[
PS24b
, Section 3]
, with some modifications to account for commutativity, and now incorporating the language of Section
2
.

3.1
Tangential pairs

Definition 56
.

A
(graded) commutative tangential pair

\Psi
consists of a pair
(\Theta\to\Phi)
of group-like connected commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids, equipped with maps to
BO
and
BS_{\pm}U
fitting into the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\Theta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BS_{\pm}U}

(3.1)

where
BS_{\pm}U
is as in Example
2.2
. We require that the homotopy fibre
F
of
\Theta\to\Phi
is simply connected.

We say that
\Phi
is
oriented
if the map on homotopy fibres
F\to\widetilde{U/O}
induces the zero map on
\pi_{2}
, and
unoriented
otherwise.

One of the main differences between our set-up here and in
[
PS24b
]
is that in
[
PS24b
]
, we did not impose any sort of commutativity.

{rmk}

Lemma
127
motivates the
\pi_{2}
condition for orientability.

{ex}

We define
fr
to be the commutative tangential pair
(\Theta=\operatorname{pt},\Phi=\operatorname{pt})
.

Commutative tangential pairs form a category in a natural way, and
fr
is initial in the category of commutative tangential pairs.

{ex}

It is often convenient to simplify the discussion by working with commutative tangential pairs where
\Phi=\operatorname{pt}
and
\Theta=F
some commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
F
over
\widetilde{U/O}
:

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{pt}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{U/O}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BS_{\pm}U}

(3.2)

{rmk}

In
[
PS24b
]
, this corresponds to what we called a
tangential structure
(as opposed to pair).

In Example
3.1
, a
\Phi
-orientation on a Liouville domain
X
is a stable framing; we may modify the construction to be able to obtain similar Fukaya-categorical structures when
X
only has a polarisation:

Definition 57
.

Let
f:F\to\widetilde{U/O}
be a map of commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids. Let
\Theta_{F}=BO\times F
,
\Phi_{F}=BO
, equipped with the maps shown in the right square of the following commutative diagram, with rows homotopy fibration sequences:

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{pt}\times\id}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{BO\times F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Proj_{1}}
\scriptstyle{Proj_{1}+(Re\circ f)}
\textstyle{BO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot\otimes\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{U/O}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Re}
\textstyle{BO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot\otimes\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{BS_{\pm}U}

(3.3)

where
Proj_{1}
denotes projection to the first factor.

We call this
(\Theta_{F},\Phi_{F})
the
polarised tangential pair
of
F
.

{ex}

We let
\widetilde{U/O}^{or}
be the commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid obtained from
\widetilde{U/O}
by taking 2-connected cover for each
n\in\mathcal{I}
. Note that for each
n
, we have a homotopy fibre sequence:

\widetilde{U/O}^{or}(n)\to\widetilde{U/O}(n)\xrightarrow{w_{2}\circ Re}K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2)

(3.4)

where the second map classifies the second Stiefel-Whitney class of a totally real subbundle of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

For a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
F
over
\widetilde{U/O}^{or}
, the polarised tangential pair of
F
is oriented, since
\pi_{2}\widetilde{U/O}^{or}=0
.

3.2
Abstract discs: fixed domain case

In this section, using Cauchy-Riemann operators, we define a model for
BO
as an infinite loop space, along with a universal bundle over it. We use the models and language developed in the previous sections to describe it.

Consider the standard Riemann disk with a single puncture,
D^{2}\setminus\{1\}
. We choose an
output strip-like end
near 1 (cf.
[
PS24a
, Section 7.3]
); explicitly, this is a biholomorphism
\varepsilon
from
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times[0,1]
onto a neighbourhood of the puncture. We choose a Riemannian metric on
D^{2}\setminus\{1\}
, standard over the strip-like end.

Definition 58
.

Consider the trivial vector bundle
\mathbb{C}\times(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times[0,1])\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times[0,1]
, and the real subbundle
