\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\{1\})\sqcup i\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\{0\})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\{0,1\}
.

We define a map of pairs
(\widetilde{e},\widetilde{f}):(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\{0,1\})\to(BO(1),BS_{\pm}U(1))
compatibly classifying the above vector bundles as follows.

We set
\widetilde{e}
to be constant at the basepoint. Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to{U/O}(1)
be the path given by
\gamma(t)=e^{\frac{i\pi}{2}}\cdot i\mathbb{R}
, and
\widetilde{\gamma}
its unique lift to the universal cover
\widetilde{U/O}(1)
sending
0
to the basepoint. We take
\widetilde{f}
to be the composition of
\widetilde{\gamma}
with the map
\widetilde{U/O}(1)\to BO(1)
.

We call this choice of vector bundle and pair of classifying maps the
standard puncture datum
.

Definition 59
.

For each
v,n\in\mathcal{I}
, there is a Thom simplicial set
(\mathbb{U}(v,n)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}(v,n))
, defined as follows. A
0
-simplex in
\mathbb{U}(v,n)_{\bullet}
consists of the following data:

•

A complex vector bundle
E
over
D^{2}\setminus\{1\}
of rank
n
.

•

A real subbundle over the boundary
F\subseteq E|_{\partial D}
, cf. Figure
1
.

The pair
(E,F)
is called the
bundle pair
;
E
is the
complex part
and
F
the
real part
.

We call
n
the
rank
.

•

A classifying map
D^{2}\setminus\{1\}\to BS_{\pm}U(n)
for
E
.

•

A classifying map
\partial D^{2}\setminus 1\to BO(n)
for
F
, compatible with the classifying map for
E
.

We require that the classifying maps agree with (the
n
-fold direct sum of) the standard puncture data over the strip-like end.

•

A 1-form
Y\in\Omega^{0,1}(E)
, and a Hermitian connection (and metric)
\nabla
on
E
.

We require some additional data and conditions (cf.
[
PS24b
, Definition 3.9]
for details). We define a
Cauchy-Riemann operator
:

D^{CR}=\overline{\partial}^{\nabla}+Y:\Gamma_{W^{2,\kappa}}\left(D^{2}\setminus\{1\},E,F\right)\to\Omega^{0,1}_{W^{2,\kappa-1}}\left(D^{2}\setminus\{1\},E\right)

(3.5)

where the subscripts
\cdot_{W^{2,\cdot}}
denote passing to suitable Sobolev completions. This operator is always Fredholm, and in particular its cokernel is finite-dimensional.

•

A linear map
f:\mathbb{R}^{v}\to\Omega^{0,1}_{W^{2,\kappa-1}}(D^{2}\setminus\{1\},E)
, such that
D^{CR}+f
is surjective.

A
k
-simplex consists of a smoothly-varying family
(E_{t},F_{t},Y_{t},\ldots)_{t\in\Delta^{k}}
of all of the above data, parametrised by the standard
k
-simplex
\Delta^{k}
.

We call
\mathbb{U}(v,n)
(and its variants we encounter later) a
space of abstract discs
; we think of a 0-simplex as such an abstract disc.

The vector bundle
\mathbb{V}(v,n)
over
|\mathbb{U}(v,n)_{\bullet}|
is defined so that for a simplex
(\sigma_{t})_{t\in\Delta^{k}}=(E_{t},F_{t},\ldots)_{t\in\Delta^{k}}
as above, the restriction of
\mathbb{V}(v,n)
to the realisation of
(\sigma_{t})_{t}
has fibre over
t\in\Delta^{k}
given by the kernel of the associated Cauchy-Riemann operator constructed with the Cauchy-Riemann data over
t
,
\ker(D^{CR}_{t})
. The total space has a natural topology on it, hence forming a vector bundle.

\mathbb{C}^{n}
i\mathbb{R}^{n}
\mathbb{R}^{n}
\varepsilon
E
F

Figure 1:
Boundary conditions of an abstract disc.

{rmk}

Together, the classifying maps of the bundle pair and the puncture datum define a map of pairs
(D^{2}\setminus\{1\},\partial D^{2}\setminus\{1\})\to(BS_{\pm}U(n),BO(n))
.

Definition 60
.

Let
[v]\to[v^{\prime}]
be an injection. There is an induced map
\mathbb{U}(v,n)_{\bullet}\to\mathbb{U}(v^{\prime},n)_{\bullet}
, defined as follows. This sends a simplex
(\sigma)_{t}=(E_{t},F_{t},Y_{t},\nabla_{t},f_{t},\ldots)
to
(E_{t},F_{t},Y_{t},\nabla_{t},f^{\prime}_{t},\ldots)
, where
f^{\prime}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{v}=\mathbb{R}^{v^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{v-v^{\prime}}\to\Omega^{0,1}_{W^{2,\kappa-1}}(D^{2}\setminus\{1\},E)
is obtained from
f
by precomposing with projection to
\mathbb{R}^{v^{\prime}}
. All the extra auxiliary data is taken to be the same.

There is a natural isomorphism between the pullback of
\mathbb{V}(v^{\prime},n)
and
\mathbb{V}(v,n)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{v^{\prime}-v}
.

Thse maps assemble to endow the pair
(\mathbb{U}(\cdot,n)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}(\cdot,n))
with the structure of a Thom
\mathcal{I}
-simplicial set.

Definition 61
.

Let
[v]\sqcup[v^{\prime}]\to[v^{\prime\prime}]
and
[n]\sqcup[n]\to[n^{\prime\prime}]
be isomorphisms in
\mathcal{I}
(i.e. bijections). We define a map of Thom simplicial sets:

\oplus:\left(\mathbb{U}(v,n)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{U}(v^{\prime},n^{\prime})_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)\to\left(\mathbb{U}(v^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime\prime})_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)

(3.6)

This takes two abstract discs (or families of them, parametrised by
\Delta^{k}
), and direct sums all pieces of data. The classifying map for the complex (resp. real) parts of the boundary conditions is given by the sum of those on the summands, using the map
BS_{\pm}U(n)\times BS_{\pm}U(n^{\prime})\to BS_{\pm}U(n^{\prime\prime})
(resp.
BO(n)\times BO(n^{\prime})\to BO(n^{\prime\prime})
) induced by the given isomorphism in
\mathcal{I}
.

Definition 62
.

We fix, once and for all,
D_{0}\in\mathbb{U}(0,1)_{0}
a choice of abstract disc, with
E=\mathbb{C}
trivial (note the space of choices of
F
is given by the space of loops in the universal cover of
U/O(1)
and is therefore contractible; the space of further choices is contractible too:
[
MS12
, Theorem C.1.10]
implies the Cauchy-Riemann operator is automatically surjective, without stabilisation). Note the index vector space
\mathbb{V}_{D_{0}}
(i.e. the fibre of
\mathbb{V}
over the 0-simplex
D_{0}
) is 0.

Let
f:[n]\to[n^{\prime}]
be an injection. Let
D_{0}^{\oplus n^{\prime}-n}
be the 0-simplex in
\mathbb{U}(0,n^{\prime}-n)
given by applying
\oplus
to
(n^{\prime}-n)
copies of
D_{0}
. We define a map of Thom simplicial sets:

\left(\mathbb{U}(v,n)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)\to\left(\mathbb{U}(v,n^{\prime})_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)

(3.7)

given by applying
\oplus
to the bijection
[n]\sqcup[n^{\prime}-n]\to[n^{\prime}]
which is
f
on the first factor (and can be anything in the second factor, it does not affect the construction), and inserting
D_{0}^{\oplus n^{\prime}-n}
.

This assembles to make
(\mathbb{U}(v,\cdot),\mathbb{V})
into an
\mathcal{I}
-object in
\Thom
.

From
[
Sei08
, Proposition 11.13]
, we may compute the rank of the index bundle:

Lemma 63
.

The rank of the vector bundle
\mathbb{V}(v,n)
is
v
.

The above structures commute with each other in the appropriate sense. Combining them, we obtain:

Proposition 64
.

\left(\mathbb{U}(\cdot,\cdot)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)
forms a commutative monoid in the category of
\mathcal{I}
-objects in the category of Thom
\mathcal{I}
-simplicial sets.

In particular, taking the diagonal
v=n
, we obtain a commutative Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
n\mapsto\left(\mathbb{U}(n,n)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)
.

It is this diagonal version that we will use throughout much of the paper: it is much more convenient to deal with a single
\mathcal{I}
-factor.

{Not}

We drop the
\cdot_{\bullet}
to denote taking realisation of this simplicial space: for example,
\mathbb{U}(v,n):=|\mathbb{U}(v,n)_{\bullet}|
.

When it is clear which index bundle we are referring to, we often drop decorations and write
\mathbb{V}
for
\mathbb{V}(v,n)
(or its variants).

Lemma 65
.

There is a weak equivalence of commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids:

\mathbb{U}\to\mathrm{hofib}(\Omega BO\to\Omega BS_{\pm}U)

(3.8)

Proof 3.1
.

Sending an abstract disc to the classifying map for the real part of its boundary conditions determines a map
\mathbb{U}(n)\to\Omega BO(n)
. The classifying map for the complex part determines a nullhomotopy of the composite to
\Omega BS_{\pm}U(n)
; together, this defines the desired map. That this is an equivalence follows from the proof of
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.25]
: essentially this is because for fixed boundary conditions
(E,F)
, the space of all extra data as in Definition
59
is contractible as
n\to\infty
.

{rmk}

All the above constructions work if
BO
and
BS_{\pm}U
are replaced with
BO
and
BU
respectively. In that case,
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.27]
shows the classifying map for
\mathbb{V}
,
\mathbb{U}\to BO
, is an equivalence. Combined with Lemma
65
, this provides an index-theoretic proof of Bott periodicity
\Omega U/O\simeq BO
as infinite loop spaces We elaborate on this further in Section
3.3
.

Definition 66
.

Let
\Psi=(\Theta,\Phi)
be a commutative tangential pair. We define
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}
to be the homotopy pullback of the following diagram:

\textstyle{\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{hofib}(\Omega\Theta\to\Omega\Phi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{hofib}(\Omega BO\to\Omega BS_{\pm}U)}

(3.9)

and
\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(n)
to be pullback of
\mathbb{V}(n)
. The pair
(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(\cdot),\mathbb{V}^{\Psi})
forms a commutative Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid, which we write as
E_{\Psi}
.

Here
\mathbb{U}
is the “diagonal”
\mathcal{I}
-monoid as in Proposition
64
, sending
n\mapsto|\mathbb{U}(n,n)_{\bullet}|
.

Note that the left vertical arrow of (
3.9
) is an equivalence.

{rmk}

Let
\mathcal{V}
be the topologically enriched category of finite-dimensional real vector spaces equipped with inner products, and morphisms given by isometric embeddings.
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
embeds a subcategory, by sending
[n]
to
\mathbb{R}^{[n]}
.

Throughout this section we worked with diagrams of spaces indexed by
\mathcal{I}
rather than
\mathcal{V}
; one reason for this is to stay closer to the framework of
[
Sch04
]
, which will be helpful in Section
8
. In
[
PS24b
]
we indexed all constructions by
\mathcal{V}
. All the constructions given in this section work over
\mathcal{V}
without issue.

In particular, if
f,f^{\prime}:[n]\to[m]
are two injective maps and
n<m
, then the maps
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(n)\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(m)
induced by
f
and
f^{\prime}
are homotopic: using the above observation, it follows from the fact that the two maps
\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
induced by
f
and
f^{\prime}
are homotopic through isometric embeddings.

{rmk}

It follows from
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.24]
that
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}
, and hence
E_{\Psi}
, are convergent.

3.3
Categorical aspects of Bott periodicity

We define the category of
oriented polarised tangential structures
to be the category of simply connected commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids
F
over
\widetilde{U/O}^{or}
. Definition
57
determines a functor
F\mapsto\Psi^{F}
from polarised tangential structures to oriented commutative tangential pairs.

In practice, sometimes the bordism theory we would like to study is given to us (in the form of a commutative Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid, say) instead of the commutative tangential pair. In this subsection we prove the following proposition, which allows us to go back and forth between these two notions, at least in the polarised case. This is important for applications in
[
PS:qi
]
.

Proposition 67
.

The assignment
F\mapsto E_{\Psi^{F}}
defines a functor from oriented polarised tangential structures to connected oriented commutative Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoids, which induces an equivalence on homotopy categories.

The proof involves combining existing results in the literature.

We write
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}
for the category of commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids. For
X\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}
, we write
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}_{/X}
for the slice category of objects over
X
.

\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}
admits a model structure with weak equivalences those that induce isomorphisms on all homotopy groups
[
SS13
, Proposition 3.1]
.
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}
is Quillen equivalent to (and hence has an equivalent homotopy category to) the category of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras in spaces
[
SS12
, Theorem 1.2]
.

The slice category of objects over
X
inherits a model category structure too, with weak equivalences (respectively fibrations, cofibrations) those whose underlying map in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}
is a weak equivalence (respectively fibration, cofibration)
[
Hir03
, Theorem 7.6.5]
.

The functor
\Omega(\cdot):\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}
given by taking based loop spaces levelwise has a left adjoint
\mathbb{B}
, which together form a Quillen adjunction
[
SS13
, Corollary 4.9]
.
\Omega
shifts homotopy groups up by one on fibrant objects
[
SS13
, Lemma 2.4]
,
\mathbb{B}
shifts homotopy groups down by one on cofibrant objects
[
SS13
, Proposition 4.2]
. They therefore induce a Quillen equivalence (cf.
[
Hov95
, Definition 1.3.12]
), and hence an equivalence of homotopy categories, between connected commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids and all commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids.

{rmk}

By the above arguments, there is an equivalence
\Omega\mathbb{B}O\simeq O
of commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoids. By applying the
\Omega-B
adjunction levelwise there is also an equivalence
\Omega BO\simeq O
. It follows that there is an equivalence
BO\simeq\mathbb{B}O
.

Proof 3.2

(Proof of Proposition
67
)
.

We write
E
as a composition of several functors, and argue that they all induce equivalences on homotopy categories:

1.

Applying the functor
\Omega(\cdot)
.

2.

Taking homotopy pullback as in (
3.9
) (but with
\mathbb{U}^{or}
instead of
\mathbb{U}
).

3.

The above gives some object in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}_{/\mathbb{U}^{or}}
; we then push forwards along the classifying map for the index bundle
\mathbb{U}^{or}\to BSO
.

As observed above,
[
SS13
]
shows (1) induces an equivalence on homotopy categories. (2) is obtained by applying fibrant replacement, follows by base-change along an equivalence, and hence induces an equivalence of homotopy categories by
[
Rez02
, Proposition 2.7]
.
[
Rez02
, Proposition 2.7]
also implies (3) induces an equivalence on homotopy categories (note
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{I}}
is proper
[
SS12
, Proposition 3.5]
).

3.4
Abstract discs

In order to connect to previous work in
[
PS24b
]
, we will use some generalisations of the construction in Section
3.2
.

Definition 68
.

We define
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}
to be the simplicial set whose
k
-simplices consist of a smooth family of Riemann surfaces
D\to\Delta^{k}
, parametrised by the standard
k
-simplex. Each fibre is the complement of
i+j
boundary points in a nodal Riemann disc (one whose smoothing is biholomorphic to
D^{2}
), for some
4
4

4

We will mostly care about the cases
j\in\{0,1\}
.

i,j\geq 0
;
i
of the boundary punctures are ordered anticlockwise and labelled incoming, and the remaining
j
are ordered similarly and are labelled outgoing. Over each open stratum of
\Delta^{k}
the topological type of the fibres is constant. The fibres are allowed to develop more nodes along deeper boundary strata. Each simplex additionally includes (i) when
j=0
, a marked point
p_{x}
in the interior of each
D_{x}
with a distinguished direction in
T_{p_{x}}D_{x}
, varying continuously in
x
, and which is required to “point to the boundary of
D
” (i.e. not to a puncture: more precisely, the geodesic in that direction hits the boundary
\partial D
at a non-puncture); (ii) data of strip-like ends near each node and incoming / outgoing strip-like ends at the corresponding boundary punctures, as well as (iii) a metric on the Riemann surface which is standard over the strip-like ends. All this data satisfies some mild constraints, cf.
[
PS24b
, Sections 3.2-3.3]
.

Lemma 69
.

The resulting simplicial sets
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}
are contractible Kan complexes.

For each
1\leq l\leq i^{\prime}
, there is a map
\#_{l}:\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{i1}\times\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{i^{\prime}j}\to\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{i+i^{\prime}-1,j}
given by concatenating the output of a nodal Riemann disc to the
l^{th}
input of another. These maps are suitably associative.

Fix some commutative tangential pair
\Psi=(\Theta,\Phi)
. Let
F
be the homotopy fibre of the map
\Theta\to\Phi
;
F
maps naturally to
\widetilde{U/O}
.

Definition 70
.

A
\Psi
-oriented puncture datum
of
rank
N
consists of:

•

A path
\gamma
from
i\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in
U/O(N)
.

Let
\widetilde{\gamma}
be the unique lift of this path to the universal cover
\widetilde{U/O}(N)
sending
1
to the basepoint.

•

A homotopy lift of
\widetilde{\gamma}
to a path in
F(N)
.

By taking products with
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
(or
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
),
\Psi
-oriented puncture data determines a map from
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times[0,1]
(or
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\times[0,1]
) to
\widetilde{U/O}(N)
and a homotopy lift to
F
.

By abuse of notation we sometimes refer to this latter data as the puncture datum.

{rmk}

Any
\Psi
-oriented puncture datum
\mathbb{E}
of rank
N
determines a pair of points
p_{\mathbb{E}}^{\pm}\in\Theta(N)
, taken to be the given lifts of
i\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
respectively to
F(N)
, pushed forwards to land in
\Theta(N)
.

Any puncture data has an associated Maslov index
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}
(cf.
[
Sei08
, Section 11(h)]
).

{ex}

The standard puncture datum from Definition
58
gives an example of
(BO,BS_{\pm}U)
-oriented puncture datum of rank
1
and Maslov index 0.

{rmk}

We may take direct sum of
\Psi
-oriented puncture datum in a natural way: for
\mathbb{E}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}

\Psi
-oriented puncture data of ranks
n
and
n^{\prime}
respectively and a bijection
[n]\sqcup[n^{\prime}]\to[n^{\prime\prime}]
,
\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
is a
\Psi
-oriented puncture datum of rank
n^{\prime\prime}
.

For an injection
[n]\to[m]
, we may direct sum
\mathbb{E}
with
m-n
copies of the standard puncture datum to obtain a puncture datum of rank
m
.

Together, this endows the set of
\Psi
-oriented puncture data with the structure of a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoid (in sets).

The following definition generalises Definition
59
to allow the domain Riemann surface to vary within
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}
(in particular, it can have more than one puncture), and to incorporate other puncture data.

Definition 71
.

Let
\Psi
be a commutative tangential pair. Let
i,j\geq 0
. Let
\mathbb{E}=(\mathbb{E}^{in}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{E}^{in}_{i},\mathbb{E}^{out}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{E}^{out}_{j})
an ordered tuple of puncture data.

For each
v,n\in\mathcal{I}
, there is a Thom simplicial set
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}}^{\Psi}(v,n)=(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n))
, constructed as follows. A
k
-simplex in
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)_{k}
consists of:

•

A simplex
D\to\Delta^{k}
in
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}
.

•

A complex vector bundle
E
over
D
of rank
n
, along with a classifying map
E:D\to BU(n)
.

•

A real subbundle over the boundary
F\subseteq E|_{\partial D}
, along with a classifying map
F:\partial D\to BO(n)
, compatible with that of
E
(so
F\otimes\mathbb{C}\simeq E
over
\partial D
).

•

Compatible homotopy lifts of the classifying maps of
F
and
E
to
\Theta
and
\Phi
.

We require that over each strip-like end near each boundary puncture, the classifying maps and vector bundles agree with those determined by the puncture data
\mathbb{E}_{x}:=\mathbb{E}_{l}^{in\textrm{ or }out}
corresponding to each puncture
x
.

•

A smooth family of 1-forms
Y_{x}\in\Omega^{01}(D_{x},E_{x})
, for
x\in\Delta^{k}
.

We call all the data so far (
\Psi
-oriented)
Cauchy-Riemann data on
D
. For each fibre over
x\in\Delta^{k}
, using the Cauchy-Riemann data one can construct a Fredholm
5
5

5

More precisely: this is Fredholm after passing to appropriate Sobolev completions on both the domain and codomain, and assuming that the Lagrangian boundary conditions are transverse at all boundary punctures, cf.
[
PS24b
, Definition 3.10]
.
operator, called a
Cauchy-Riemann operator
:

D^{CR}_{x}=\overline{\partial}+Y_{x}:\Gamma(E|_{D_{x}},F|_{\partial D_{x}})\to\Omega^{01}(D_{x},E|_{D_{x}})

(3.10)

•

A family of linear maps (called
stabilisations
)
f_{x}:\mathbb{R}^{v}\to\Omega^{01}_{D_{x}}(E|_{D_{x}})
such that
f_{x}
is compactly supported and
D^{CR}_{x}+f_{x}
is surjective, for all
x
.

We impose mild assumptions on all this data, such as local constancy over the strip-like ends. We call these simplicial sets
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}}^{\Psi}(v,n)_{\bullet}

spaces of abstract discs
.

Forgetting all data except the domain
D
defines maps
\dom:\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)\to\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}
for all
v,n
.

{rmk}

We will sometimes suppress
\Psi
from the notation, to mean we work with the universal case
\Psi=(BO\to BS_{\pm}U)
.

Lemma 72
.

Each
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)_{\bullet}
is Kan.

Proof 3.3
.

This is a consequence of a standard linear gluing argument.

A similar argument shows:

Lemma 73
.

Let
W_{\bullet}
be the subsimplicial set of
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)_{\bullet}
consisting of abstract discs whose domain has no nodes. Then the inclusion
W_{\bullet}\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)_{\bullet}
is an equivalence.

Lemma 74

(
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.18]
)
.

There is a canonical (up to contractible choice) vector bundle
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)
over
|\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)_{\bullet}|
, called the
index bundle
.

Each
(\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}}(\cdot,n)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{E}}(\cdot,n))
then defines a Thom
\mathcal{I}
-simplicial set in a natural way.

{rmk}

The fibres of the corresponding vector bundle are given by the kernels of the stabilised Cauchy-Riemann operator. Note however that even though the set of fibres is canonical, the structure of a vector bundle is not automatic, this requires construction (and some mild choices).

The structure of a Thom
\mathcal{I}
-simplicial set is given by maps which roughly replace the stabilisations
f_{x}:\mathbb{R}^{v}\to\Omega^{01}(\ldots)
with
f_{x}\oplus 0:\mathbb{R}^{v}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{w-v}\to\Omega^{01}(\ldots)
.

Given two sets of Cauchy-Riemann data and stabilisation data
(E,F,Y,f)
and
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},Y^{\prime},f^{\prime})
on the same
k
-simplex
D\to\Delta^{k}
in
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}
, one can take their direct sum
(E\oplus E^{\prime},F\oplus F^{\prime},Y\oplus Y^{\prime},f\oplus f^{\prime})
fibrewise over
\Delta^{k}
, and their index bundles are naturally additive under this operation. This defines maps of Thom simplicial sets:

\oplus:\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)_{\bullet}\times_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}}\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(v^{\prime},n^{\prime})_{\bullet}\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(v+v^{\prime},n+n^{\prime})_{\bullet}

These are compatible with stabilisation in the
v
direction and so defines maps of Thom
\mathcal{I}
-simplicial sets

\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}}^{\Psi}(n)_{\bullet}\times_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}}\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})_{\bullet}\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(n+n^{\prime})_{\bullet}

This is commutative, and if we consider the same construction applied to iterated fibre products, it is associative.

Lemma 75

(
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.31]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{E}_{01}
be a tuple of
\Psi
-oriented puncture data with no inputs and 1 output.

There are weak equivalences of
\mathcal{I}
-spaces
\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(\cdot,n)\to\Omega U/O(n)
and, more generally,
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(\cdot,n)\to\Omega F
(viewing the codomain as constant
\mathcal{I}
-spaces in both cases).

Lemma 76
.

Let
\mathbb{E}_{01}
be as in Lemma
75
.

The inclusion

\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n)\times_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{01}}\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n^{\prime})\to\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n)\times\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n^{\prime})

is an equivalence of Thom
\mathcal{I}
-simplicial sets.

Proof 3.4
.

This follows from
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.31]
.

{rmk}

One can thus attempt to define a product

\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n)\times\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n^{\prime})\to\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n+n^{\prime})

via a zig-zag:

\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n)\times\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n^{\prime})\xleftarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n)\times_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{01}}\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\oplus}\mathbb{U}_{\mathbb{E}_{01}}(n+n^{\prime})

(3.11)

This is useful as a way to think about this operation, but we do not give it an axiomatic treatment here.

We may also concatenate puncture data as follows. Given sets of puncture data
\mathbb{E}=(\mathbb{E}^{in}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{E}^{in}_{i},\mathbb{E}^{out})
and
\mathbb{F}=(\mathbb{F}^{in}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{F}_{i^{\prime}}^{in},(\mathbb{F}^{out}))
(we use the brackets
(\mathbb{F}^{out})
do indicate that we omit
\mathbb{F}^{out}
if appropriate) and some
1\leq l\leq i^{\prime}
, we may form a new set of puncture data:

\mathbb{E}\#_{l}\mathbb{F}=\left(\mathbb{F}^{in}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{F}^{in}_{l-1},\mathbb{E}^{in}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{E}^{in}_{i},\mathbb{F}^{in}_{l+1},\ldots,\mathbb{F}^{in}_{i},(\mathbb{F}^{out})\right)

(3.12)

Given a pair of abstract discs
\mathbb{D}\in\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)
and
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{F}}(v^{\prime},n)
, and some
1\leq l\leq i^{\prime}
such that the puncture data at the output of
\mathbb{D}
agrees with that of the
l^{th}
input of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
, we may form a new abstract disc:

\mathbb{D}\#_{l}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}\#_{l}\mathbb{F}}(v+v^{\prime},n)

(3.13)

This can be upgraded to a map of simplicial sets, and can be made compatible with the index bundles:

Lemma 77

(
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.18]
)
.

Let
[v]\sqcup[v^{\prime}]\to[v^{\prime\prime}]
be an injection. We write
v^{\prime\prime}-v-v^{\prime}
for the complement of the image.

There are maps of Thom simplicial sets:

\#_{l}:\left(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n),\mathbb{V}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(v^{\prime},n),\mathbb{V}\right)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{v^{\prime\prime}-v-v^{\prime}}\to\left(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}\#_{l}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(v^{\prime\prime},n),\mathbb{V}\right)

(3.14)

These maps are suitably associative, and commute with stabilisation in the
v
-direction. Furthermore they are compatible with the concatenation maps
\#_{l}
on the
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}
under the map
\dom
.

{rmk}

Though it does not require any choices to define the set of fibres of the index bundles
\mathbb{V}
, to endow it with the structure of a vector bundle requires making some contractible choices. The proof of
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.18]
makes these choices via an inductive scheme over simplices, so that they can be made compatibly with concatenation.

By construction, the concatenation maps
\#_{l}
are also compatible with direct sum in the
n
-direction, as well as stabilisation in the
v
direction.

Lemma 78

(
[
PS24b
, Section 3.3]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{E}=(\mathbb{E}^{in}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{E}^{in}_{i},\mathbb{E}^{out}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{E}^{out}_{j})
be a choice of puncture data. Then:

\operatorname{Rank}(\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n))=v+\mu(\mathbb{E})+n(1-j)

(3.15)

where
\mu(\mathbb{E})
is the
total Maslov index

\sum_{l}\mu(\mathbb{E}^{in}_{l})-\sum_{k}\mu(\mathbb{E}^{out}_{k})
.

Let
\mathbb{E}^{std,fr}
be some choice of rank one framed puncture data of Maslov index 0, and
\mathbb{E}^{std,fr}(n)
its
n
-fold direct sum. Let
\mathbb{E}^{std,\Psi}(n)
be the induced
\Psi
-oriented puncture data (of any rank
n
). Let
\mathbb{E}^{std,\Psi}_{ij}(n)
be the tuple of puncture data with
i
inputs and
j
outputs, all of which are
\mathbb{E}^{std,\Psi}(n)
. We write
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{ij}(v,n)
for
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}^{std,\Psi}_{ij}(n)}(v,n)
.

{rmk}

Note that the space
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(n)
from Section
3.2
is a subspace of
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{01}(n,n)
: it is a fibre of
\dom
.

The maps
\dom
have coherent sections:

Lemma 79
.

There are sections
st=st_{ij}:\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{ij}(v,n)
of
\dom
.
These can be chosen to be compatible with concatenation
\#_{l}
.

When
j=0
, by (
3.15
) we have that
\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}_{ij}(0,n)=0
, so the maps
st
are automatically maps of Thom spaces.

Proof 3.5
.

We carry this out in the case
v=0
; by stabilising the general case then follows.

Because Cauchy-Riemann operators are not necessarily surjective,
\mathbb{U}^{fr}_{ij}(0,n)
is not necessarily contractible. However, in the case
n=1
,
[
MS12
, Theorem C.1.10]
implies that it is contractible. Hence we may choose sections
st:\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{ij}\to\mathbb{U}^{fr}_{ij}(0,1)
for all
i,j
, and the space of such choices is contractible. By making these choices over simplices inductively over the difference between the maximum and minimum number of nodes of the Riemann surface (similarly to the proof of
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.18]
), we may choose these to be compatible with concatenation, as required.

By taking iterated direct sums, we obtain the desired maps in the case
n>1
; by composing with the map of commutative tangential pairs
fr\to\Psi
, we obtain them for any
\Psi
.

Using this, when there are
j=0
outputs, there are maps of Thom simplicial sets for each injection
[n]\to[n^{\prime}]
:

\left(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)\to\left(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{std,\Psi}_{ij}(n^{\prime}-n)}(v,n^{\prime})_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)

(3.16)

On base spaces, this sends a simplex
\sigma\mapsto\sigma\oplus st\circ\dom(\sigma)
; there is a canonical isomorphism of vector bundles covering this coming from the fact that the index bundle over the codomain of
st_{\mathbb{E}^{std,\Psi}_{ij}(n)}
is
0
when
j=0
. These maps are compatible with all other structures constructed in this section thus far.

There is a similar story when there are
j=1
outputs. In this case, using a similar inductive argument, we can choose trivialisations
\mathbb{V}^{fr}_{i0}(0,1)\simeq\mathbb{R}
compatibly with concatenation, and hence obtain maps of Thom spaces
st:(\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}_{i0},\mathbb{R})\to(\mathbb{U}^{fr}_{i0}(0,1),\mathbb{V})
. Direct summing with these maps, similarly to above (but now with
j=1
), provides us maps of Thom simplicial sets for each injection
[n]\to[n^{\prime}]
:

\left(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}-n}\to\left(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{std,\Psi}_{ij}(n^{\prime}-n)}(v,n^{\prime})_{\bullet},\mathbb{V}\right)

(3.17)

{rmk}

All structures constructed in this section are functorial in
\Psi
.

3.5
Homotopy types of spaces of abstract discs

Let
\Psi=(\Theta\to\Phi)
be a commutative tangential pair.

Definition 80
.

Let
\mathbb{E}=(\mathbb{E}^{in}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{E}^{in}_{i},(\mathbb{E}^{out}))
be a tuple of puncture data with
i
inputs and
j\in\{0,1\}
outputs. Each puncture datum
\mathbb{F}\in\mathbb{E}
determines a pair of points
p_{\mathbb{F}}^{\pm}
in
\Theta(n)
, whose images in
\Phi(n)
agree, as in Remark
3.4
.

If
\mathbb{E}
is non-empty (i.e.
i+j\neq 0
), we define
\Omega_{\mathbb{E}}\Theta(n)
to be the space of
(i+j)
paths in
\Theta(n)
with endpoints specified as follows; cf. Figure
2
. These are paths
p^{-}_{\mathbb{E}^{in}_{l}}
to
p^{+}_{\mathbb{E}^{in}_{l+1}}
for
0\leq l\leq i-1
; then if
j=0
, a path
p^{-}_{\mathbb{E}^{in}_{i}}
to
p^{+}_{\mathbb{E}^{in}_{0}}
, and if
j=1
two paths: one from
p^{-}_{\mathbb{E}^{in}_{i}}
to
p^{-}_{\mathbb{E}^{out}}
and one from
p^{+}_{\mathbb{E}^{out}}
to
p^{+}_{\mathbb{E}^{in}_{1}}
.

p^{+}_{\mathbb{E}^{out}}
p^{-}_{\mathbb{E}^{out}}
p^{+}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}^{in}}
p^{-}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}^{in}}
p^{+}_{\mathbb{E}_{2}^{in}}
p^{+}_{\mathbb{E}_{i}^{in}}
p^{-}_{\mathbb{E}_{i}^{in}}

Figure 2
:
Schematic picture of an element of
\Omega_{\mathbb{E}}\Theta(n)

\Omega_{\mathbb{E}}\Theta(n)
admits a natural map to the free loop space
\mathcal{L}\Phi(n)
, by composing all the paths with the map
\Theta(n)\to\Phi(n)
, and then concatenating them. We define
\Psi_{\mathbb{E}}(n)
to be the space of pairs
(\gamma,d)
, where
\gamma\in\Omega_{\mathbb{E}}(\Theta(n))
and
d:D^{2}\to\Phi(n)
is a filling of the corresponding loop in
\mathcal{L}\Phi(n)
.

If
\mathbb{E}
is empty, we define:
\Psi_{00}(n)=\Psi_{\emptyset}(n)
to be the homotopy pullback of:

\textstyle{\Phi(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}\Theta(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}\Phi(n)}

(3.18)

where the vertical arrow is the inclusion of constant loops.

{rmk}

For
\mathbb{E}
non-empty,
\Omega_{\mathbb{E}}\Theta(n)
is non-canonically homotopy equivalent to
(\Omega\Theta(n))^{i+j}
. Additionally choosing a distinguished puncture determines an equivalence:

\Psi_{\mathbb{E}}(n)\to\mathrm{hofib}(\Omega_{\mathbb{E}}\Theta(n)\to\Omega\Phi(n))

(3.19)

Note that the homotopy types of these spaces only depends on
i
and
j
.

Concatenation defines natural maps:

\#_{l}:\Psi_{\mathbb{E}}(n)\times\Psi_{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(n)\to\Psi_{\mathbb{E}\#_{l}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(n)

(3.20)

{rmk}

Thus far, none of the constructions in Section
3.5
have used the monoidal structure on
\Theta
or
\Phi
.

For a bijection
[n]\sqcup[n^{\prime}]\to[n+n^{\prime}]
, the monoid structures on
\Theta
and
\Phi
determine maps:

\oplus:\Psi_{\mathbb{E}}(n)\times\Psi_{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\to\Psi_{\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}(n+n^{\prime})

(3.21)

when
\mathbb{E}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
have the same numbers of inputs and outputs.

{rmk}

These constructions simplify in the case
\Phi=\operatorname{pt}
, and
\Theta
comes from some
F\to\widetilde{U/O}
as in Example
3.1
. In this case, if
\mathbb{E}
is non-empty,
\Psi_{\mathbb{E}}(n)
is equivalent to
(\Omega F(n))^{i+j}
,
\#_{l}
concatenates loops in an appropriate way, and
\Psi_{\emptyset}(n)\simeq\mathcal{L}F(n)
.

Definition 81
.

There are maps of spaces:

ev=ev^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n):\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(v,n)\to\Psi_{\mathbb{E}}(n)

(3.22)

defined by sending an abstract disc to the given homotopy lifts of its classifying maps (cf. Definition
71
) to
\Theta
and
\Phi
.

The maps
ev
are compatible with all the relevant structures (direct sum, stabilisation in the
v
direction, and concatenation).

Generalising Lemma
75
, we have:

Lemma 82

(
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.31]
)
.

The connectivity of the map
ev^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{V}}(v,n)
goes to
\infty
as
v\to\infty
.

In particular,
ev^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{V}}(\cdot,n)
defines an equivalence of
\mathcal{I}
-spaces from
\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}_{\mathbb{E}}(\cdot,n)
to
\Psi_{\mathbb{E}}(n)
, viewing the codomain as a constant
\mathcal{I}
-space.

{rmk}

The theory still makes sense without the final piece of data in Definition
71
, but is useful for our purposes so that the resulting homotopy type is “correct”: for example, without it, if
\Phi\simeq\operatorname{pt}
, we would have a highly-connected (as
v\to\infty
map to the homotopy orbits
\mathbb{U}_{00}^{\Theta,\operatorname{pt}}(v,n)\to(\mathcal{L}\Theta(n))_{hS^{1}}
, instead of
\mathcal{L}\Theta(n)
.

4
Flow categories over an
\mathcal{I}
-monoid

Let
E
denote a Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid (i.e. a monoidal object in Thom
\mathcal{I}
-spaces), which is furthermore commutative.

4.1
Unoriented flow categories

An unoriented flow category
\mathcal{F}
comprises

1.

a finite set of objects
x\in\mathcal{F}
;

2.

for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
a smooth manifold with corners
\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}
;

3.

embeddings
\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{F}_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}\to\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime\prime}}
whose images cover and enumerate the codimension one boundary strata.

We will always assume that we have a degree function

|\cdot|:\mathrm{Ob}\,\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{Z}

such that
\mathcal{F}_{xy}
has dimension
|x|-|y|-1
.

Let
M^{n}
be a manifold with corners, and
p
a point in
M
. Then there is a chart near
p

\phi:U\subseteq M\rightarrow V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-i}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{i}
sending
p
to
0
, where
i=\Gamma(p)
is the codimension of
p
in
M
.

Definition 83
.

Let
M
be a manifold with corners. Then
M
is a manifold with faces if and only if for each point
p
in
M
,
p
lies in the closure of exactly
\Gamma(p)
boundary faces.

We write
D(M)
for the set of codimension 1 connected faces of
M
, and
D({F\subseteq M})
for the set of codimension 1 (connected) faces of
M
which touch
F
.

A
system of collars

\mathcal{C}
on
M
consists of collar neighbourhoods

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{FG}:F\times[0,\varepsilon)^{D({F\subseteq G})}\hookrightarrow G

meaning an embedding onto a neighbourhood of
F\subseteq G
, whenever
F
and
G
are faces of
M
such that
F\subseteq G
. We require that the collars restrict to each other on subfaces, and come with maps

\mathcal{C}:F\times[0,\varepsilon)\hookrightarrow M

for all boundary faces
F
such that for all distinct pairs of boundary faces
F_{i},F_{j}
, the following diagram commutes,

\textstyle{F_{i}\cap F_{j}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}^{F_{i}}\times\id_{[0,\varepsilon)}}
\scriptstyle{\left(\mathcal{C}^{F_{j}}\times\id_{[0,\varepsilon)}\right)\circ\tau}
\textstyle{F_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\textstyle{F_{j}\times[0,\varepsilon)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\textstyle{M}

(4.1)

where
\tau
swaps the two factors in
[0,\varepsilon)^{2}
.

A
system of collars
on a flow category
\mathcal{F}
consists of a system of collars
\mathcal{C}_{xy}
on all
\mathcal{F}_{xy}
, restricting to the product system of collars on
\mathcal{F}_{xz}\times\mathcal{F}_{zy}
induced from
\mathcal{C}_{xz}
and
\mathcal{C}_{zy}
. Systems of collars always exist, are unique up to isotopy, and can be chosen to extend (compatibly) given collars on subsets of boundary faces.

The abstract index bundle
I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xy}\to\mathcal{F}_{xy}
is
T\mathcal{F}_{xy}\oplus\mathbb{R}
, with the final factor generated by an element
\tau_{y}
.

We now fix ‘stabilisation data’ for the flow-category
\mathcal{F}
, comprising non-negative integers
\nu_{xx^{\prime}}
, for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
. Each such defines a finite-dimensional inner product space
\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime}}}
. We insist there are injections

[\nu_{xx^{\prime}}]\sqcup[\nu_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}]\to[\nu_{xx^{\prime\prime}}]

(4.2)

defining isometric embeddings

\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime}}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime\prime}}}

and we insist that the injections (
4.2
) are associative for quadruples, meaning in particular that

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime}}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{x^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime\prime\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime}}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime\prime}}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{x^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime\prime\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime\prime\prime}}}}

commutes.

Definition 84
.

The Thom
\mathcal{I}
-space
(\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}},I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}})
has

(\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}},I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}})(n):=I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}

{rmk}

To simplify notation, we will often write
(\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}},I^{\mathcal{F}})
in place of
(\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}},I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}})
, or write
I^{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot)
to indicate that we are considering the Thom
\mathcal{I}
-space rather than the index bundle.

In the setting of Floer theory, and a tangential pair
\Psi
with associated Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
E_{\Psi}=(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi},\mathbb{V}^{\Psi})
, the vector bundle
\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(\nu)\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(\nu)
has rank
\nu
given by the dimension of the stabilisation vector space. On the other hand, the vector bundle arising in Definition
84
has rank determined by both the
\mathcal{I}
-index
\nu
and the rank
|x|-|x^{\prime}|
of the index bundle
I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}}
.

This leads us to introduce a variation of the notion of ‘stabilisation data’.

Definition 85
.

Index data for a flow category
\mathcal{F}
comprises choices of finite sets
d_{xx^{\prime}}
of cardinality
|x|-|x^{\prime}|
, for each
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
, such that there are (not necessarily order-preserving) injections

d_{xx^{\prime}}\sqcup d_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}\to d_{xx^{\prime\prime}}

which satisfy that for objects
x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}
the diagram

\textstyle{d_{xx^{\prime}}\sqcup d_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}\sqcup d_{x^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d_{xx^{\prime}}\sqcup d_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d_{xx^{\prime\prime}}\sqcup d_{x^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d_{xx^{\prime\prime\prime}}}

commutes.

{rmk}

There are analogues of stabilisation and index data for morphisms of flow categories, bordisms of morphisms, bilinear maps, associators etc. When considering index data for a bilinear map, one encounters a diagram of the form

\textstyle{d_{xx^{\prime}}\sqcup d_{yy^{\prime}}\sqcup d_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime};z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d_{xx^{\prime}}\sqcup d_{x^{\prime}y;z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d_{yy^{\prime}}\sqcup d_{xy^{\prime};z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d_{xy;z}}

which must commute. At this point it is essential that the various
d_{xy;z},d_{xx^{\prime}}
etc are finite sets which are not canonically ordered, and the morphisms of finite sets in the definition of index data are injections which need not be order-preserving (as for morphisms in sets of
\mathcal{I}
). Geometrically this reflects the fact that the two breakings at the inputs of a bilinear map have no preferred order.

{rmk}

There is no natural
E_{\infty}
or indeed
E_{2}
map
\mathbb{Z}\to BO\times\mathbb{Z}
(e.g. one splitting projection), but there is a natural map
\Omega^{\infty}(\mathbb{S})\to BO\times\mathbb{Z}
, which gives a homotopical explanation for the appearance of (unordered) finite sets in the construction of index data.

The following is perhaps not obvious in light of Remark
4.1
; a more detailed argument, in the Floer-theoretic settings where we require the result, follows from the same argument that proves Lemma
126
.

Lemma 86
.

Given a finite collection of flow categories, morphisms, bilinear maps, associators, and bordisms of such, index data exists.

Proof 4.1

(Sketch)
.

For a fixed flow category
\scrF
, this is proved inductively in the dimension of
\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}
. More generally one considers the finite set of all moduli spaces arising in constructing the required structure, and works inductively in their dimension.

4.2
Orientation relative to an
\mathcal{I}
-monoid

Fix a monoid
E
in Thom
\mathcal{I}
-spaces. Thus we have base spaces
\Base(E(n))
and bundles
E(n)\to\Base(E(n))
, with suitably compatible addition maps.

To define an
E
-orientation on a flow category
\mathcal{F}
, we will use two auxiliary pieces of data:

1.

stabilisation data
\{\nu_{xx^{\prime}}\}
for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
;

2.

index data

\{d_{xx^{\prime}}\}
for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
,

as defined in the previous section. For a finite set
d
we write
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
\mathbb{R}
-
\mathrm{Span}\langle d\rangle
.

Definition 87
.

An
E
-oriented flow category comprises a flow category
\mathcal{F}
with stabilisation data
\{\nu_{xx^{\prime}}\}
and index data
\{d_{xx^{\prime}}\}
, together with maps of Thom spaces

\rho:(\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}},I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}})(\nu_{xx^{\prime}})\to E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime}})

(4.3)

for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
, which are compatible with and associative under composition. Note that
\rho
incorporates a map of base spaces
\rho_{xx^{\prime}}:\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}\to\Base(E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime}}))
and a covering isomorphism of bundles
\mathrm{st}_{xx^{\prime}}:I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime}}}\to E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime}})
.

{rmk}

We do not formulate this in terms of maps of (i.e. functors of categories enriched in) Thom
\mathcal{I}
-spaces, because a map
I^{\mathcal{F}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n}\to E(n)
which arises as part of such a functor necessarily lands in the
\mathrm{Sym}_{n}
-fixed points of
\Base(E(n))
. This imposes an unwanted homotopical constraint.

To clarify the compatibility requirements in Definition
87
, we have that the
\rho_{xx^{\prime}}
fit into commuting diagrams

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{F}_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{xx^{\prime}}\times\rho_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}}
\textstyle{\Base E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime}})\times\Base E(\nu_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}+d_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{xx^{\prime\prime}}}
\textstyle{\Base E(\nu_{xx^{\prime\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime\prime}})}

(where the vertical map is the monoidal composition on
\Base(E)
).

The covering isomorphisms satisfy

\textstyle{I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}}+\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime}}}+I^{\mathcal{F}}_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}+\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime}})+E(\nu_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}+d_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime\prime}}+\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(\nu_{xx^{\prime\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime\prime}})}

{rmk}

The commutation rules for associativity of these injections for quadruples of objects will involve moving
\nu
’s past one another and
d
’s past one another, but never interchanging those; the structure is compatible with a partial order on sets in
\mathcal{I}
in which
\nu\leq d
for all
\nu,d
. This follows immediately from the fact that the Thom spaces on the RHS of the diagram arise as postcompositions with natural maps
E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}})\oplus\mathbb{R}^{d_{xx^{\prime}}}\to E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime}})
.

Definition 88
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be flow categories over
E
, and let
\mathcal{W}
be a morphism from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{G}
. Assume that all of
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{W}
have been equipped with stabilisation data and index data.

Then
\mathcal{W}
is
E
-oriented if for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
and
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
we have
maps of Thom spaces

\rho:(\mathcal{W}_{xy},I^{\mathcal{W}}_{xy})(\nu_{xy})\to E[1](\nu_{xy}+d_{xy})

(4.4)

which fit into diagrams

\textstyle{(\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}},I^{\mathcal{F}})(\nu_{xx^{\prime}})\times(\mathcal{W}_{x^{\prime}y},I^{\mathcal{W}})(\nu_{x^{\prime}y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime}})\times E[1](\nu_{x^{\prime}y}+d_{x^{\prime}y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{W}_{xy},I^{\mathcal{W}})(\nu_{xy})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(1+\nu_{xy}+d_{xy})}

(and similarly for breaking in
\mathcal{G}
on the right). These diagrams should furthermore be associative under further breaking.

{rmk}

The shifts
[1]
account for the fact that, when
E=E_{\Psi}
, if
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
and
y\in\mathcal{G}
then

I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}}\oplus V_{xx^{\prime}}=\rho_{xx^{\prime}}^{*}\mathbb{V}_{xx^{\prime}}

whilst

I^{\mathcal{W}}_{xy}\oplus V_{xy}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\rho_{xy}^{*}\mathbb{V}_{xy}.

Here, in the notation from
[
PS24b
, Lemma 3.18]
,
V_{xx^{\prime}}=\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xx^{\prime}}}
is the stabilisation space,
\rho
denotes the domain map to the space of abstract discs and
\mathbb{V}_{xx^{\prime}},\mathbb{V}_{xy}
are the corresponding index bundles over the codomain of
\rho
.

A bordism
\mathcal{R}
between morphisms
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
, all equipped with index and stabilisation data, has maps of Thom spaces

(\mathcal{R}_{xy},I^{\mathcal{R}}_{xy})(\nu_{xy})\to E[2](\nu_{xy}+d_{xy})

which satisfy an analogous set of compatibilities under breaking. For instance, since
\mathcal{W}_{xy}\sqcup\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{xy}
occur as codimension one unbroken faces in
\mathcal{R}_{xy}
, there are isomorphisms of Thom spaces

I^{\mathcal{R}}_{xy}(\cdot)|_{\mathcal{W}_{xy}}=I^{\mathcal{W}}_{xy}(1+\cdot)

and similarly for
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
.
An
E
-oriented bordism has maps (of bundles over the boundary stratum
\mathcal{W}_{xy}\sqcup\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{xy}
)

\textstyle{(\mathcal{W}_{xy},I^{\mathcal{W}}_{xy})(1+\nu_{xy})\sqcup(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{xy},I^{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}_{xy})(\nu_{xy})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E[1](1+\nu_{xy}+d_{xy})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{R}_{xy},I^{\mathcal{R}}_{xy})(\nu_{xy})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E[2](\nu_{xy}+d_{xy})}

and similarly has maps

\textstyle{(\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}},I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}})(\nu_{xx^{\prime}})\times(\mathcal{R}_{x^{\prime}y},I^{\mathcal{R}}_{x^{\prime}y})(\nu_{x^{\prime}y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime}})\times E[2](\nu_{x^{\prime}y}+d_{x^{\prime}y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{R}_{xy},I^{\mathcal{R}}_{xy})(\nu_{xy})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E[2](\nu_{xy}+d_{xy})}

(and similarly for breaking in
\mathcal{G}
on the right).

Lemma 89
.

There is a category
\Flow^{E}
of flow categories living over
E
, with morphisms being bordism classes of
E
-oriented morphisms.

Proof 4.2
.

Identical to
[
PS24b
, Section 5.2]
subject to appropriate changes in notation.

{ex}

Consider two
E
-orientations
\iota,\tilde{\iota}
on
\mathcal{F}
, given by maps
\iota_{xx^{\prime}}:\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}\to\Base(E)
and
\tilde{\iota}_{xx^{\prime}}:\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}\to\Base(E)
covered by appropriate bundle data. We say these are
concordant
if there are homotopies between
\iota_{xx^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{\iota}_{xx^{\prime}}
which are compatible with breaking, and lifts of the homotopies to
E
. The corresponding lifts of
\mathcal{F}
to objects of
\Flow^{E}
are then equivalent. (One can similarly define concordance of all the other data discussed above.)

Definition 90
.

The
shift

\mathcal{F}[i]
of a flow category has the same objects and morphism spaces, but degree function
|x|_{\mathcal{F}[i]}=|x|_{\mathcal{F}}+i
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is
E
-oriented, then we define an
E
-orientation on
\mathcal{F}[i]
to be given by the same maps
(\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}},I^{\mathcal{F}}_{xx^{\prime}})(\nu_{xx^{\prime}})\to E(\nu_{xx^{\prime}}+d_{xx^{\prime}})
.

As in
[
PS24a
,
PS24b
]
there are truncations
\tau_{\leq i}\mathcal{F}
of an
E
-oriented flow category, in which one prescribes all manifolds of dimension
\leq i
and asserts the existence of manifolds of dimension
i+1
, satisfying the correct breaking. Equipping all such with
E
-orientations yields a category
\tau_{\leq i}\Flow^{E}
, where
\Flow^{E}=\tau_{\leq\infty}\Flow^{E}
. There are truncation functors
\tau_{\leq i}\Flow^{E}\to\tau_{\leq j}\Flow^{E}
whenever
j\leq i\leq\infty
, which are furthermore associative.

4.3
Left and right modules

An unoriented right module
\mathcal{N}
of degree
i
over an unoriented flow category
\mathcal{F}
comprises moduli spaces
\mathcal{N}_{x}
for
x\in\mathcal{F}
, of dimension
i-|x|
, with embeddings of boundary faces
\mathcal{N}_{x^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{F}_{x^{\prime}x}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{x}
.

Stabilisation and index data now comprises positive integers
\nu_{*x}
, and finite sets
d_{*x}
of cardinality
i-|x|
, satisfying the obvious compatibilities. An
E
-orientation of
\mathcal{N}
involves giving maps of Thom spaces

I^{\mathcal{N}}_{\ast x}(\nu_{*x})\to E[1](\nu_{*x}+d_{*x})

compatible under breaking. Bordism of left modules is defined similarly, and there is a group
\Omega_{*}^{E}(\mathcal{F})
of bordism classes of left modules.

{rmk}

An
E
-oriented right module of degree
i
is exactly a morphism
\ast[i]\to\mathcal{F}
of
E
-oriented flow categories. The bordism group
\Omega_{i}^{E}(\mathcal{F})
is the morphism group
[\ast[i],\mathcal{F}]
in the category
\Flow^{E}
.

A degree
i
left module over
\mathcal{F}
is a morphism
\mathcal{F}\to\ast[-i]
, and the bordism group
\Omega_{E}^{i}(\mathcal{F})
of
E
-oriented such modules is the morphism group
[\mathcal{F},\ast[-i]]
in
\Flow^{E}
.

Recall that a bilinear map
\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
of flow categories comprises manifolds with faces
\mathcal{B}_{xy,z}
for
x\in\mathcal{F},y\in\mathcal{G},z\in\mathcal{H}
, of dimension
|x|+|y|-|z|
, with codimension one boundary faces covered by embeddings

\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{B}_{x^{\prime}y,z},\ \mathcal{G}_{yy^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{B}_{xy^{\prime},z},\ \mathrm{and}\ \mathcal{B}_{xy,z^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{H}_{z^{\prime}z}.

In the presence of stabilisation and index data, an
E
-orientation of
\mathcal{R}
comprises maps

(\mathcal{B}_{xy;z},I^{\mathcal{B}}_{xy;z})(\nu_{xy;z})\to E[1](\nu_{xy;z}+d_{xy;z})

which are compatible with breaking and associative for compatible breaking (cf. Remark
4.1
in this case).

If
\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is an
E
-oriented bilinear map of flow categories there is an induced map

\mathcal{B}_{*}:\Omega_{i}^{E}(\mathcal{F})\times\Omega_{j}^{E}(\mathcal{G})\to\Omega_{i+j}^{E}(\mathcal{H}).

(4.5)

{rmk}

For
x\in\mathcal{F},y\in\mathcal{G},z\in\mathcal{H}
the association
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{yx;z}:=\mathcal{B}_{xy;z}
shows that bilinear maps
\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
naturally biject with bilinear maps
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{H}
, compatibly with
E
-orientations.

4.4
Bordism groups

Let
E
be a commutative Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid. Then there is a Thom commutative ring spectrum
\Thom(E)
(cf. Remark
2.9
) spectrum which has an associated bordism theory,
\Omega^{E}_{*}
. Concretely, an element of
\Omega_{i}^{E}
is realised by a closed
i
-dimensional manifold
M
and a map of Thom spaces
(M,TM)(k)\to E(n_{i,k})
, where
M\to\Base(E)
lands in a component where the rank of the bundle
E(n_{i,k})
is
i+k
. One can always further stabilise to increase
k
and accordingly
n_{i,k}
. Two such elements
(M,TM)(k)\to E(n_{i,k})
and
(N,TN)(k^{\prime})\to E(n_{i,k^{\prime}})
are bordant if there is some
\kappa
with
k\sqcup k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\kappa
and a commutative diagram

\textstyle{(M,TM)(\kappa)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(n_{i,\kappa})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(W,TW)(\kappa)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E[1](n_{i,\kappa})}
\textstyle{(N,TN)(\kappa)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(n_{i,\kappa})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where
W
is a bordism between
M
and
N
and the diagram represents the required
E
-orientation data. We write
\Omega_{*}^{E}
for the resulting groups.

The definition adapts directly to the case of maps
M\to X
to a target
X
which need not be a point. More generally, assume
X
is equipped with a vector bundle
V\to X
of rank
r
. We may define
\Omega^{E}_{i}(X,V)
to be the group of closed
i
-manifolds
M
equipped with a map of Thom spaces
(M,TM)(k+r)\to(X,V)\otimes E(n_{i,k})
, considered up to stabilisation and bordism as above.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a(n unoriented) flow category. A left module
\mathcal{N}

lives over
X
if there are maps
\mathcal{N}_{x*}\to X
which are compatible with the module action of
\mathcal{F}
.

Given a commutative Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid
E
and an
E
-orientation on
\mathcal{F}
, a left module
\mathcal{N}
over
\mathcal{F}
living over
X
is
E
-oriented relative to
V
if it is equipped with maps of Thom spaces
(\mathcal{N}_{*x},I^{\mathcal{N}}_{*x})(\nu_{x*}+r)\to(X,V)\otimes E[1](\nu_{x*}+d_{x*})
, which are compatible with breaking. The underlying maps of spaces
\mathcal{N}_{*x}\to X
are required to agree with the given ones.

We may define bordisms of modules, as well as bilinear maps
\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}\to*[i]
, over
X
, and
E
-orientations relative to
V
, similarly.

Our eventual theory of interest,
\Omega^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}(X,\phi)
, is a variant on this which is only defined for commutative Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoids arising from tangential pairs, cf. Section
5.2
.

4.5
Unitality

We discuss units, following
[
AB24
, Section 6]
. Their key idea is to introduce a novel stratification of
B\times[0,1]
for a manifold with corners
B
.

{rmk}

We now assume that the set of boundary strata of a manifold with corners admits a partial order which is a total order on the facets (i.e. codimension one faces) adjacent to any corner. This will hold for all the manifolds with corners arising as morphism spaces in Floer (or Morse) flow categories, cf. Theorems
5.1
and
107
for a comprehensive list.

For a
k
-dimensional manifold with corners
X
, with the further data from Remark
4.5
,
[
AB24
, Lemma 6.3 & Definition 6.4]
construct a
(k+1)
-dimensional manifold with corners
\mathbb{D}(X)
, the ‘conic degeneration of
X
’. If in a local chart
X
is modelled on
[0,\infty)^{k}
, then
\mathbb{D}X
maps to
[0,\infty)^{k}
with fibres a union of
i+1
-intervals over the locus where
i
co-ordinates vanish.

Lemma 91
.

For a flow category
\mathcal{F}
, there is a morphism
\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}
with spaces

(\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F})_{xy}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{F}_{xy})&x\neq y\\
\{pt\}&x=y\end{cases}

Proof 4.3
.

The boundary facets of the conic degeneration are enumerated as

\partial\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F}_{xy}=\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{F}_{x^{\prime}y}\cup\mathcal{F}_{xy^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F}_{y^{\prime}y}.

See
[
AB24
, Lemma 6.7]
.

Lemma 92
.

\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F}
defines the unit for
\mathcal{F}
in a unital structure on the category
\Flow
.

Proof 4.4
.

It follows from
[
AB24
, Lemma 6.10]
(and the fact that the diagonal bimodule gives a system of degeneracies for their semisimplicial category) that for any morphism
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
, there is a bordism
\mathcal{W}\simeq(\mathcal{W}\circ\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F})
.

Write
p:\mathcal{D}_{n}\to[0,\infty)^{n}
for the local conic degeneration of the basic manifold with corners.
[
AB24
, Lemma 6.11]
construct compatible isomorphisms

T\mathcal{D}_{n+1}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}\to T[0,\infty)^{n}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathbb{R}

with the properties that (i) on the interior, the first factor of the isomorphism is that induced by the projection
p
up to a positive diagonal rescaling, and (ii) the map
\underline{\mathbb{R}}\to\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathbb{R}
sends the generator
1
to a point in the positive quadrant. Such isomorphisms are compatible with the inductive construction of the conic degenerations, so for any manifold with corners
B
one gets analogous isomorphisms in the setting of the map
p:\mathbb{D}B\to B
assembled from the local conic degenerations over strata.

Lemma 93
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is an
E
-oriented flow category, then the unit morphism
\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F}
is naturally an
E
-oriented morphism.

Proof 4.5
.

Suppose first
x\neq y
are distinct objects of
\mathcal{F}
. The maps
\mathcal{F}_{xy}\to E
induce corresponding maps on
(\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F})_{xy}
.
The
E
-orientation on
\mathcal{F}
gives isomorphisms

I_{xy}^{\mathcal{F}}(\nu_{xy})\to E(\nu_{xy}+d_{xy})

for stabilisation spaces
\mathbb{R}^{\nu_{xy}}
and index data
d_{xy}
,
compatible with breaking. The previous comments show that there are also coherent isomorphisms

T\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F}_{xy}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{y}\to p^{*}T\mathcal{F}_{xy}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{y}\oplus\mathbb{R}

Combining these gives rise to isomorphisms

I_{xy}^{\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F}}(\nu_{xy})\to E[1](\nu_{xy}+d_{xy})

where the map from
\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F}
to
\Base(E)
is induced from the corresponding classifying map for
\mathcal{F}
and the forgetful map
p
.

Finally, for any
x
we map
(\mathbb{D}\mathcal{F})_{xx}
to the basepoint in the (base of the) monoid
E
.

Lemma 94
.

The homotopy category
\Flow^{E}
is unital.

Proof 4.6
.

Combine the arguments in the proofs of Lemmas
92
and
93
.

In
[
PS24b
]
we constructed a category
\Flow^{\Psi}
of
\Psi
-oriented flow categories, for any graded tangential structure

\textstyle{\Theta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BS_{\pm}U}

(4.6)

Conjecture 95
.

Suppose
\Psi
is commutative and (
4.6
) is a diagram of infinite loop spaces, so there is a commutative monoid
E_{\Psi}
in Thom
\mathcal{I}
-spaces. Then there is a unital functor
\Flow^{\Psi}\to\Flow^{E_{\Psi}}
.

{rmk}

This is not immediate from the constructions, essentially because concatenation in
\Flow^{\Psi}
relies on the product on
\Omega U/O
coming from concatenation of loops, whereas concatenation in
\Flow^{E_{\Psi}}
uses the infinite loop space addition on
U/O
. It seems likely that one could adapt the construction of ‘twisting data’ and arguments from Section
5.4
below to prove Conjecture
95
.

In particular, the functor of Conjecture
95
should not be an equivalence. Let
R
be the commutative ring spectrum
\Thom(E_{\Psi})
. When
\Phi
is contractible, we expect
\Flow^{\Psi}
to be equivalent to the category of
R
-
R
bimodules
[
PS24b
, Conjecture 5.10]
, and
\Flow^{E_{\Psi}}
to be equivalent to the category of
R
-modules.

4.6
Cones

Definition 96
.

Let
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of unoriented flow categories. There is an unoriented flow category
\Cone(\mathcal{W})
with objects
\mathcal{A}[1]\sqcup\mathcal{B}
, with morphisms given by

\Cone(\mathcal{W})_{xy}:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{A}_{xy}&\,\mathrm{if}\,x,y\in\mathcal{A}\\
\mathcal{B}_{xy}&\,\mathrm{if}\,x,y\in\mathcal{B}\\
\mathcal{W}_{xy}&\,\mathrm{if}\,x\in\mathcal{A},\,y\in\mathcal{B}\\
\emptyset&\,\mathrm{if}\,x\in\mathcal{B},\,y\in\mathcal{A}\end{cases}

Then
\Cone(\mathcal{W})
contains
\mathcal{A}[1]
and
\mathcal{B}
as full subcategories.

If
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{W}
are
E
-oriented then the shift
\mathcal{A}[1]
and the mapping cone
\Cone(\mathcal{W})
each inherit an
E
-orientation, defined using the given stabilisation and index data and the associated maps (schematically denoted)
(\mathcal{A},I^{\mathcal{A}})\to E
,
(\mathcal{B},I^{\mathcal{B}})\to E
and
(\mathcal{W},I^{\mathcal{W}})\to E[1]
.

Proposition 97
.

Let
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be
E
-oriented morphisms of
E
-oriented flow categories.

1.

If
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
are bordant (via a
E
-oriented bordism
\mathcal{R}
), then
\Cone(\mathcal{W})\simeq\Cone(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})\in\Flow^{E}
.

2.

If
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
are isomorphisms, then
\Cone(\mathcal{W})\simeq\Cone(\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}\circ\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{V}^{\prime})
.

Proof 4.7
.

For the first part, we first write down a morphism
\mathcal{Q}
from
\Cone(\mathcal{W})
to
\Cone(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
. Precisely, we use letters
a,b
for elements of
\Cone(\mathcal{W})=\mathcal{A}[1]\sqcup\mathcal{B}
and letters
\alpha,\beta
for elements of
\Cone(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}[1]\sqcup\mathcal{B}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{a\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}\mathcal{A}_{a\alpha}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{a\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{a\beta}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{b\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}\mathcal{B}_{b\beta}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{b\alpha}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

Note that the boundary strata
\mathcal{W}_{a\beta}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{a\beta}
in
\partial\mathcal{R}_{a\beta}
are then accounted for in the boundary of
\mathcal{Q}
by the factors arising from the point strata
(\mathbb{D}\mathcal{A})_{aa}
and
(\mathbb{D}\mathcal{B})_{\beta\beta}
. The
E
-orientation on
\mathcal{Q}
is inherited from those on
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{R}
using that the diagonal bimodules are canonically then
E
-oriented.

\mathcal{Q}
also defines a morphism
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
from
\Cone(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
to
\Cone(\mathcal{W})
. The composite
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}\circ\mathcal{Q}
is an endomorphism of
\Cone(\mathcal{W})
. The underlying spaces of this are given by appropriate partial gluings

(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}\circ\mathcal{Q})_{aa^{\prime}}=\cup_{\alpha}\,\mathbb{D}\mathcal{A}_{a\alpha}\times\mathbb{D}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha a}

which is bordant to
\mathbb{D}\mathcal{A}_{aa^{\prime}}
by the idempotent property of the unit morphism for
\mathcal{A}
. The morphism spaces for pairs
(b,b^{\prime})\in\Ob\Cone(\mathcal{W})\times\Ob\Cone(\mathcal{W})
are described similarly. Finally, for a pair
(a,b)
, one obtains a quotient of

(\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}\circ\mathcal{Q})_{ab}=\cup_{a^{\prime}}\,\mathbb{D}\mathcal{A}_{aa^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{R}_{a^{\prime}b}\cup\cup_{b^{\prime}}\mathcal{R}_{ab^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{D}\mathcal{B}_{b^{\prime}b}

Taking
a=a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}=b
(when the diagonal bimodule morphism spaces are just a point) shows that there are two boundary faces equal to
\mathcal{W}_{ab}
(whilst the two corresponding
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{ab}
faces have been glued together). The whole union is then bordant to
\mathbb{D}\mathcal{W}_{ab}
, which shows that the composite map is bordant to the unit, and so
\mathcal{Q}
itself was an equivalence. The
E
-orientations come along for the ride.

The second part is similar.

4.7
Morse complexes

Let
\det(V)
denote the ‘usual’ determinant line of a finite-dimensional vector space or vector bundle
V
.

Definition 98
.

We say that a monoid in Thom
\mathcal{I}
-spaces
E
is
oriented
if we are given trivialisations of
\det(E(n))
which are compatible with both the maps
E(n)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{m-n}\to E(m)
and also with the maps
E(n)\times E(m)\to E(n+m)
underlying the monoidal structure
E\times E\to E
.

If
E
is oriented, and
Z
is an
E
-oriented zero-manifold, then we write
[Z]\in\mathbb{Z}
for the associated signed count.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
E
-oriented flow category and
M
a
\mathbb{Z}
-module. We define the
Morse chain complex

CM_{*}(\mathcal{F};M)
to be the chain complex defined as follows. As a graded abelian group,

CM_{*}(\mathcal{F}):=\bigoplus\limits_{x\in\mathcal{F}}M\cdot x

(4.7)

where for each
x\in\mathcal{F}
, we treat
x
as a formal generator in degree
|x|
.

The differential is defined by:

\partial x:=\sum\limits_{|x|-|y|=1}\sum\limits_{p\in\mathcal{F}_{xy}}[p]\cdot y

(4.8)

where
[p]\in\{\pm 1\}
denotes the
E
-orientation of
p
; this is then extended to be
\mathbb{Z}
-linear. This defines a chain complex, whose homology is written
HM_{*}(\mathcal{F};M)
.

As in
[
PS24b
]
, we have that
HM_{*}(\mathcal{F};M)
is functorial under
E
-oriented morphisms. An
E
-oriented bilinear map induces a degree zero chain-map, which descends to homology. If the relevant four bilinear maps admit an
E
-oriented associator, the product is associative on homology.

Write
[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]^{E,\leq i}
for morphisms in the category of truncated
E
-oriented flow categories
\tau_{\leq i}\Flow^{E}
.

Lemma 99
.

If
E
is oriented,
\pi_{0}\Thom(E)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, and
M
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module,
there is an isomorphism

[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]^{E,\leq 0}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M\longrightarrow\Hom(CM_{*}(\mathcal{F};M),CM_{*}(\mathcal{G};M)).

For any
E
with
\pi_{0}\Thom(E)\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
, and any
\mathbb{Z}/2
-module
M
,
there is an isomorphism

[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]^{E,\leq 0}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}M\longrightarrow\Hom(CM_{*}(\mathcal{F};M),CM_{*}(\mathcal{G};M)).

Proof 4.8
.

The proofs are essentially identical to one another and to those given in
[
PS24a
, Lemma 5.41]
and
[
PS24b
, Lemma 5.25]
.

4.8
Obstruction theory

The following is a version of
[
PS24a
, Theorem 5.48]
, incorporating
E
-orientations, and crucially allowing the case in which neither domain nor target flow category is a point. The statement and proof make reference to the bordism theory introduced in Section
4.4
.

Proposition 100
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-flow categories, and
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
an
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i}
-morphism. Then there is a homology class

[\mathcal{O}^{E}]\in\operatorname{Hom}\left(CM_{*+i+2}(\mathcal{A}),CM_{*}(\mathcal{B};\Omega^{E}_{i+1})\right)

(4.9)

such that if
[\mathcal{O}^{E}]
vanishes,
\mathcal{W}
is the truncation of an
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-morphism
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
.

Proof 4.9
.

This is an adaptation of the proof of
[
PS24b
, Proposition 5.38]
. Let
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be an extension of
\mathcal{W}
to a
E
-oriented pre-
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-morphism. The obstruction to
\mathcal{W}
being a
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-morphism is constructed as follows. For
x\in\mathcal{A},y\in\mathcal{B}
with
|x|-|y|=i+2
, we set

\mathcal{Y}_{xy}=\bigcup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}y}\cup\bigcup_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{W}_{xy^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{B}_{y^{\prime}y}

(4.10)

where we glue along the common faces of the form
\mathcal{A}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{W}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{B}_{y^{\prime}y}
. As written, this is not automatically a smooth manifold; however, it can be smoothed to a closed smooth
(i+1)
-manifold. We have index and stabilisation data for
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
, and their
E
-orientations are compatible with breaking and hence with the identifications of faces that enter into defining
\mathcal{Y}
. Equipping
\mathcal{Y}
with the product index and stabilisation data therefore equips it with an
E
-orientation, compare to
[
PS24b
, Section 5.7]
or
[
PS24a
, Section 5.5]
. The resulting manifold is well-defined up to
E
-oriented cobordism, and in particular gives a well-defined class in
\Omega^{E}_{i+1}
. These bordism classes together form the coefficients for a linear map of chain complexes

\mathcal{Y}:CM_{*+i+2}(\mathcal{A})\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{B};\Omega^{E}_{*})

(4.11)

If this vanishes, we may choose a filling of each
\mathcal{Y}_{xy}
, and use this to construct the required extension of
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
(cf.
[
PS24b
, Lemma 5.30]
).

Given an exact map of chain complexes
F:CM_{*+i+2}(\mathcal{A})\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{B};\Omega^{E}_{*})
, we use the coefficients to modify the extension
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
; the resulting obstruction map
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is exactly
\mathcal{R}+F
. Therefore if
\mathcal{Y}
is exact for some choice of extension
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
, we may choose a different extension
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
so that
\mathcal{Y}
vanishes (cf.
[
PS24b
, Lemma 5.32]
).

It remains to argue that
\mathcal{Y}
is a chain map, and so represents a homology class
[\mathcal{O}^{E}]
. This is equivalent to showing that the manifold

\bigsqcup\limits_{|x|-|x^{\prime}|=1}\mathcal{A}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{W}_{x^{\prime}y}\sqcup\bigsqcup\limits_{|y^{\prime}|-|y|=1}\mathcal{W}_{xy^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{B}_{y^{\prime}y}

(4.12)

represents the zero class in
\Omega^{E}_{i+1}
for all
x\in\mathcal{A}
,
y\in\mathcal{B}
with
|x|-|y|=i+3
. We construct a nullbordism as follows. For such
x,y
, define

\mathcal{X}_{xy}=\bigcup\limits_{|x|-|x^{\prime}|>1}\mathcal{A}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}y}\cup\bigcup\limits_{|y^{\prime}|-|y|>1}\mathcal{W}_{xy^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{B}_{y^{\prime}y}

(4.13)

where we glue along common faces of the form
\mathcal{A}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{A}_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\prime}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{x^{\prime\prime}y}
,
\mathcal{W}_{xy^{\prime\prime}}\times\mathcal{B}_{y^{\prime\prime}y^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{B}_{y^{\prime}y}
and
\mathcal{A}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{B}_{y^{\prime}y}
, for
|x|-|x^{\prime}|>1
and
|y^{\prime}|-|y|>1
. We again smooth this and equip it with a classifying map for its abstract index bundle
I^{\mathcal{X}}_{xy}\to E
as before, using the obvious inherited index and stabilisation data. This now provides the required nullbordism.

Such extensions always exist if one is allowed to modify the target flow category:

Proposition 101
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-flow categories, and
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
a
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i}
-morphism.

Then there is a
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-flow category
\mathcal{C}
, and a
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-equivalence
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
, and such that
\tau_{\leq i}\mathcal{C}=\tau_{\leq i}\mathcal{B}
and
\tau_{\leq i}\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{W}
.

By induction on
i
, this implies:

Corollary 102
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be
E
-oriented flow categories, and
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
an
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq 0}
-morphism.

Then there is a
E
-oriented flow category
\mathcal{C}
, and a
E
-oriented equivalence
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
, such that
\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{C}=\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{B}
and
\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{W}
.

Proof 4.10

(Proof of Proposition
101
)
.

\mathcal{C}
is defined to have objects the same as
\mathcal{B}
(with the same gradings), and

\mathcal{C}_{yy^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{B}_{yy^{\prime}}&\textrm{ if }|y|-|y^{\prime}|-1\leq i\\
\mathcal{B}_{yy^{\prime}}\sqcup P_{yy^{\prime}}&\textrm{ if }|y|-|y^{\prime}|-1=i+1\end{cases}

(4.14)

where for each
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
with
|y|-|y^{\prime}|-1=i+1
,
P_{yy^{\prime}}
is a choice of closed
(i+1)
-manifold with tangent bundle stably classified by a map
P_{yy^{\prime}}\to\Base(E)
(we will choose the
P_{yy^{\prime}}
in due course).
\mathcal{C}
is a
E
-oriented pre-
\tau_{\leq i+1}
flow category, where the maps to
E
are inherited from those given on
\mathcal{B}
and the
P_{yy^{\prime}}
. We write this as
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(P)
to indicate the choice involved; in particular,
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}(\emptyset)
.

By taking a matrix whose coefficients are given by the bordism class of each
P_{yy^{\prime}}
, we obtain a linear map of chain complexes

P:CM_{*+i+2}(\mathcal{B})\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{B};\Omega_{i+1}^{E})

(4.15)

Claim 103
.

\mathcal{C}
is an
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-flow category if and only if
P
is a map of chain complexes.

Proof 4.11

(Proof of claim.)
.

Let
(dP)_{xy}\in\Omega^{E}_{i+1}
be the coefficients of the matrix representing the linear map

dP:CM_{*+i+3}(\mathcal{B})\to CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{B};\Omega_{i+1}^{E}\right)

(4.16)

By construction, a nullbordism of (representatives of) all
(dP)_{xy}
is exactly the data of an extension of the pre-
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-flow category
\mathcal{C}
.

\mathcal{W}
defines a
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i}
-morphism
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
. From Proposition
100
, the obstruction to this admitting an extension to a
E
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-morphism
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
is a class

[\mathcal{O}^{E}(P)]\in\operatorname{Hom}\left(CM_{*+i+2}(\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{M}_{*}(\mathcal{B};\Omega^{E}_{i+1})\right)

(4.17)

Inspecting the construction of the class
[\mathcal{O}^{E}(P)]
, we find that

[\mathcal{O}^{E}(P)]=[\mathcal{O}^{E}(0)+P\circ\mathcal{W}_{*}]

(4.18)

where
\mathcal{W}_{*}:CM_{*}(\mathcal{A})\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{B})
is the map induced by
\mathcal{W}
. Since
\mathcal{W}_{*}
is a quasi-isomorphism, we may therefore choose
P
so that the homology class of
[\mathcal{O}^{E}(P)]
vanishes, and so the
\Psi
-oriented
\tau_{\leq i+1}
-extension
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{W}
exists for this
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(P)
.

{rmk}

Just as Proposition
100
adapts
[
PS24a
, Theorem 4.48]
to the case of
E
-orientations, one can analogously establish a version of
[
PS24a
, Theorem 5.51]
in our setting. As a consequence, if
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
is an
E
-oriented morphism of
E
-oriented flow categories and composition with
\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{W}
is surjective on groups of right
\tau_{\leq 0}
-modules, then composition with
\mathcal{W}
is in fact surjective as a map
[\ast[i],\mathcal{F}]^{\Flow^{E}}=\Omega^{E}_{i}(\mathcal{F})\to\Omega^{E}_{i}(\mathcal{G})=[\ast[i],\mathcal{G}]^{\Flow^{E}}
, for every
i
.

4.9
Flow categories over a target

Let
L
be a finite cell complex. We write
\mathcal{P}L
for the
Moore path space
of
L
, comprising pairs
(r,\alpha)
where
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\alpha:[0,r]\to L
. This admits an obvious concatenation operation. We will call
r
the
Moore length
of a given path.

Definition 104
.

A
flow category over
L
comprises a flow category
\mathcal{F}
, along with a functor of topologically enriched categories
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{P}L
.

{rmk}

Though Morse/Floer moduli spaces most naturally map to path spaces of
L
, it is sometimes convenient to work with based loop spaces (such as in Section
7
). Given a flow category
\mathcal{F}
over
L
and a basepoint in
\ell_{0}
, we may construct another flow category
\mathcal{F}
over
L
, with all
x\in\mathcal{F}
sent to
\ell_{0}
(using an idea from
[
BC07
]
).

To do this, we choose a
spider
, meaning a contractible set of arcs
Y\subseteq L
containing all the points each
x\in\mathcal{F}
are sent to, and connecting them to
\ell_{0}
. The quotient map
L\to L/Y
is a homotopy equivalence, so we may choose a homotopy inverse
\theta:L/Y\to L
, inducing a map
\theta:\Omega_{[\ell_{0}]}L/Y\to\Omega_{\ell_{0}}
. Then we take the compositions:
\mathcal{F}_{xy}\to\mathcal{P}_{xy}L\to\Omega_{[\ell_{0}]}L\to\Omega_{\ell_{0}}L
.

Explicitly, this consists of a point in
L
for each
x\in\mathcal{F}
(by abuse of notation we denote this by
x\in L
) and maps of spaces
\mathcal{F}_{xy}\to\mathcal{P}_{xy}L
, compatible with concatenation. We can define morphisms and bordisms of flow categories over
L
in the obvious way. To define composition of morphisms, note that the coequaliser diagram in
[
PS24b
, Equation (33)]
is compatible with evaluation to
\mathcal{P}L
, so the composition of morphisms over
L
inherits the structure of a morphism over
L
.

Definition 105
.

An
E
-oriented flow category over
L
consists of an
E
