\Delta_{\mathcal{P}}=\lambda_{l}(\Delta_{\mathcal{M}},\Delta_{\mathcal{N}})
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
be an unbroken Floer gluing. Then
\Delta_{\mathcal{P}}=\Delta_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Definition 123
.

Let
(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})
be
l
-composable. We define
\pi=\pi_{\Delta\Delta^{\prime};l}:\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta\sqcup\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{\prime}\to\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\lambda_{l}(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})
to send a boundary component to the component it is sent to in the glued disc.

From the definition, we see that
\pi
is surjective, and the fibres of
b
under
\pi
are a single point unless
b
is in the image of the boundary component(s) adjacent to the output of
\Delta
.

When
\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{P}
is a simple Floer concatenation of gluing number
l
, we sometimes write
\pi_{\mathcal{M}\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}}
for
\pi_{\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}\Delta_{\mathcal{N}};l}
.

Similarly when
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
is an unbroken Floer face, there is a natural bijection
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{P}}\to\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
, since
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are defined using the same Lagrangian boundary conditions and asymptotic conditions at the punctures (but not necessarily the same PDE).

Let
\mathcal{F}=\prod_{l}\mathcal{A}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}
be any Floer gluing. Since it can be factored as a sequence of unbroken and simple Floer gluings (cf. Remark
6.1
), we by composing the maps
\pi
, we obtain maps
\pi=\pi_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{M}}:\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}\to\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
; this is well-defined.

The
\pi
are suitably associative, in the sense that when
\mathcal{F}_{l}=\prod_{k}\mathcal{A}_{kl}\to\mathcal{G}_{l}
,
\prod_{l}\mathcal{G}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}
and
\prod_{l}\mathcal{F}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}
are Floer gluings, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{l}\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{l}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{l}\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{G}_{l}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}

(6.6)

For each
L\in\mathcal{L}
, choose a spider
Y_{L}\subseteq L
as in Section
4.9
through basepoints
\ell_{L}\in L
. We may assume
Y_{L}
passes through (the image in
L
of) all Floer generators
\mathcal{X}(L,K)
,
\mathcal{X}(K,L)
for all
K
(note this is a finite subset of
L
). Choose homotopy inverses
\theta_{L}:L/{Y_{L}}\to L
to the collapse maps.

Assuming all
L\in\mathcal{L}
are equipped with Riemannian metrics, there are then maps
ev=ev^{\mathcal{M}}
from each Floer moduli space
\mathcal{M}
to the (Moore) based loop spaces of each
L_{b}/Y_{L_{b}}
, where
L_{b}
are the Lagrangians appearing in the boundary conditions defining
\mathcal{M}
: these send
u:D\to X
to the restriction of
u
to the boundary componend
b
, parametrised anticlockwise. Composing with
\theta_{L_{b}}
and reparametrising so each path has speed 1, we obtain maps:

ev=ev^{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\to\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}\Omega L_{b}

(6.7)

which are compatible with Floer gluing and concatenation of loops in the natural way.

6.3
Boundary stabilisation data

Definition 124
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a choice of Lagrangian data.
Boundary stabilisation data

S
for
\mathcal{L}
consists of:

•

For each Floer moduli space
\mathcal{M}
and each
b\in\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
, an element
n_{b}=n_{b,\mathcal{M}}^{S}\in\mathcal{I}
.

•

For each Floer gluing
\mathcal{F}=\prod_{l}\mathcal{A}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}
, injections of finite sets:

\bigsqcup\limits_{b^{\prime}\in\pi_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{M}}^{-1}\{b\}}[n_{b^{\prime}}]\to[n_{b}]

(6.8)

We require that the maps (
6.8
) are suitably associative, meaning whenever
\mathcal{F}_{l}=\prod_{k}\mathcal{A}_{kl}\to\mathcal{G}_{l}
,
\prod_{l}\mathcal{G}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}
and
\prod_{l}\mathcal{F}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}
are Floer gluings and
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{b^{\prime\prime}\in\pi_{\prod_{l}\mathcal{F}_{l};\mathcal{M}}^{-1}\{b\}}[n_{b^{\prime\prime}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{b^{\prime}\in\pi_{\prod_{l}\mathcal{G}_{l};\mathcal{M}}^{-1}\{b\}}[n_{b^{\prime}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{[n_{b}]}

(6.9)

A
morphism
of boundary stabilisation data
S\to S^{\prime}
consists of injections of finite sets
[n^{S}_{b}]\to[n^{S^{\prime}}_{b}]
for all Floer moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}
and all
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
, which are suitably compatible with the maps (
6.8
)

Lemma 125
.

Let
S,S^{\prime}
be two choices of boundary stabilisation data.

Then there exists a choice
T
of boundary stabilisation data, such that there are morphisms
S,S^{\prime}\to T
.

Proof 6.1
.

Set
n^{T}_{b}=n^{S}_{b}+n^{S^{\prime}}_{b}
for all
b
. This induces suitable maps (
6.8
) in a natural way.

Lemma 126
.

Let
m
be an integer. Then there exists a choice of boundary stabilisation data
S
with
n^{S}_{b}\geq m
for all
b
.

Proof 6.2
.

We choose all the
n_{b}
for
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
, inductively on the dimension of the Floer moduli space
\mathcal{M}
. Consider the following collection of data:

•

Objects
n_{b}\in\mathcal{I}
for all
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
, whenever
\mathcal{M}
is a Floer moduli space with
\dim(\mathcal{M})\leq k
.

•

Injections (
6.8
) whenever
\dim(\mathcal{P})\leq k
, such that (
6.9
) commutes whenever
\dim(\mathcal{P})\leq k
.

For
k=0
, it is clear this exists, by setting
n_{b}=m
for all relevant
b
; note there are not yet any conditions of the form (
6.9
).

Now assume we have this data for some
k
. We choose similar data extending this to
k+1
. We define finite sets for all
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{P}}
for Floer moduli spaces with
\dim(\mathcal{P})=k+1
:

T_{b}:=\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{F}=\prod_{l}\mathcal{A}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}\,\textrm{Floer gluing}\\
b^{\prime}\in\pi_{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{M}}^{-1}\{b\}\end{subarray}}[n_{b}]\right)/\sim

(6.10)

where
\sim
is the minimal equivalence relation defined such that (
6.9
) always commutes for this
\mathcal{P}
, where each
[n_{b^{\prime}}]\to T_{b}
is the natural inclusion for all
b^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}\{b\}
.

Set
n_{b}=\max\{\#T_{b},m\}
. Choose injections
T_{b}\to[n_{b}]
; composing with the natural maps
[n_{b^{\prime}}]\to T_{b}
defines the maps required. (
6.9
) then commutes whenever it is defined by construction. The fact that each map
[n_{b^{\prime}}]\to T_{b}
is injective follows from the imposed commutativity of (
6.9
).

We therefore obtain boundary stabilisation data
S
for
\mathcal{L}
by induction.

An alternative more geometric argument is to choose a large finite subset
A\subseteq X
. Then for
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
, let
A_{b}
be the set of
x\in A
such that there is some
u\in\mathcal{M}
such that the restriction of
u
to
b
hits
x
under the evaluation map. Let
n_{b}=\#A_{b}
and choose bijections
A_{b}\simeq[n_{b}]
arbitrarily. The maps
\pi
, under this bijection, send
x\in A
to
x
. For
A
chosen generically, this is always injective.

7
The Fukaya category with monodromy local systems

For the entirety of this section, we fix a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoidal tangential pair (cf. Definition
56
)
\Psi=(\Theta,\Phi)
, and let
F=\mathrm{hofib}(\Theta\to\Phi)
. Fix a finite CW complex
L
.

Let
E_{\Psi}
be the commutative Thom
\mathcal{I}
-monoid constructed from
\Psi
(cf. Definition
66
), and
R_{\Psi}
the commutative ring spectrum constructed from
E_{\Psi}
(cf. Remark
2.9
). Let
GL_{1}(R_{\Psi})
be its commutative
\mathcal{I}
-space of units (cf. Definition
46
); for brevity, we will abbreviate this to
GL_{1}^{\Psi}
throughout much of this section. We write
B(GL_{1})_{h\mathcal{I}}=BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}
for space given by the bar construction applied to the monoid
GL_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}
.

Note that since
E_{\Psi}
is connective, so are both
R_{\Psi}
and
B(GL_{1})_{h\mathcal{I}}
.

Lemma 127
.

R_{\Psi}
is a connective ring spectrum. Furthermore:

\pi_{0}R_{\Psi}\simeq\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}&\textrm{ if $\Psi$ is oriented}\\
\mathbb{Z}/2&\textrm{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(7.1)

Proof 7.1
.

For the first statement, any Thom spectrum is connective.
F
is simply connected by assumption so
\Omega F\simeq\Base(E_{\Psi})
is connected. By the stable Hurewicz theorem,
\pi_{0}R\simeq H_{0}(R)
. By the Thom isomorphism theorem,
H_{0}(R)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
if the index bundle over
\Base(E_{\Psi})
is orientable, and
H_{0}(R)\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
otherwise.

By Hurewicz and the fact that
\pi_{2}U/O\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
, the class
[w_{2}\circ Re]\in H^{2}(\widetilde{U/O};\mathbb{Z}/2)
detects
\pi_{2}
. Therefore it follows from
[
Geo13
, Theorem 1.1]
(cf. also
[
dS98
, Section 3]
) that the index bundle is orientable if and only if
F\to\widetilde{U/O}
is 0 on
\pi_{2}
.

7.1
Monodromy local systems

Definition 128
.

A
(
\Psi
-)(monodromy) local system on
L
(MLS)
consists of a map of spaces
\xi:L\to B(GL_{1})_{h\mathcal{I}}
.

A
homotopy
between local systems
\xi
and
\zeta
consists of a homotopy between the corresponding maps.

{ex}

The
trivial local system

\xi:L\to B(GL_{1})_{h\mathcal{I}}
is the constant map to the basepoint.

Lemma 129
.

If
\Psi
is oriented, there is a function from the set of
\Psi
-monodromy local systems on
L
to the set of
\mathbb{Z}
-local systems on
L
.

Proof 7.2
.

By Lemma
127
, there is a map of ring spectra
R\to H\mathbb{Z}
, hence we obtain a map
BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})\to BGL_{1}(H\mathbb{Z})\simeq K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)
; postcomposition with this gives the required map.

7.2
Twisting data

We fix a set
\mathcal{L}
of Lagrangian data as in Section
5.1
.

Definition 130
.

Let
S
be boundary stabilisation data for
\mathcal{L}
.
Twisting data

\Xi
for
\mathcal{L}
(with respect to
S
) consists of maps of spaces:

\Xi_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\to\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n_{b}^{S})

(7.2)

for each Floer moduli space
\mathcal{M}
. We require that the following diagram commutes for each Floer gluing map
\prod_{l}\mathcal{A}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}
:

\textstyle{\prod_{l}\mathcal{A}_{l}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod\limits_{l}\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{A}_{l}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n^{S}_{b})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod\limits_{b^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n_{b^{\prime\prime}}^{S})}

(7.3)

Here the right-hand vertical arrow is given by the product induced by (
6.8
).

A
homotopy
between twisting data
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\prime}
(both with respect to the same boundary stabilisation data
S
) consists of a 1-parameter family of twisting data
\{\Xi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
interpolating between
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\prime}
. This can be encoded equivalently as maps
\mathcal{M}\times[0,1]\to\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n_{b}^{S})
satisfying a similar associativity condition.

Definition 131
.

Let
S\to T
be a morphism of boundary stabilisation data, and
\Xi
twisting data for
\mathcal{L}
with respect to
S
. The
induced
twisting data for
\mathcal{L}
with respect to
T
is given by composing each
\Xi_{\mathcal{M}}
with (products of) the maps
GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n_{b}^{S})\to GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n_{b}^{T})
induced by the maps
[n_{b}^{S}]\to[n_{b}^{T}]
in
\mathcal{I}
.

A
concordance
between twisting data
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\prime}
(with respect to boundary stabilisation data
S
and
S^{\prime}
respectively) consists of morphisms of boundary stabilisation data
S,S^{\prime}\to T
for some boundary stabilisation data
T
, along with a homotopy between the two induced sets of twisting data with respect to
T
.

We now incorporate
\Psi
-monodromy local systems.

Definition 132
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a choice of Lagrangian data. A choice of
local system data

\xi_{\mathcal{L}}
on
\mathcal{L}
consists of a
\Psi
-monodromy local system
\xi_{L}:L\to BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}
for each
L\in\mathcal{L}
.

Proposition 133
.

Let
\xi_{\mathcal{L}}
be a choice of local system data on a set of Lagrangian data
\mathcal{L}
, and
S
sufficiently large boundary stabilisation data. Then associated to
\xi_{\mathcal{L}}
there is a preferred homotopy class of twisting data for
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof 7.3
.

Assume the boundary stabilisation data
S
is large enough such that for each Floer moduli space
\mathcal{M}
, the map induced by inclusions of 0-simplices

\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n_{b})\to\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}(GL_{1}^{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}=\left(GL_{1}^{\Psi}\right)_{h\mathcal{I}}^{\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}

(7.4)

is
(\dim(\mathcal{M})+2)
-connected.

Consider the following lifting problem for each Floer moduli space
\mathcal{M}
:

\textstyle{\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n^{S}_{b})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}
\textstyle{\left(GL_{1}^{\Psi}\right)_{h\mathcal{I}}^{\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\iota}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ev}
\scriptstyle{\exists?}
\textstyle{\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}\Omega L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega\xi}
\textstyle{\left(\Omega B(GL_{1}^{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}\right)^{\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}}

(7.5)

The upper vertical arrow is highly-connected by the choice of
S
.
\widetilde{\iota}_{1}
is the zig-zag from Lemma
19
; recall each map in the zig-zag is a monoid map which is a homotopy equivalence of spaces. The map
ev
is from (
6.7
).

Therefore for each Floer moduli space
\mathcal{M}
there exists a homotopy lift: this is a diagonal map as in the diagram, along with a homotopy between the two ways around the diagram. Similarly, if such a homotopy lift is already chosen over the subspace given by the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
, we may choose a homotopy lift over all of
\mathcal{M}
to extend this one.

Using this, by inducting over
\dim(\mathcal{M})
(which we can do, since
\sum_{l}\dim(\mathcal{A}_{l})<\dim(\mathcal{M})
whenever
\prod_{l}\mathcal{A}_{l}\to\mathcal{M}
is a Floer gluing), we may choose homotopy lifts as above so that the resulting maps
\mathcal{M}\to\Pi_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n^{S}_{b})
are suitably associative, and so assemble into a set of twisting data with respect to
S
.

The same argument shows that for any two such sets of twisting data
\Xi,\Xi^{\prime}
constructed in this way, there is a homotopy between them.

7.3
Transverse sections

Let
(X,E)
be a Thom space, and
M
a manifold. Assume the rank of
E
is at least 5. Let
f:M\to\Thom(E\to X)
be a continuous map.

Let
M^{\prime}=f^{-1}\mathrm{Tot}(E\to X)
and
M_{0}=f^{-1}\{0\}\subseteq M^{\prime}
, both subspaces of
M
. The map
f:M^{\prime}\to\mathrm{Tot}(E\to X)
defines a section
s_{f}
of the pullback bundle
f^{*}E\to M^{\prime}
(really this is
(\pi\circ f)^{*}E
, where
\pi:E\to X
is the projection). Its zero set is exactly
M_{0}
.

\mathrm{Tot}(f^{*}E\to M^{\prime})
does not automatically have a smooth structure. However, its tangent microbundle has a canonical vector bundle lift, given by
f^{*}E\oplus TM^{\prime}
, so by smoothing theory (using the rank assumption above), it can be equipped with a smooth structure canonically up to contractible choice; we choose such a structure implicitly.

{rmk}

The smoothing of a topological manifold with a vector bundle lift of its tangent microbundle admits a relative version. Given a manifold with faces, flow-category, etc, one can therefore construct such smoothings compatibly over the strata, cf. the analogous inductive argument given in
[
BX22
]
.

We say
f
is
smooth along 0
if
s_{f}
is smooth along
M_{0}
, and if this holds, that
f
is
transverse along 0
if the vertical derivative
TM|_{M_{0}}\to f^{*}E
is fibrewise surjective. If
f
is smooth and transverse along 0,
M_{0}
is naturally a smooth manifold. The following is standard:

Lemma 134
.

Let
f:M\to\Thom(E\to X)
be as above. Then there is a
C^{0}
-small perturbation
f^{\prime}
of
f
that is smooth and transverse along 0.

Furthermore, if
f
is smooth and transverse over some open subset
U\subseteq M
, we can take
f^{\prime}
to agree with
f
over
U
.

If
f
is smooth and transverse along 0, there is a canonical (up to contractible choice) isomorphism of vector bundles over
M_{0}
:

TM_{0}\oplus f^{*}E\simeq TM|_{M_{0}}

(7.6)

If
M
has boundary (or faces), we additionally require that
f
is smooth and transverse over each boundary face. In that case,
M_{0}
is also a manifold with boundary (or faces, with a system of faces induced by that of
M
). (
7.6
) is compatible with passing to boundary faces in the natural way.

7.4
Twisted moduli spaces

Assume we are given some twisting data
\Xi
. Recall that by definition,
GL_{1}(n)
is a union of components of
\Omega^{n}\Thom(\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(n)\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(n))
. Hence the maps
\Xi_{\mathcal{M}}
give maps, for each Floer moduli space
\mathcal{M}
:

\hat{\Xi}_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\times S^{(\sum_{b}n_{b})}\to\Thom\left(\bigoplus_{b}\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(n_{b})\to\prod_{b}\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(n_{b})\right)

(7.7)

where
b
runs over
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
.
By Lemma
134
, we may assume these maps are all smooth and transverse along 0. We define the
twisted Floer moduli spaces
to be the preimage of 0 of each
\hat{\Xi}_{\mathcal{M}}
. We use superscripts
\xi
to denote the twisted Floer moduli spaces: for an arbitrary Floer moduli space, we write its twisted version
\hat{\Xi}_{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}\{0\}
as
\mathcal{M}^{\xi}
, and for a specific Floer moduli space e.g.
\mathcal{M}^{LL^{\prime}}_{xx^{\prime}}
associated to Lagrangians
L,L^{\prime}
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{LL^{\prime}}
, we write
\mathcal{M}^{\xi_{L},\xi_{L^{\prime}}}_{xx^{\prime}}
for its twisted version. Note that since the rank of each
\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(n_{b})
is
n_{b}
, this manifold has the same dimension as
\mathcal{M}
.

Since the maps
\Xi_{\mathcal{M}}
are compatible with Floer gluing maps, the
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
inherit similar maps
\prod_{l}\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{l}\to\hat{\mathcal{M}}
. In particular, we obtain all the same algebraic structure: for example, given
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
with local systems
\xi_{L},\xi_{L^{\prime}}
, the
\mathcal{M}^{\xi_{L},\xi_{L^{\prime}}}_{xx^{\prime}}
assemble to form a flow category.

7.5
E_{\Psi}
-orientations on twisted moduli spaces

We now return to Equation
7.7
and the twisted moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\xi}=\hat{\Xi}_{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}(0)
. The
E_{\Psi}
-orientation on the moduli space
\mathcal{M}
is given by maps

(\mathcal{M},I^{\mathcal{M}})(\nu_{\mathcal{M}})\to E(\nu_{\mathcal{M}}+d_{\mathcal{M}})=(\mathbb{U}^{\Psi},\mathbb{V}^{\Psi})(\nu_{\mathcal{M}}+d_{\mathcal{M}})

(7.8)

(perhaps with a further shift
[i]
on the RHS if the moduli space
\mathcal{M}
arises from a morphism
(i=1)
, bordism of morphisms or associator
(i=2)
, etc), which induce isomorphisms of pullback bundles.

Consider Equation
7.6
, with
M_{0}=\mathcal{M}^{\xi}\subset\mathcal{M}\times S^{n}=M
, with
n=\sum_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}(\partial_{\mathcal{M}})}n_{b}
, and with
E=\oplus_{b}\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(n_{b})
. We see that

T\mathcal{M}^{\xi}\oplus\oplus_{b}\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(n_{b})=T\mathcal{M}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Together with (
7.8
), we have

I^{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}(\nu_{\mathcal{M}})\oplus\oplus_{b}\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(n_{b})=\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(\nu_{\mathcal{M}}+d_{\mathcal{M}})\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n}

(7.9)

Recall the notion of an inverse for a map to an
\mathcal{I}
-monoid, as in Definition
49
.

Lemma 135
.

Fix a finite collection of Floer moduli spaces
\{\mathcal{M}\}
with stabilisation, boundary stabilisation and index data. Construct twisted moduli spaces
\{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}\}
as above. We may choose:

1.

n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}
and
l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}
and injections
[\sum_{b\subset\partial\mathcal{M}}n_{b}]\sqcup[n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}]\hookrightarrow[l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}]
;

2.

maps
\mathcal{M}^{\xi}\to\mathbb{U}(n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}})
inverse to the maps
\mathcal{M}^{\xi}\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(\sum_{b}n_{b})

coherently under breaking.

Proof 7.4

(Sketch)
.

For a single moduli space, existence is given by Lemma
50
. We pick
n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}},l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}
, and the inverse map, together with associated nullhomotopy of the map
\mathcal{M}^{\xi}\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(\sum_{n}n_{b})\times\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}})\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}})
, inductively in the dimension of
\mathcal{M}^{\xi}
(equivalently the dimension of
\mathcal{M}
). Since, by Lemma
50
, the space of choices is arbitrarily highly connected for large
n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}},l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}
, these choices can always be extended from boundary strata to higher dimensional moduli spaces in the given finite collection.

Adding
\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}})\oplus\mathbb{R}^{l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}-\sum_{b}n_{b}-n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}}
to both sides of (
7.9
), where the first term is pulled back via the inverse map constructed in Lemma
135
, we obtain that

I^{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}(\nu_{\mathcal{M}})\oplus\mathbb{R}^{l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}}=\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(\nu_{\mathcal{M}}+d_{\mathcal{M}})\oplus\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}})\oplus\mathbb{R}^{l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}-n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}}

where the two
\mathbb{V}
-terms on the RHS arise respectively from the maps
\mathcal{M}^{\xi}\to\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(\nu_{\mathcal{M}}+d_{\mathcal{M}})
and the ‘inverse map’
\mathcal{M}^{\xi}\to\mathbb{U}^{\Psi}(n_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}})
.

Combining this with the maps

\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(\bullet)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{V}^{\Psi}(\bullet+k)

from the Thom
\mathcal{I}
-space structure, we find that if we define

\nu_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}=\nu_{\mathcal{M}}+l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}\qquad\mathrm{and}\quad d_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}=d_{\mathcal{M}}+l_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}

(7.10)

then we obtain valid choices of index and stabilisation data for the twisted moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\xi}
.

The coherence of the classifying maps to
E_{\Psi}(\nu_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}}+d_{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}})
under breaking is inherited from (i) the corresponding coherence for the
E_{\Psi}
-orientation on
\mathcal{M}
, (ii) the compatibility of the maps
\Xi_{\mathcal{M}}
with Floer gluings, and (iii) the coherence in the construction of the inverse maps in Lemma
135
. This serves to define coherent
E_{\Psi}
-orientations on the twisted moduli spaces
\{\mathcal{M}^{\xi}\}
.

7.6
The Fukaya category with local systems

We pick a finite set of Lagrangians
\mathcal{L}
, and for each one pick some finite collection of
\Psi
-monodromy local systems
\{\xi^{(j)}_{L}\,|\,1\leq j\leq J(L),\,L\in\mathcal{L}\}
(
J(L)
is some number). We define a spectral (Donaldson-)Fukaya category
\scrF^{loc}(X;\Psi)
with objects
(L,\xi_{L})
, where
\xi_{L}\to L
is one of this finite set of distinguished monodromy local systems. We will typically abbreviate the pair (
L,\xi_{L})
to just
\xi_{L}
, and we call such pairs
\Psi
-branes
.

We fix a commutative
\mathcal{I}
-monoidal tangential structure
\Psi
and
E_{\Psi}
-orientations on all the twisted moduli spaces, as above. We thus have twists

\mathcal{M}^{\xi_{L},\xi_{K}}

of
\mathcal{M}^{LK}
as in Section
7.4
, which are
E_{\Psi}
-oriented flow categories.

Definition 136
.

The morphism group
\scrF^{loc}_{i}((L,\xi_{L}),(K,\xi_{K});\Psi)
is the group of bordism classes of right flow modules
\Omega_{i}^{E_{\Psi}}(\mathcal{M}^{\xi_{L},\xi_{K}})
.

It follows from the discussion in Section
7.4
that all the algebraic structures in Theorems
5.1
,
107
and
111
exist for tuples of pairs
(L,\xi_{L})
rather than just Lagrangians
L
.

Therefore in the same way as in Definition
112
, the objects
\xi_{L}
and morphism groups above define a category
\scrF^{\loc}(X;\Psi)
.

There are truncations
\tau_{\leq i}\scrF^{loc}(X;\Psi)
, similarly to Definition
113
.

Definition 137
.

Let
(L,\xi_{L})
be an object in
\scrF^{loc}(X;\Psi)
. We define the class
[L,\xi]\in\Omega^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}_{d}(L,\phi|_{L})
to be the bordism class of the closed manifold
\Upsilon_{\xi_{L}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\xi_{L}}
, with its natural
E_{\Psi}
-orientation relative to
\phi|_{L}
.

{rmk}

Note that in contrast to Lemma
115
, the definition of
[L,\xi_{L}]
is not obviously topological: it uses the Floer moduli spaces. We give a topological description of this class in Section
8
.

Proposition 138
.

Let
(L,\xi_{L})
and
(K,\xi_{K})
be objects in
\scrF^{loc}(X;\Psi)
.

If
(L,\xi_{L})
and
(K,\xi_{K})
are isomorphic, then
\iota^{L}_{*}[L,\xi_{L}]=\iota^{K}_{*}[K,\xi_{K}]
.

Proof 7.5
.

Similarly to Proposition
117
, this follows from Theorem
107
(4) and (5), twisting the appropriate moduli spaces by the local systems as usual.

7.7
Invariance

We fix Lagrangian and local system data
\mathcal{L}
, meaning a finite collection of pairs
(L,\xi_{L})
of Lagrangians (with their tangential lifts) equipped with spectral local systems
\xi_{L}:L\to B(GL_{1})_{h\mathcal{I}}
, as in Section
7.6
. We always suppose that
\mathcal{L}\supset\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{triv}}
includes the
\Psi
-branes associated to the trivial local system on each Lagrangian
L
appearing in some pair in
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma 139
.

Let
(L,\xi_{L})
and
(K,\xi_{K})
be two pairs in
\mathcal{L}
.

Then flow category
\mathcal{M}^{\xi_{L},\xi_{K}}
is well-defined up to isomorphism in
\Flow^{\Psi}
: it does not depend on the choice of Floer data, boundar stabilisation data, nor the choice of representative
\Xi
of the equivalence class of twistings defined from
\mathcal{L}
.

Its isomorphism class is also invariant under replacing the
\Psi
-local systems
\xi_{L}
with homotopic ones.

Proof 7.6
.

The local systems, along with a choice of boundary stabilisation data
S
, determines a unique homotopy class of transverse twisting data. A homotopy of transverse twisting data defines a morphism of the twisted flow categories associated to the transverse end-points, composition with which is an equivalence in
\Flow
(one can build an inverse in a similar manner). Coherently
\Psi
-orienting the homotopy lifts this equivalence to
\Flow^{\Psi}
. Changing the boundary stabilisation data up to concordance (so changing the ranks of the twisting data) yields a stabilisation of the associated twisted flow categories in the sense of
[
PS24b
, Remark 5.8]
.

Proposition 140
.

The subcategory
\scrF^{\loc;\mathcal{L}}(X;\Psi)
associated to the objects
\mathcal{L}
is well-defined up to quasi-isomorphism.

Proof 7.7
.

Independence of boundary stabilisation data follows from Lemma
139
(a similar argument applies to bilinear maps). Independence of the generic almost complex structures and perturbations used in the construction follows as in the case without local systems.

Independence with respect to the choice of spider follows from the same argument as in
[
BDHOb
, Section 6.2]
.

If
\zeta_{L}\to L
is a MLS and
\{L_{t}\}
is a Hamiltonian isotopy from
L=L_{0}
to
L^{\prime}=L_{1}
, there is an induced MLS
\zeta_{L^{\prime}}\to L^{\prime}
. By expanding if necessary, suppose both
(L,\zeta_{L})
and
(L^{\prime},\zeta_{L^{\prime}})
belong to the fixed Lagrangian data
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma 141
.

The pairs
(L,\zeta_{L})
and
(L^{\prime},\zeta_{L^{\prime}})
are quasi-isomorphic in
\scrF^{\loc;\mathcal{L}}(X,\Psi)
.

Proof 7.8
.

Follows from a standard continuation map argument, twisted as in the proof of Lemma
139
.

Proposition 142
.

The full subcategory

\scrF^{\loc,\mathcal{L}^{triv}}(X;\Psi)\subset\scrF^{\loc,\mathcal{L}}(X;\Psi)

is isomorphic to the category with the same objects as defined in Section
5.2
.

Proof 7.9
.

All the Floer moduli spaces relevant to
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{triv}}
carry trivial local systems. We take boundary stabilisation data
S\equiv\{0\}
on all moduli spaces, so
GL_{1}(n_{b}^{S})=\{pt\}
reduces to the base-point. Note that the inductive argument which constructs pretwisting data goes through for this
S
, since
\Omega\xi
is a constant map. The associated twisting data can then be taken to give maps
\hat{\Xi}_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\to GL_{1}(0)=\{pt\}
, so the twisted moduli spaces agree identically with the untwisted ones. The result then follows from independence of the construction on the choice of boundary stabilisation data.

7.8
Relation to ordinary Floer theory

We begin with a brief discussion of the appropriate tangential pairs for Liouville domains which may fail to be spin or even orientable.

Lemma 143
.

Assume
\Psi
is an oriented commutative tangential pair. Write
\theta:\Theta\to BO
,
\phi:\Phi\to BS_{\pm}U
,
f:F\to\widetilde{U/O}
and
g:\Theta\to\Phi
be the corresponding maps, and
w_{2}\in H^{2}(BO_{h\mathcal{I}};\mathbb{Z}/2)
for the universal second Stiefel-Whitney class.

Then
\theta^{*}w_{2}
lies in the image of
g^{*}:H^{2}(\Phi_{h\mathcal{I}};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{2}(\Theta_{h\mathcal{I}};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Additionally,
g^{*}
is injective (so the preimage of
\theta^{*}w_{2}
is unique).

Definition 144
.

Let
\Psi=(g:\Theta\to\Phi)
be an oriented commutative tangential pair.

We define the
universal background class for
\Psi
to be the (unique) preimage
\theta^{*}w_{2}
under
g^{*}
, where
\theta
is the map
\Theta\to BO
. We write
\omega_{2}\in H^{2}(\Phi_{h\mathcal{I}};\mathbb{Z}/2)
for this class.

{rmk}

Let
\Psi=(\Theta,\Phi)
be an oriented tangential pair, and
\phi
a
\Phi
-orientation on
X
. Then Lemma
143
implies any
\Psi
-oriented Lagrangian
L\subseteq X
is relatively Spin, relative to the background class
\phi^{*}\omega_{2}\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

Proof 7.10

(Proof of Lemma
143
)
.

Throughout this proof, we write
H^{*}(\cdot)
for
H^{*}(\cdot;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and for an
\mathcal{I}
-space
X
(by abuse of notation) we write
X
for
X_{h\mathcal{I}}
.

For any fibration sequence
G\to E\to B
with simply-connected fibre
G
, an application of the Serre spectral sequence shows that there is a short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow H^{2}(B)\to H^{2}(E)\to\ker\left(d_{3}:H^{0}(B;H^{2}(G))\to H^{3}(B)\right)\to 0

(7.11)

where
d_{3}
is the differential on the
E_{3}
-page. In particular this implies
g^{*}
is injective. Note this exact sequence is functorial.

Applying this to the homotopy fibration sequences
\widetilde{U/O}\to BO\to BS_{\pm}U
and
F\to\Theta\to\Phi
, we obtain a commutative diagram with exact rows:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(BS_{\pm}U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(BO)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{*}}
\textstyle{\ker d_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\Phi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\Theta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ker d^{\prime}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

(7.12)

where
d_{3}
and
d^{\prime}_{3}
are the differential pages on the two Serre spectral sequences respectively. We next argue that the map
p:\ker d_{3}\to\ker d^{\prime}_{3}
vanishes; the lemma then follows by a diagram chase.

Since
\widetilde{U/O}
is simply connected, the Hurewicz map
\mathbb{Z}/2\simeq\pi_{2}\widetilde{U/O}\to H_{2}(\widetilde{U/O})
is an isomorphism.
\mathbb{Z}/2
has no non-trivial automorphisms so
\pi_{1}BS_{\pm}U
must act trivially on it, and so
H^{0}(BS_{\pm}U;H^{2}(\widetilde{U/O}))\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
. It is standard that
H^{2}(BO)\simeq(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 2}
and
H^{2}(BS_{\pm}U)\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
, so by a dimension count we find that
d_{3}:\mathbb{Z}/2\to\ldots
must also vanish.

Since
\Psi
is oriented,
f_{*}:\pi_{2}F\to\pi_{2}\widetilde{U/O}
vanishes; since
F
and
\widetilde{U/O}
are simply connected,
f_{*}
must vanish on
H_{2}(\cdot;\mathbb{Z})
and hence
f^{*}
vanishes on
H^{2}
. Since
p=H^{2}(\phi^{*};f^{*}):H^{0}(BS_{\pm}U;H^{2}(\widetilde{U/O}))\to H^{0}(\Phi;H^{2}(F))
,
p
must vanish.

{ex}

The map
w_{2}:BSO\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2)
associated to the second Stiefel-Whitney class is realised by an infinite loop map. (This is because
\pi_{2}(BSO)=\mathbb{Z}/2
is the lowest non-trivial homotopy group, the map is associated to the Postnikov truncation
bso\to\tau_{\leq 2}bso
in spectra, which is always infinite loop.) There is therefore a commutative tangential pair
(\Theta\to\Phi)=(hofib(BSO\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2))\to BS_{\pm U})
. In this case the universal class
w_{2}
vanishes, and
\Theta
-oriented Lagrangians are graded and
Spin
, recovering a ‘classical’ setting.

{rmk}

Oscar Randal-Williams pointed out to us that, by contrast,
w_{2}:BO\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2)
does not deloop. Therefore
w_{1}^{2}+w_{2}
also does not deloop on
BO
(since the infinite loop automorphism
-1
of
BO
exchanges these two classes).

Recall that the
\Phi
-orientation on
X
means it is also equipped with a grading. Let
\scrF^{loc}(X;\mathbb{Z})
denote the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded compact Fukaya category whose objects are (exact, graded so Maslov index zero) Lagrangian branes
(L,\xi_{\mathbb{Z}})
equipped with rank one
\mathbb{Z}
-local systems
\xi_{\mathbb{Z}}:\pi_{1}(L)\to H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}/2
.

For
w\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
there is a
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficient
\mathbb{Z}
-graded Fukaya category
\scrF(X,w;\mathbb{Z})
whose objects are graded exact Lagrangian submanifolds which are relatively spin for the background class
w
; choosing
w
amounts to fixing a particular coherent orientation scheme for the Fukaya category, see
[
Sei08
, Section 8]
. Similarly, we write
\scrF^{loc,\mathcal{L}}(X,w;\mathbb{Z})
for the
w
-sign-twisted Fukaya category with objects Lagrangians drawn from
\mathcal{L}
, perhaps equipped with
\mathbb{Z}
-local systems.

Proposition 145
.

Let
\Psi
be an oriented (graded) tangential pair. Fix a set of
\Psi
-oriented Lagrangian data
\mathcal{L}
. Then there is a fully faithful functor

\tau_{\leq 0}\scrF^{loc,\mathcal{L}}(X;\Psi)\to\scrF^{loc,\mathcal{L}}(X,\phi^{*}w_{2};\mathbb{Z}).

Proof 7.11
.

By definition, the morphism group
\scrF_{i}^{loc}(\xi_{L},\xi_{K};\Psi)=[\ast[i],\mathcal{M}^{\xi_{L},\xi_{K}}]^{\Flow_{E_{\Psi}}}
. As observed above, any
\Theta
-oriented Lagrangian is
\phi^{*}w_{2}
-relatively spin, so defines an object in
\scrF^{loc,\mathcal{L}}(X,\phi^{*}w_{2};\mathbb{Z})
; Lemma
129
allows one to extend this association to Lagrangians equipped with local systems. Lemma
99
then says that it suffices to compare the signs associated to
\Psi
-oriented zero-manifolds and those arising in classical Floer theory. In both cases, these are determined by the fact that moduli spaces of Floer strips are canonically oriented relative to the orientation lines of the
\overline{\partial}
-operators associated to the puncture data at the ends.

Recall that
H^{1}(\mathcal{L}U/O;\mathbb{Z}/2)=\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
with the generators being the mod
2
reduction of the Maslov class and the pullback of
w_{2}
under the map
S^{1}\times\mathcal{L}(U/O)\to U/O\to BO
. In particular, on loops of Maslov index zero (or any fixed Maslov index) the isomorphism class of the determinant line of the
\overline{\partial}
-operator associated to a loop of Lagrangian subspaces is determined by
w_{2}
, cf
[
Sei08
, Lemmas 11.7 & 11.17]
(note that the group
\mathrm{Pin}_{n}
in
op.cit.
is the one, sometimes called
\mathrm{Pin}^{+}
, which is associated to
w_{2}\in H^{2}(BO_{n};\mathbb{Z}/2)
).

The image of the functor is the set of those
\mathbb{Z}
-local systems on Lagrangians in
\mathcal{L}
lying in the image of the map from Lemma
129
.

8
Open-closed map and local systems

In this section, we give a topological description of the classes
[L,\xi]\in\Omega^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}_{d}(L,\phi|_{L})
from Section
7.6
.

Fix some commutative tangential pair
\Psi
, and let
E_{\Psi}
,
R=\Thom(E_{\Psi})
and
GL_{1}^{\Psi}=GL_{1}(R)
be as in Section
7
.

Theorem 146
.

Let
(L,\xi)
be an object of
\scrF(X;\Psi)
: so
L
is a closed exact
\Psi
-oriented Lagrangian in
X
, and
\xi
a
\Psi
-local system on
L
. Let
[L,\xi]\in\Omega^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}_{d}(L,\phi|_{L})
be the class defined in Definition
137
, and
[L]\in\Omega^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}_{d}(L,\phi|_{L})
the fundamental class of
L
. Then:

[L,\xi]=[L]\cap[\eta\xi]\in\Omega_{d}^{E_{\Psi},\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}}(L,\phi_{L})

(8.1)

where
\cap
is the module action from Lemma
114
, and
[\eta\xi]
is the degree 0
R
-cohomology class given by the following composition:

L\xrightarrow{\xi}B(GL_{1}^{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}\xrightarrow{\eta_{*}}(GL_{1}^{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\Omega^{\infty}R

(8.2)

Here
\eta_{*}
is the action of the Hopf map
S^{3}\to S^{2}
on the infinite loop space
GL_{1}^{\Psi}
as in Section
2.6
.

Theorem
1
is obtained by pushing forwards along the inclusion
L\to X
.

8.1
Overview

We prove Theorem
146
in several steps.

1.

We interpret the class
[L,\xi]
as the twist of a single manifold in Section
8.2.2
, by a map which naturally lands in a cyclic bar-type construction applied to
GL_{1}^{\Psi}
(which we describe in detail in Section
8.4
).

2.

In Section
8.5
, we factor this “cyclic twist map” through the constant loops, roughly using the fact that for appropriate Floer data, the relevant holomorphic curves approximate Morse trajectories. The free loops we see then look like loops which follow a gradient flow followed by its reverse; we may then apply Lemma
28
.

3.

In Section
8.6
, we relate this construction to Schlichtkrull’s description of
\eta
: he shows that for an infinite loop space
E
, the composition:

BE\xrightarrow{const}\mathcal{L}BE\simeq\Omega BE\times BE\simeq E\times BE\xrightarrow{project}E

(8.3)

is homotopic to
\eta_{*}
.

At most stages of the argument, we must keep track of “finite-level” approximations for cyclic bar constructions and similar: our maps land more naturally in e.g.
GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n)
for finite
n
than
(GL_{1}^{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}
.

Throughout this section, we fix
(L,\xi)
as in Theorem
146
.

8.2
Set-up

We begin by introducing some notation and intermediary spaces.

8.2.1
Labelling

We write
\mathcal{M}
for the flow category
\mathcal{M}^{LL}
,
\mathcal{E}
for the right flow module
\mathcal{E}^{L}
and
\Upsilon
for the left flow module
\Upsilon_{L}
.

By setting
\mathcal{M}_{xx}:=\operatorname{pt}
,
\mathcal{M}
becomes a unital topological category, and
\mathcal{E}
and
\Upsilon
are right and left modules over it respectively.

Definition 147
.

We define the
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}
-moduli spaces
to be the Floer moduli spaces
\mathcal{E}_{x}
,
\Upsilon_{x}
and
\mathcal{M}_{xx^{\prime}}
, for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
.

\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}
-boundary stabilisation data

S
consists of the same data as in Definition
124
, but only for the
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}
-moduli spaces; along with one additional piece of data, namely some
n_{0}=n^{S}_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
and (suitably associative) injections
\sqcup_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}}[n_{b}]\to[n_{0}]
for all
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}
-Floer moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}
.

We say
S
is
saturated
if all the injections (
6.8
), as well as all injections of the form:

\left(\sqcup_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{E}_{x}}}[n_{b}]\right)\sqcup\left(\sqcup_{b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\Upsilon_{x}}}[n_{b^{\prime}}]\right)\to[n_{0}]

(8.4)

are in fact bijections.

Lemma 148
.

Saturated
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}
-boundary stabilisation data
S
exists. Furthermore given some
m
, we may choose
S
so that each
n^{S}_{b}\geq m
.

Proof 8.1
.

We construct such an
S
explicitly (though note that we could alternatively prove it more abstractly; it is essentially the same as the proof of Lemma
126
). We choose large integers
A,B,C\geq m
, and set
n_{b}=A-2C|x|
for
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{E}_{x}}
,
n_{b}=C(|x|-|x^{\prime}|)
for
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}^{LL}_{xx^{\prime}}}
, and
n_{b}=B+2C|x|
for
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\Upsilon_{x}}
. We set
n_{0}=A+B
. The data of the maps can be chosen similarly.

Fix some choice
S
of saturated
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}
-boundary stabilisation data for
L
.

{rmk}

We could have instead not made
\mathcal{M}
unital, but then various simplicial sets we will define in what follows would only be semisimplicial sets.

For convenience, we also set
\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{xx}}\in\mathcal{D}_{11}
to be the labelled disc with 2 punctures, both labelled by
x
, and both boundary components labelled by
L
. For both
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{xx}}
, we set
n_{b}=0
. For a sequence
x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathcal{M}
, we define the set
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}
to be the disjoint union:

\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}:=\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{A}_{x_{0}}}\sqcup\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{x_{0}x_{1}}}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{x_{k-1}x_{k}}}\sqcup\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{D}_{x_{k}}}

(8.5)

For each
n\in\mathcal{I}
, the finite set
[n]
has a natural ordering. Pulling back this ordering on
[n_{0}]
along the injection

\bigsqcup\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}[n_{b}]\to[n_{0}]

(8.6)

induces an ordering on the LHS (and hence on all its subsets), which we call the
unnatural ordering
.
[n^{+}_{b}]
admits a similar ordering. By the associativity from
6.9
, the maps (
6.8
) always respect the unnatural ordering.

For any subset
U\subseteq\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}
, we define

\omega=\omega^{U}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}:\bigsqcup\limits_{b\in U}[n_{b}]\xrightarrow{\simeq}\left[\sum\limits_{b\in U}n_{b}\right]

(8.7)

to be the unique order-preserving bijection, with respect to the unnatural ordering on the LHS and the natural ordering on the RHS.

For
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
, we let
b^{+}\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{xx^{\prime}}}
be the upper boundary component (going counterclockwise from the output to the input), and
b^{-}
the lower boundary component.

Gluing at
x_{i}
defines maps
d_{i}:\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}\to\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots\hat{x}_{i}\ldots x_{k}}
, and inserting a repeat of
x_{i}
defines maps
s_{i}:\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}\to\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{i}x_{i}\ldots x_{k}}
. These satisfy the simplicial identities, and furthermore in this case the
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}
-boundary stabilisation data determines bijections:

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{b\in d_{i}^{-1}\{b^{\prime}\}}[n_{b}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[n_{b^{\prime}}]}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{b\in s_{i}^{-1}\{b^{\prime}\}}[n_{b}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[n_{b^{\prime}}]}

(8.8)

for all
b^{\prime}
, which satisfy similar identities.

We partition
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}}
into two pieces
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}}^{\pm}
. These are defined by:

\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}^{+}:=\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{E}_{x_{0}}}\sqcup\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{x_{0}x_{1}}}^{+}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{x_{k-1}x_{k}}}^{+}\sqcup\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\Upsilon_{x_{k}}}

(8.9)

and

\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}^{-}:=\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{x_{0}x_{1}}}^{-}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{\mathcal{M}_{x_{k-1}x_{k}}}^{-}

(8.10)

We also define
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{\sim}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}
to be the quotient set
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}/\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{+}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}
, and write
b^{+}=b^{+}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}
as the image of
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{+}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}
. Since the
d_{i}
and
s_{i}
send
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{+}
to
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{+}
, they descend to maps
d_{i}^{\sim}
and
s_{i}^{\sim}
between the
\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{\sim}
, still satisfying the simplicial identities. By construction, the
d_{i}
,
d^{\sim}_{i}
,
s_{i}
,
s_{i}^{\sim}
commute with the quotient maps. We set:

n_{b^{+}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}:=\sum\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{+}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}n_{b}\in\mathcal{I}

(8.11)

We also set
\omega^{+}:=\omega^{\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{+}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}
; this equips
[n_{b^{+}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}]
with an unnatural ordering. By identifying
n_{b^{+}_{\ldots}}
with
\sqcup_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{+}_{\ldots}}[n_{b}]
via
\omega^{+}
, the maps (
8.8
) induce bijections:

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{b\in(d_{i}^{\sim})^{-1}\{b^{\prime}\}}[n_{b}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[n_{b^{\prime}}]}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{b\in(s_{i}^{\sim})^{-1}\{b^{\prime}\}}[n_{b}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[n_{b^{\prime}}]}

(8.12)

which are compatible with the maps (
8.8
) in the appropriate way.

Note that the maps in (
8.8
) and (
8.12
) all preserve the unnatural order on both sides.

For each
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{\sim}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}
, we define a simplex
\tau_{b}\in(S^{1})_{k}
by setting
\tau_{b^{+}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}
to be
\tau_{0}
, and proceding inductively by specifying that for
b
directly counterclockwise of
b^{\prime}
,
\tau_{b}=\tau_{l+1}
when
\tau_{b^{\prime}}=\tau_{l}
.

We define

8.3
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}

Before proving Proposition

Let
\hat{B}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M},\Upsilon)=\mathrm{colim}_{[k]\in\Delta^{op}}B(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M},\Upsilon)_{k}
; here we take the colimit over the simplex category, in contrast to the usual bar construction, which is the homotopy colimit. The natural transformation
\mathrm{hocolim}\to\mathrm{colim}
provides a natural map
\overline{L}=B(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M},\Upsilon)\to\hat{B}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M},\Upsilon)
; this is a homotopy equivalence since the inclusion of a boundary face into a manifold with faces is a cofibration.
(\Upsilon\circ\mathcal{E})
is constructed as a subspace of a coequaliser diagram in the form of
[
PS24b
, Equation (33)]
, which deformation retracts to
\hat{B}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M},\Upsilon)
(via the projection
[0,\varepsilon)\to\{0\}
), so we obtain a map
(\Upsilon\circ\mathcal{E})\to\overline{L}
.

Let
\widehat{(\Upsilon\circ\mathcal{E})}
be the “twist” of

by the composition

8.6
Projection

We will use Propisition
42
to identify the Hopf map. To do so, we must pass from
\mathcal{I}
-spaces to
\Gamma
-spaces. Unfortunately, this involves passing through the equivalence
GL_{1}^{\Psi,\Gamma}(S^{1}_{+})\to V^{cyc}GL_{1}^{\Psi}
, which does not (to our knowledge) admit a concrete homotopy inverse. We construct an explicit homotopy lift of the map
B^{cyc}_{S}GL_{1}^{\Psi}\to B^{cyc}(GL_{1}^{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}\to V^{cyc}GL_{1}^{\Psi}
along this equivalence; roughly, this uses the fact that
B^{cyc}_{S}GL_{1}^{\Psi}
is built out of finitely many pieces, whereas
B^{cyc}(GL_{1}^{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}
is a homotopy colimit over infinitely many terms. Slightly more precisely, we exploit the unnatural order to build various functors between categories we index homotopy colimits over.

Definition 159
.

For
x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathcal{M}
, we define an object
\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}\in\mathcal{S}((S^{1}_{+})_{k})
as follows (recall objects of this category are functors
\mathcal{P}({(S^{1}_{+})_{k}})\to\mathcal{I}
satisfying a condition, cf. Definition
31
).

For
1\leq l\leq k
, we set
n_{b_{l}}
to be
n_{b}
for
b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{-}_{\mathcal{M}_{x_{l-1}x_{l}}}
the unique element, and we set
n_{b_{0}}
to be
n^{+}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}
.

Let
U\subseteq\overline{\left(S^{1}_{+}\right)_{k}}
.
We set:

\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}(U):=\sum\limits_{\tau_{j}\in U}n_{b_{j}}\in\mathcal{I}

(8.27)

For an inclusion
i:U\to U^{\prime}
, the corresponding map
\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}(U)\to\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}(U^{\prime})
is defined so that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\left[\sum\limits_{\tau_{j}\in U}{n_{b_{j}}}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}(i)}
\textstyle{\left[\sum\limits_{\tau_{j}\in U^{\prime}}{n_{b_{j}}}\right]}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\tau_{j}\in U}\left[{n_{b_{j}}}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\tau_{j}\in U^{\prime}}\left[{n_{b_{j}}}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{U^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

(8.28)

where the bottom right arrow is the identity
\left[{n_{b_{j}}}\right]\to\left[{n_{b_{j}}}\right]
for all
\tau_{j}\in U
.

Lemma 160
.

The following equalities hold:

1.

(d_{i})_{*}\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}=\theta_{x_{0}\ldots\hat{x}_{i}\ldots x_{k}}

2.

(s_{i})_{*}\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}=\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{i}x_{i}\ldots x_{k}}

where
d_{i}:(S^{1}_{+})_{k}\to(S^{1}_{+})_{k-1}
and
s_{i}:(S^{1}_{+})_{k}\to(S^{1}_{+})_{k+1}
are the face and degeneracy maps respectively.

Proof 8.7
.

This is essentially by opening up definitions. On objects, this follows from the saturatedness condition. On morphisms, this follows from existence of the unnatural ordering and the fact that for
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
, morphisms
\theta_{\ldots}(U)\to\theta_{\ldots}(U^{\prime})
are constructed to be unnatural order-preserving.

{rmk}

The reason we imposed saturatedness on
S
is to ensure
equalities
of functors in Lemma
160
. Without imposing the saturatedness condition, we would instead have that
(d_{i})_{*}\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}
and
\theta_{x_{0}\ldots\hat{x}_{i}\ldots x_{k}}
are related by a natural transformation, which would necessitate incorporating more coherence technology into the definition of
j
.

Definition 161
.

We define a map of simplicial spaces
j_{\bullet}:\left(B_{S}^{cyc}GL_{1}^{\Psi}\right)_{\bullet}\to\left(GL_{1}^{\Psi,\Gamma}(S^{1}_{+})\right)_{\bullet}
, inducing a map
j
on their realisations, as follows.

On
k
-simplices,
j_{k}
is defined on the component corresponding to
x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}
(cf. (
8.22
)) to be the composition:

\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{\sim}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n_{b})\xrightarrow{=}\prod\limits_{\tau_{l}\in\overline{(S^{1}_{+})_{k}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}\left(\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}(\{\tau_{l}\})\right)\xrightarrow{\iota_{0}}GL_{1}^{\Psi,\Gamma}\left(\left(S^{1}_{+}\right)_{k}\right)

(8.29)

where the first map comes from identifying
n_{b}=\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}(\{\tau_{b}\})
, and the second map
\iota_{0}
is the inclusion of 0-simplices corresponding to the object
\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}\in\mathcal{S}({(S^{1}_{+})_{k}})
in the category which
GL_{1}^{\Psi,\Gamma}
is a homotopy colimit over (cf. Definition
32
).

It follows from Lemma
160
that
j_{\bullet}
is compatible with the face and degeneracy maps and so does define a map of simplicial spaces.

Lemma 162
.

The following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

\textstyle{B^{cyc}_{S}GL_{1}^{\Psi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{B^{cyc}(GL_{1}^{\Psi})_{h\mathcal{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{GL_{1}^{\Psi,\Gamma}(S^{1}_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V^{cyc}GL_{1}^{\Psi}}

(8.30)

Proof 8.8
.

We construct an explicit 3-step homotopy.

As a first step, we define an intermediate homotopy
\{F_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}:B^{cyc}_{S}GL_{1}^{\Psi}\to V^{cyc}GL_{1}^{\Psi}
, for
j=0,1
, as follows.

Here we use the observation in Remark
3.2
that any isometric embedding
\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{l}
induces a map
GL_{1}^{\Psi}(k)\to GL_{1}^{\Psi}(l)
. For any
n\in\mathcal{I}
and
t\in[0,1]
, let
f^{n}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
be the isometric embedding given by the block matrix
\begin{pmatrix}\cos\frac{\pi}{2}t\cdot\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\\
\sin\frac{\pi}{2}t\cdot\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\end{pmatrix}
with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{2n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note
f^{n}_{0}
and
f^{n}_{1}
are the inclusions of the first
n
and the last
n
co-ordinates respectively, so they’re exactly the linear maps induced by the two corresponding inclusions
[n]\to[2n]
.

On the component of the
k
-simplices corresponding to
x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathcal{M}
, we define
F_{t}
to be the map which is induced factorwise by
f_{t}^{n_{b}}
:

\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{\sim}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n_{b})\to\prod\limits_{b\in\operatorname{Cpt}\partial\Delta^{\sim}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(2n_{b})

(8.31)

followed by the inclusion of the RHS into the homotopy colimit (cf. (
2.30
)) corresponding to the object
(n_{b_{0}},\ldots,n_{b_{k}},\theta_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}})\in\mathcal{I}^{k+1}\times\mathcal{S}((S^{1}_{+})_{k})
, where the
b_{i}
are labelled going counterclockwise, beginning with
b_{0}:=b^{+}
. This specifies
F_{t}
over the
k
-simplices, and is compatible with the face and degeneracy maps and so induces a map on realisations.

In particular, note that the endpoints of the homotopy
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
use only functoriality of
GL_{1}^{\Psi}(\cdot)
under maps in
\mathcal{I}
, rather than arbitrary isometric embeddings.

By existence of the family
\{F_{t}\}
,
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are homotopic.

Claim 163
.

F_{0}
is homotopic to the composition of
\iota_{0}
with the right vertical arrow in (
8.30
).

Proof 8.9

(Proof of claim)
.

Choose a component of the
k
-simplices of the domain corresponding to
x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathcal{M}
. Let
\mathcal{C}=\{\operatorname{pt}\}
and
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{I}^{k+1}\times\mathcal{S}((S^{1}_{+})_{k})
. The two maps we wish to compare are of the form

\prod_{b\in\partial^{\sim}\mathbb{D}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(n_{b})\to\underset{\mathcal{D}}{\mathrm{hocolim}}GL_{1}^{\Psi}(\ldots)\times\ldots\times GL_{1}^{\Psi}(\ldots)

(8.32)

We think of the left hand side as a homotopy colimit over the appropriate constant functor
\mathcal{C}\to Spc_{*}
. The two maps are induced (as in Definition
5
, and using the same notation) by functors
A,A^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
along with appropriate natural transformations. Here
A
sends
\operatorname{pt}
to
(n_{b_{0}},\ldots,n_{b_{k}},0)
and

sends
\operatorname{pt}
to
(n_{0},\ldots,n_{k},\theta_{x_{0}\ldots x_{k}})

f_{0}
factorwise on the RHS in (
2.30
) corresponding to
A(\operatorname{pt})
and
A^{\prime}(\operatorname{pt})
.

Lemma
6
then constructs the required homotopy. A direct check shows this homotopy is compatible with the face and degeneracy maps, and so defines a homotopy of simplicial spaces and therefore a homotopy on realisations.

An identical argument to the proof of Claim
163
shows that
F_{1}
is homotopic to the composition of
j
with the lower horizontal arrow in (
8.30
). These three homotopies together provide the required homotopy.

Proof of Corollary

Proof 8.13

(Proof of Corollary
2
(1))
.

Corollary
2
(1) follows from the fact that
BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})
is connected, so the composition (
8.2
) lands in the path-component of the unit in
\Omega^{\infty}R_{\Psi}
.

Proof 8.14

(Proof of Corollary
2
(3))
.

Corollary
2
(3) follows from the fact that
\eta_{*}
is 2-torsion on homotopy groups.

Recall from Remark
2.8
that since
BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})
and
GL_{1}(R_{\Psi})
correspond to infinite loop spaces, they give rise to connective spectra
bgl_{1}(R_{\Psi})
and
gl_{1}(R_{\Psi})
respectively, and that
bgl_{1}(R_{\Psi})\simeq\Sigma gl_{1}(R_{\Psi})
.

Applying the
\Sigma^{\infty}
-
\Omega^{\infty}
adjunction to the composition of maps of spaces:

L\xrightarrow{\xi}BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})\xrightarrow{\eta_{*}}GL_{1}(R_{\Psi})

(8.38)

gives a composition of maps of spectra:

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}L\xrightarrow{\xi}bgl_{1}(R_{\Psi})\xrightarrow{\eta}gl_{1}(R_{\Psi})

(8.39)

p
-localisation

Fix
p
an odd prime. We use two forms of
p
-localisation.

1.

A spectrum
Y
is
p
-local
if the groups
\pi_{*}Y
are uniquely
p
-divisible.
7
7

7

See
[
Bou79
, Proposition 2.4]
for a comparison to a potentially more familiar definition of
p
-locality.

2.

A space
Y
is
p
-local
if, whenever
U\to V
is a map of spaces which induces an isomorphism on homology with
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-coefficients,
[V,Y]\to[U,Y]
is a bijection.

There are ‘
p
-localisation functors’
Y\mapsto Y_{p}
, from (a). spectra to
p
-local spectra and (b). nilpotent spaces to
p
-local spaces, along with (in both cases) a natural transformation
Y\to Y_{p}
. In both cases, the homotopy groups of
Y_{p}
are the
p
-localisations of those of
Y
(cf.
[
Bou79
, Section 2]
,
[
MP12
, Theorem 6.1.2]
).

Since
\Omega^{\infty}
sends spectra to spaces which have nilpotent components and
\Omega^{\infty}
preserves homotopy groups for connective spectra, we have:

Lemma 167
.

p
-localisation commutes with
\Omega^{\infty}
. More precisely, if
Y
is a spectrum, then
\Omega^{\infty}(Y_{p})\simeq(\Omega^{\infty}Y)_{p}
.

Proof 8.15

(Proof of Corollary
2
(2))
.

Consider the homotopy commutative diagram of spectra:

\textstyle{bgl_{1}(R_{\Psi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{gl_{1}(R_{\Psi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{bgl_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{gl_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{p}}

(8.40)

The bottom horizontal map may be identified with
\eta_{*}:\Sigma gl_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{p}\to gl_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{p}
. Since both these spectra are
p
-local, the action of
\pi_{*}\mathbb{S}
factors through an action by the
p
-local sphere
\pi_{*}\mathbb{S}_{p}
. However, since
\eta
is 2-torsion, its image in
\pi_{*}\mathbb{S}_{p}
vanishes, and so this bottom horizontal map is trivial.

By Lemma
167
and the above discussion, delooping (
8.40
) we obtain a homotopy commutative diagram of spaces:

\textstyle{BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{*}}
\textstyle{GL_{1}(R_{\Psi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BGL_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{const}}
\textstyle{GL_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{p}}

(8.41)

Combining with the following commutative diagram of spaces:

\textstyle{GL_{1}(R_{\Psi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\infty}R_{\Psi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{GL_{1}(R_{\Psi})_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\Omega^{\infty}R_{\Psi})_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\infty}((R_{\Psi})_{p})}

(8.42)

(where the arrow labelled
=
comes from Lemma
167
)
we may conclude that the image of
[\eta\xi]
in the
p
-localisation of
R^{0}_{\Psi}(L)
agrees with the unit
1
(since it factors through a constant map) for all odd
p
, and therefore that
1-[\eta\xi]\in R^{0}_{\Psi}(L)
is
2^{l}
torsion for some
l
.
